
===== [p170-172] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > Multiphoton Lithography (MPL) for Photopolymerization (14块) =====
--[p171]-- Initial work has explored the fabrication of open cellular structures as biomaterials or photonic crystals [39, 40]. In principle, the technique is amenable to any rendering accessible through traditional computerassisted drawing (CAD) software, and this has been demonstrated through the fabrication of many complex objects, such as a bull, frogs, and composite culture scafolds (Figure 8.1) [31–33].
--[p170]-- This is achieved most commonly with high-energy femtosecond pulsed laser sources that significantly increase the probability of absorbing multiple photons nearly simultaneously within the focal volume [30]. Owing to the nonlinear nature of the two photon process, the probability of excitation decays as a function of distance from the focal plane with quadratic dependence, which compresses the axial spread of the point spread function [30].
--[p170]-- Multiphoton techniques were first introduced into the biology and biomaterials communities through the development and proliferation of multiphoton microscopy (MPM) by Watt and colleagues [34, 35]. MPM is now widely available and an attractive tool for high-resolution fluorescent imaging in thick tissues as well as in live animals [30].
--[p172]-- These advantages aford true 3D patterning at size scales commensurate with the focal volume of the multiphoton laser (<1 fl or 1 μm<sup>3</sup>), which conveniently accesses subcellular length scales pertinent to cell and tissue biology.
While MPL improves the voxel resolution for photopolymerized biomaterials and hydrogels, practical constraints of the technology still exist.
--[p170]-- Beyond fluorescent imaging, multiphoton excitation is commonly employed for molecular uncaging of small molecules [36], fluorescence recovery after photobleaching (FRAP) [37], and biophysical investigations [38]. Importantly, multiphoton excitation has been incorporated directly into traditional laser scanning microscopes (LSMs) or LSL setups to image or fabricate materials with micrometer- and nanometer-scale features.
--[p170]-- In practical terms, this means that nearly all of the excitation is confined to the focal plane, providing high resolution (<1 μm) in the axial or z-dimension (Figure 8.1a). Assuming reasonable optics, this translates to sub-femtoliter focal volumes. For additional reading, the concepts and physics behind multiphoton excitation are handled with suficient rigor in Part I and Chapter 6.
--[p171]-- The same principles above apply to photopolymerization reactions: if two photons with lesser energy are absorbed by the same photoinitiator nearly simultaneously, the molecule may undergo cleavage and generate initiating radicals within the sub-femtoliter focal volume [28]. Accordingly, rapid prototyping with MPL afords the fabrication of objects with sub-micrometer feature sizes.
--[p172]-- Advances in computational quantum chemistry are beginning to elucidate novel photoinitiators that are susceptible to multiphoton excitation and cleavage to generate initiating radicals [41]. Excitingly, water-soluble initiators with favorable two-photon cross sections are under development for biological applications [32].
--[p172]-- The main advantage of MPL over traditional LSL is the greatly increased z-resolution aforded because the axial dispersion of the point spread function is significantly decreased compared to single-photon excitation [30]. Additionally, the use of IR or near-IR light enables increased penetration depth, as these wavelengths are minimally absorbed and scatter less in most media, including biological samples [30].
--[p170]-- Multiphoton Lithography (MPL) for Photopolymerization
The theory of multiphoton excitation was described initially by Maria Goeppert-Mayer in her doctorate dissertation; Goeppert-Mayer is also the recipient of the 1963 Nobel Prize for Physics for articulating the nuclear shell model of atomic nuclei [29].
--[p170]-- The principle of multiphoton excitation, as proposed by Goeppert-Mayer, allows a photoactive molecule to enter an excited state by absorbing multiple photons of lesser energy (longer wavelength) than a single photon of greater energy (shorter wavelength) that would normally result in photoexcitation [29].
--[p171]-- As multiphoton systems have become increasingly accessible, they have been adopted by the materials science and polymers community for high-resolution 3D photopolymerizations [28]. MPL has successfully generated a range of complex, 3D photopolymerized structures (Figure 8.1).
--[p172]-- MPL remains limited in total thickness without using advanced stages and by the throughput of fabrication, which remains low for high-feature-content, millimeter-sized objects. Additionally, the available photoinitiator toolbox, those photoinitiators that are amenable to multiphoton excitation, should be improved.
--[p170]-- This phenomenon, now well tested and established, is traditionally achieved with two photons from the same laser possessing roughly half the energy of a single photon capable of excitation [29]. As both photons are needed to bump the molecule into the excited quantum state, they must both be absorbed within femtoseconds of each other.

===== [p101-103] 5 STED-Inspired Approaches to Resolution Enhancement > 5.1 Introduction (13块) =====
--[p101]-- A number of groups have also explored the use of radical quenchers to create photoresists with a steeper exposure contrast curve, which allows for finer control of the exposure threshold dose and thereby enables the creation of smaller voxels [4–6].
A facet of multiphoton lithography that can be inconvenient is that voxels are not spherical.
--[p102]-- In a typical implementation of such a photoresist, a beam of light of one color is used for two-photon exposure, and a beam of a second color of light is used to counteract the efects of the exposure. By appropriate spatial patterning of these two beams, it is possible to attain feature sizes and resolution that far surpass what can be achieved with two-photon exposure alone.
This chapter focuses on two-color approaches to resolution enhancement in two-photon polymerization [10–14].
--[p101]-- 5.1 Introduction
Many potential applications of multiphoton lithography require extremely high resolution (which is defined as the minimum pitch of features). One important element in attaining high resolution is minimization of the feature size. The simplest means ofreducing the size ofvoxels are (i) using an objective with the highest possible numerical aperture; (ii) using the shortest possible exposure wavelength; and (iii) using the smallest possible exposure dose at each point.
--[p102]-- This phenomenon arises from the fact that the multiphoton pointspread function (PSF) of a tightly focused laser beam is longer along the optical axis than it is perpendicular to this axis [7]. As a result, voxels are prolate spheroids that are typically a factor of 3–5 longer than their width [8]. This irregular shape presents significant challenges in the fabrication of high-resolution structures that require features that are ofthe minimum possible size in all dimensions.
--[p101]-- The first two strategies arise directly from the Abbe criterion (i.e., that the resolution is approximately the exposure wavelength divided by twice the numerical aperture of the imaging system). However, the third strategy allows two-photon polymerization to achieve feature sizes and resolutions that are far smaller than the Abbe criterion predicts. For any negative-tone photoresist, there is an exposure threshold below which solubility is maintained.
--[p103]-- We close the chapter by considering challenges that must be addressed to unlock the full potential of two-color techniques in multiphoton polymerization.
--[p102]-- This issue can be addressed in part by using a diferent transverse and axial step size during fabrication; but for structures that require uniform, fine features, such a strategy limits the resolution to that in the axial direction.
--[p101]-- Two-photon polymerization is typically performed with a laser beam with a Gaussian spatial profile; and by operating under appropriate exposure conditions, the threshold dose is received only at the very top of this profile (Figure 5.1). This phenomenon allows for features with transverse dimensions on the order of 100 nm to be attained routinely using 800-nm exposure.
Each of the above strategies has been exploited to improve the feature size and resolution of multiphoton polymerization.
--[p102]-- However, these methods cannot improve the resolution to the extent that is desired for many applications, and they have little efect on the voxel asymmetry problem. Thus, researchers have looked in new directions to address these issues. One of the most promising approaches is the creation of new photoresists that respond diferently to two colors or two beams of light.
--[p101]-- The use of photoresists that can be exposed at shorter wavelengths has also proven to be an efective means of attaining higher resolution [3], although this strategy requires the use of ultrafast laser systems that are not as widely available as those producing light in the near-infrared.
--[p102]-- Furthermore, the elongated shape of voxels can cause the mechanical properties of structures that are fabricated parallel to the substrate to difer substantially from those of structures that are fabricated perpendicular to the substrate [9]; this phenomenon is all the more significant when diferent step sizes are used in diferent directions.
The approaches outlined above can provide significant enhancement of the resolution of two-photon polymerization.
--[p103]-- We begin by discussing the optical technique that has inspired most of the approaches to two-color lithography, stimulated emission depletion (STED) fluorescence microscopy [15–17]. We then consider the various approaches that have been taken to apply some of the ideas of STED to two-color lithography, and discuss the state of the art for each method.
--[p101]-- For instance, schemes have been explored to increase the efective numerical aperture of the imaging system used for fabrication [1]. The influence of the refractive index of the photoresist on resolution has also been explored [2], which is important because a higher refractive index enables a higher numerical aperture to be used.

===== [p213-213] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Other Optically Active Microstructures (5块) =====
--[p213]-- Other Optically Active Microstructures
Optically birefringent microstructures with properties that can be altered by external means have direct application in optical data storage, optical switches, and waveguides. A reversibly birefringent microstructure was fabricated by MPL using a composite consisting of a triacrylate resin doped with the azo-aromatic chromophore disperse red 13 (DR13) [54]. A birefringence of $5 \times 1 0 ^ { - 5 }$ was achieved using 1 wt% DR13.
--[p213]-- DR1 was reacted with (3-isocyanatopropyl) triethoxysilane to form the functionalized silicon alkoxide precursor SGDR1, which was then mixed with methacryloxypropyl trimethosilane (MAPTMS) and the photoinitiator Irgacure 369. This composite was used to fabricate a woodpile PC. The structure did not exhibit a complete photonic bandgap due to the low refractive index contrast of SGDR1 and air, which is roughly 0.5.
--[p213]-- As azo-aromatic chromophores have low residual birefringence, microstructures were fabricated using an azo-polymer HEMA-DR13, which was shown to increase the residual birefringence by ∼35% [55].
A nonlinear 3D PC was fabricated by MPL using the nonlinear chromophore disperse red 1 (DR1) (Figure 9.5) [7].
--[p213]-- The birefringence arises as a result of optical ratcheting of DR13 molecules that occurs under continuous exposure using linearly polarized excitation at $\lambda = 5 1 4 . 5 \mathrm { n m }$ . The birefringence could be induced and later erased by either heating the sample or by exposing it to circularly polarized laser light.
--[p213]-- Fourier transform infrared spectroscopy (FTIR) indicated that a stopband was present in certain directions at infrared wavelengths.

===== [p107-111] 5 STED-Inspired Approaches to Resolution Enhancement > Stimulated Emission Depletion in Multiphoton Lithography (30块) =====
--[p109]-- For every photoinitiator tested, we found that the beam that was intended to inhibit polymerization instead increased polymerization, even though this beam did not lead to polymerization in the absence of the excitation beam.
Why did the deactivation beam have the opposite of the intended efect in these photoresists? As discussed above, stimulated emission always competes with EEA.
--[p111]-- It has been suggested by some authors that stimulated emission is the best choice for the photophysical deactivation of photopolymerization for resolution enhancement [13, 45]. It should be noted, however, that these sorts of claims have not yet been supported by any rigorous theoretical analysis or experimental comparisons.
--[p110]-- Thus, the photochemistry and photophysics upon multiphoton absorption may difer significantly from those observed for linear absorption. We have indeed observed that rhodamine 6G acts as an eficient multiphoton radical initiator in acrylic photoresists [44].
These sorts of ideas led Wegener and coworkers to discover a laser dye, 7-diethylamino-3-thenoylcoumarin (DETC), that can act as an efective multiphoton initiator and can be deactivated by CW 532 nm light [45, 46].
--[p109]-- The combined beams enter the reflected light port of an inverted microscope, and are focused into the sample through a high-numericalaperture objective. Good overlap of the focal points of the two beams was ensured by using multiphoton-absorption-induced luminescence [34] from gold nanoparticles deposited on a cover slip.
Typical photoresist formulations contained equal weight percentages ofethoxylated(6) trimethylolpropane triacrylate and tris(2-hydroxyethyl)isocyanurate triacrylate.
--[p107]-- Furthermore, achieving a greater degree of crosslinking in an individual voxel generally entails making the voxel larger. If crosslinking can be inhibited efectively outside of the desired region of exposure, however, it is possible to crosslink the desired region to a higher degree than is typically possible without increasing the voxel size. This efect, which holds for any deactivation mechanism, ofers the possibility of creating smaller structures with improved mechanical properties.
--[p107]-- A number aspects of the photophysics and photochemistry of aryl ketones appear favorable for the use of STED to enhance resolution in multiphoton polymerization. In addition to there being suficient time to deactivate excited photoinitiators using stimulated emission, the requirements for deactivation are considerably less stringent than in fluorescence microscopy.
--[p108]-- We developed an instrument to test whether STED could be applied to inhibit crosslinking following multiphoton excitation of a radical photoinitiator (Figure 5.5). In this apparatus, two independently tunable, mode-locked Ti:sapphire lasers (Coherent Mira 900-F) produce pulses of sub-200 fs duration. The two lasers are synchronized to one another with ∼250 fs of timing jitter using commercial hardware (Coherent SynchroLock). One laser is used as the source for multiphoton excitation.
--[p110]-- However, its two-photon absorption spectrum has a strong peak at about 835 nm in the same solvent [42], which would correspond to a linear absorption peak at 417 nm. No such peak is seen in the linear absorption spectrum of rhodamine 6G in methanol [43], which suggests that the symmetry of this excited state is such that linear absorption from the ground state is forbidden.
--[p107]-- Stimulated Emission Depletion in Multiphoton Lithography
In principle, STED should also be able to enhance resolution in the multiphoton exposure of negative-tone photoresists. Here we will concentrate on the application of STED to radical photoresists, but similar principles apply to cationic photoresists [12, 30].
As a representative example, here we consider the aryl ketone family of radical photoinitiators, which is exemplified by benzophenone [31].
--[p111]-- There are two key timescales for this process: the IVR time in the ground electronic state, and the apparent fluorescence lifetime (which is related directly to the timescale of initiation). Stimulated emission must take place on a timescale that is slower than the former process yet faster than the latter process. Ifeither condition is not met, then initiation will not be prevented efectively.
--[p111]-- Because IVR must also occur in the excited electronic state before stimulated emission commences, the window for stimulated emission lasts from a minimum of a few picoseconds after excitation to a maximum of a few hundred picoseconds after excitation. The latter value depends on the acceptable amount of initiation in areas that are intended to be soluble after exposure.
--[p110]-- Although there is more than one mechanism for the deactivation of this dye [39, 46], at least one mechanism appears to be stimulated emission. This system was able to achieve significant axial and transverse resolution enhancement as compared to the same photoresist used without a deactivation beam. Wollhofen et al. subsequently demonstrated feature sizes of 55 nm at 120 nm pitch using a photoresist containing DETC with pulsed, 780-nm, multiphoton excitation and CW 532-nm deactivation [47].
--[p110]-- However, such molecules may behave diferently in a viscous photoresist than they do in a low-viscosity solvent. Additionally, multiphoton excitation often takes molecules to diferent excited states than does linear excitation, opening up new photochemical pathways. As an example, rhodamine 6G has a fluorescence quantum yield of 0.93 upon linear excitation in methanol [40], and its absorption spectrum peaks at about 528 nm [41].
--[p111]-- If deactivation is performed with CW radiation, then the vast majority ofdeactivation photons go waste, as the repetition time ofthe excitation laser is typically on a timescale ofnanoseconds. In this case, the deactivation beam must be operated at high intensity so that deactivation is eficient within the useful time window. However, high intensity also leads to sample heating through Raman scattering and other efects.
--[p110]-- Conventional radical photoinitiators difer in a number of important ways from the fluorophores used for STED microscopy. For instance, conventional photoinitiators generally have rather small Stokes’ shifts. More importantly, these photoinitiators are typically designed to have relatively low extinction coeficients, so that it is possible to photocure thick films containing these materials.
--[p110]-- An alternative strategy for using STED to improve the resolution of multiphoton polymerization is to search for molecules that have high extinction coeficients and can also act as photoinitiators. Ideally, such molecules should also have high fluorescence quantum yields, so that STED can be efective. A molecule having a high fluorescence quantum yield would not typically be considered for use as a radical photoinitiator, as the quantum yield for radical formation would necessarily be low.
--[p107]-- Upon excitation to $\mathrm { S } _ { 1 }$ , these molecules can undergo intersystem crossing to the lowest triplet state $\mathrm { T } _ { 1 }$ (Figure 5.4). In this class of molecule, $\mathrm { T _ { 1 } }$ is usually a triplet diradical centered on the carbonyl, which can either abstract hydrogen atoms from other molecules or undergo homolytic bond cleavage [32]. In either case, two or more radicals are generated, and can initiate a polymerization chain reaction.
--[p111]-- Thus, even if the amount of heat that is deposited during a single cycle is small, the ability to undergo up to one cycle per laser pulse can still allow a significant amount ofheat to be deposited using STED.
Whether the disadvantages described above outweigh the advantages is strongly dependent on the desired application. With this in mind, we now proceed to discuss other reported methods of deactivation.
--[p109]-- These trifunctional monomers create a highly crosslinked polymer with low shrinkage and good adhesion [35]. Photoinitiators were incorporated at 1–3 wt%.
We investigated a wide range of conventional radical photoinitiators that have been used widely for two-photon polymerization [35, 36], including 2-benzyl-2-(dimethylamino)-1-[4-(morpholinyl)phenyl)]-1-butanone (Irgacure 369), 2,4,6-trimethylbenzoylphenyl phosphinate (Lucirin TPO-L), 2-isopropylthioxanthone (ITX), and many others.
--[p110]-- They were able to achieve line widths of 65 nm, and the resolution enhancement was limited only by additional crosslinking caused at higher intensities of the deactivation light. Subsequent work has led to the alternative suggestion that some or all of the inhibition of polymerization in this system actually involves triplet states of ITX rather than stimulated emission [38, 39].
--[p111]-- Stimulated emission is a rapid process given a high-enough depletion intensity and oscillator strength. In the absence of other photophysical processes, stimulated emission returns the photoinitiator to its ground electronic state, which is the most desirable situation.
--[p110]-- A small oscillator strength between $\mathrm { S } _ { 0 }$ and $\mathrm { S } _ { 1 }$ implies that $\mathrm { S } _ { 1 }$ must have large oscillator strength to higher excited states. Higher singlet states often have faster intersystem crossing rates than does $\mathrm { S } _ { 1 }$ , and so can lead to even more eficient initiation of crosslinking.
Based on these issues, the use of STED to inhibit most conventional radical photoinitiators is likely to be challenging.
--[p111]-- STED may also generate less local heating that other deactivation schemes [45], as each excitation/deactivation cycle only leaves $2 \hbar \omega _ { \mathrm { e x } } - \hbar \omega _ { \mathrm { S E } }$ of energy in the sample.
We have developed a detailed kinetic model of STED-based deactivation in two-photon lithography that reveals some of the potential disadvantages of this approach [12].
--[p111]-- Indeed, the mechanism of initiation for the molecules that can be deactivated through STED is not yet known, and is clearly of importance for making comparisons with other techniques. In reality, there are inevitable tradeofs for all deactivation schemes that have been demonstrated to date, and the optimal scheme is likely to be dependent upon the application.
STED has some undeniable advantages for resolution enhancement.
--[p111]-- Thus, deactivation is best performed with pulses of a timescale on the order of picoseconds to tens of picoseconds. The maximum intensity that can be employed is limited by the potential for competition by EEA and by the requirement that the deactivation transition not be fully saturated. In addition, it is important to note that, if stimulated emission is eficient, then it is possible for a molecule to be excited and de-excited as often as once every excitation pulse.
--[p107]-- However, returning the molecule to $\mathrm { S } _ { 0 }$ through stimulated emission before it has a chance to undergo intersystem crossing can turn ofthe initiation process. The timescale for intersystem crossing in these molecules is generally on the order of 100 ps [33]. Although this time is considerably less than the fluorescence lifetime of a good fluorophore, it is still long enough to provide plenty oftime to intercept the intersystem crossing process using stimulated emission.
--[p110]-- The same is likely to hold true for the photoacid generators used in cationic polymerization as well. It should be noted, however, that Wegener and coworkers have successfully demonstrated the deactivation of ITX using continuous-wave (CW) radiation at 532 nm following pulse multiphoton excitation [37]. They suggested that the deactivation occurred through stimulated emission, as 532 nm is within the fluorescence band of ITX.
--[p107]-- In imaging, the vast majority of excited molecules outside of the region of interest must be deactivated to create the greatest possible resolution enhancement. On the other hand, in photolithography it is necessary only to decrease the concentration of excited photoinitiators outside of the region of interest to below the threshold exposure dose. This latter task could potentially be accomplished by deactivation of a small percentage of the excited photoinitiators.
--[p108]-- To meet this condition, the deactivation pulse is passed one or more times through a long-path-length cell containing a highly dispersive liquid. Depending on the deactivation wavelength, pulse durations of as much as 10 ps can be attained.
The two beams are of orthogonal polarizations, and are combined in a polarizing beam cube. If desired, a phase mask can also be placed in the path of the deactivation beam.
--[p108]-- The other laser is tuned either to a wavelength within the fluorescence spectrum of the photoinitiator or to twice the wavelength within the fluorescence spectrum (in which case the output is subsequently frequency-doubled). As described above, it is desirable for the duration of the deactivation pulse to be significantly longer than the IVR time in the ground electronic state.

===== [p35-37] 2 Characterization of 2PA Chromophores > 2.3.1.4 White-Light-Continuum Z-Scan (WLC Z-Scan) (9块) =====
--[p35]-- Again, this is not a necessary condition for performing Z-scans but it greatly simpli“es the analysis [39…41].
Nonlinear spectroscopy usingZ-scan is possible, as shown in Figure 2.10, where the 2PA spectrum of a squaraine molecule shown in the “gure was measured by performing open-apertureZ-scans at many wavelengths using an optical parametric ampli“er (OPA).
--[p36]-- Thus, once characterized, the WLC can be used to perform nonlinear spectroscopy, while the OPG/A needs to be recharacterized after each tuning cycle.
This WLC Z-scan has the potential of serving as the nonlinear equivalent of a linear spectrophotometer.
--[p35]-- Such a spectrum can take several days to perform because theZ-scan needs to be realigned for each wavelength, as the OPA beam alignment changes upon tuning [42].
A solution that allows rapid Z-scan characterization of materials over a broad spectral range is the white-light-continuum, WLC Z-scan [39–41, 43]. Here, the Z-scan methodology is the same but we replace the usual “single” frequency source in the Z-scan with a spectrally filtered WLC [44].
The apparatus is shown in Figure 2.11.
--[p36]-- The WLC is produced by weak focusing in a long (∼1.5 m) cell filled with Kr gas at a pressure of ∼2 atm. Using Gaussian spatial profile, femtosecond excitation pulses of∼1 mJ at ∼800 nm produce a clean spatial profile pulse of spectral content from <400 to >800 nm, that is, greater than one octave.
The entire WLC cannot be used in a single Z-scan because, in addition to the degenerate 2PA, there will be a strong nondegenerate 2PA, and these two processes cannot be simply separated.
--[p35]-- 2.3.1.4 White-Light-Continuum Z-Scan (WLC Z-Scan)
Performing nonlinear spectroscopy usingZ-scan can be time consuming because most broadly tunable sources, for example, optical parametric sources, require lengthy characterization each time they are tuned since the temporal and, in particular, the spatial pro“les are not reproducible. Usually, spatial “ltering is required if Gaussian pro“le pulses are to be used.
--[p37]-- When automated, a researcher can insert a sample, initiate a measurement, and then after sometime (perhaps <1 h for kilohertz sources) retrieve the degenerate NLA spectrum; however, with this nonlinear spectrophotometer, there is an added benefit not available with a linear spectrometer. The dispersion of the NLR is measured simultaneously with the closed-aperture $Z -$ scan, as shown in Figure 2.11. The data of Figure 2.13 were taken using this configuration.
--[p36]-- We have found that OPG/As when tuned change their spatial profile, and usually spatial filtering is needed to obtain Gaussian profile beams. Figure 2.12 shows the characterization of a WLC demonstrating that near-Gaussian profile beams are obtained at all the wavelengths over an octave span. New research is extending the useful range to greater than two octaves using diferent excitation wavelengths.
--[p36]-- The simplest method to apply is to spectrally filter the WLC prior to the sample and perform a normal single-frequency Z-scan. Then the spectral filter can be changed (e.g., using a computer-controlled rotating platform holding many spectral filters as shown in Figure 2.11, or using a linear variable filter) and another Z-scan is performed.
The filters must be spectrally broad enough to support the pulses used, which is ∼100 fs in this case.
--[p36]-- The diference between using the WLC and, for example, tuning an optical parametric generator/amplifier (OPG/A) is in the length of time it takes to measure a nonlinear spectrum (or nonlinear dispersion curve). As it turns out, once characterized in terms of the spatial properties and temporal profiles at all wavelengths, the WLC is stable from day to day and week to week. This cannot be said of optical parametric sources.

===== [p69-77] Modeling of Polymerization Processes > Efect of Fluctuations on the Minimum Feature Size (42块) =====
--[p72]-- Here, the connectivity percolation-like transition occurs, which is a sort of phase transition of the second kind. As discussed earlier, such a transition is one of the physical mechanisms of the phenomenological polymerization threshold. The threshold expectation of the voxel size is calculated as $r _ { \mathrm { t h } } =$ $w \sqrt { 2 \ln ( A / f _ { \mathrm { t h } } ) }$ , similar to Eq. (3.16).
--[p76]-- 4 _ { i }$ , one gets $r _ { \mathrm { c r } } \approx 4 2 \mathrm { n m } , \sigma _ { + } \approx 3 5 \mathrm { n m }$ , and $r _ { \mathrm { v o x } } = r _ { \mathrm { c r } } + \sigma _ { + } \approx 7 7$ nm.
It can be shown that the minimum voxel size can be made smaller by adding an inhibitor to a polymerizable resin. Here, we consider the case of a nondifusing inhibitor.
--[p70]-- To speed up the calculations, all the monomer molecules, both individual and already reacted, are placed at the sites of a cubic lattice. Connections between adjacent sites simulate the covalent bonds between monomer units in the macromolecules. The macromolecular chains are simulated with the random walk method. Intersections of the random walks (as well as self-intersections) simulate the crosslinks. The chain lengths are random and obey the distribution
$$
--[p72]-- } , z )$ of the center of mass, $D [ z _ { \mathrm { c m a s s } } ] = { \langle z _ { c m a s s } } ^ { 2 } \rangle$ . --[p69]-- Efect of Fluctuations on the Minimum Feature Size
A polymer substance consists of building blocks, namely the monomers, that are connected to each other forming macromolecular chains and crosslinked clusters. Such connections have a certain degree of randomness. Because of the relatively large size of a single monomer, the fluctuations within the polymer substance may become one of the principal factors that limit the minimum feature size in the DLW technique.
--[p70]-- p _ { \mathrm { l } } \propto \exp ( - l / l _ { 0 } ) ,\tag{3.22}
$$
where $p _ { l }$ is the probability that a polymer chain has length $l \ ( l \geq 2 )$ , and $l _ { 0 }$ is a parameter of the model that determines the average chain length.
The starting sites of the polymer chains are also random and not correlated. In the simplest case, probability distribution $f ( { \vec { r } } )$ to start the random walk at a site at $\vec { r }$ is contributed only by the photoinitiation reaction.
--[p72]-- Its average density is size-dependent and significantly deviates from the density of the bulk gel. The observation of the voxel realizations and correlation length analysis [35] suggest pronounced density fluctuations within this zone, which is further referred to as “the layer of fluctuations.” The smaller voxels do not have any kernel at all. Their average density is size-dependent everywhere and does not match that of the macroscopic polymer substance.

===== [p172-172] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > MPL Equipment for Biomaterial Fabrication (4块) =====
--[p172]-- Such systems are adapted for writing instead of reading by rastering the focal volume through a precursor solution within defined regions of interest (ROIs) that define the shape to be polymerized. Advances in CAD-supported ROI software are facilitating easy translation of arbitrary structures into 3D photopolymerized materials using commercial microscopes.
--[p172]-- MPL Equipment for Biomaterial Fabrication
A complete discussion of the equipment and techniques for MPL can be found in Part II. For the polymer or biomaterial scientist, the user will likely employ either commercial LSMs or home-built setups.
--[p172]-- In fact, the main limitations are the requirement for a multiphoton laser source (e.g., a femtosecond pulsed Ti:sapphire laser), a suitable light path with focusing optics, a high-fidelity xyz stage, and software to raster the laser focal volume through the sample, or vice versa. All of these requirements are met with commercial MPMs (e.g., Zeiss 710 NLO, Nikon A1R MP+, Olympus Fluoview FVMPE series) designed primarily for imaging biological samples.
--[p172]-- Additionally, to achieve high-resolution patterning with high fidelity, MPL equipment requires an automated [42] stage with independent x-, y-, and z-dimension resolution at the sub-micrometer scale.

--[p47]-- \delta _ { \mathrm { 2 P A s a m p l e } } = \frac { \langle F \rangle _ { \mathrm { s a m p l e } } n _ { \mathrm { s a m p l e } } ^ { 2 } \Phi _ { \mathrm { F r e f } } C _ { \mathrm { M r e f } } \langle P \rangle _ { \mathrm { r e f } } ^ { 2 } } { \langle F \rangle _ { \mathrm { r e f } } n _ { \mathrm { r e f } } ^ { 2 } \Phi _ { \mathrm { F s a m p l e } } C _ { \mathrm { M s a m p l e } } \langle P \rangle _ { \mathrm { s a m p l e } } ^ { 2 } } \delta _ { \mathrm { 2 P A r e f } }\tag{2.40}

--[p48]-- where F is the integrated fluorescence and P is the average excitation power ⟨ ⟩which is proportional to pulse energy, $C _ { M }$ ⟨ ⟩is the concentration of the solution obtained by measuring the 1PA spectrum, and $\Phi _ { \mathrm { F } }$ is the fluorescence quantum yield measured by 1PF.
There are several other factors contributing to accurate 2PF measurements.
--[p47]-- 2.3.2.4 Two-Photon-Absorption-Induced Fluorescence Spectroscopy
Many organic molecules show significant fluorescence quantum yields. This allows measurements of both one-photon absorption-induced fluorescence (1PF) and two-photon absorption-induced fluorescence (2PF) to obtain a degenerate 2PA spectrum. A typical experimental setup is shown in Figure 2.23.
After simultaneous absorption of two photons, the excited state rapidly relaxes to the zero vibrational level of the first excited state.
--[p47]-- This technology was further developed to measure 2PA spectra [73, 74] and for two-photon microscopy [75, 76]. Due to the uncertainty of various experimental parameters – for example, collection geometry and eficiency – absolute calibration of the fluorescence signal to determine the 2PA is problematic.
--[p48]-- The concentration of the solution should be as high as possible (but below the aggregation threshold) to increase the probability of 2PA; thus, reabsorption can be a significant issue. Assuming the fluorescence spectrum and quantum yield are independent of the concentration of the solution and the type of excitation (i.e., 1PA or 2PA), the 2PF spectrum can be fitted to the 1PF spectrum measured at a lower concentration (with peak OD < 0.1) to obtain a correct spectral contour.
--[p47]-- The fluorescence spectrum also needs to be corrected for the spectral sensitivity of the detector and for any residual reabsorption by the solution. To determine the 2PA spectrum of the sample solution, the integrated fluorescence signal is then compared to a reference molecule with a known 2PA cross section under identical measuring conditions. The 2PA cross section for the sample can then be calculated from
$$
--[p48]-- It is also not sensitive to the
Figure 2.24 Linear absorption spectrum (black line, right vertical and top horizontal scales), normalized fluorescence spectrum (red dash–dot line, top horizontal scale), 2PA spectrum using reference calibrated 2PF (green symbols, left and bottom scales), and 2PA spectra by Z-scan (black squares) of the molecule shown to the right. (Makarov 2011 [78]. Reproduced with permission of The Optical Society.)
--[p48]-- Figure 2.24 shows the 2PF spectrum of an organic dye dissolved in chloroform calibrated against a known standard [78]. Z-scans of the 2PA spectrum are also shown, which are in agreement with the 2PF data.
2PF spectroscopy is, in principle, a rapid experimental technique to measure 2PA spectra in comparison to other commonly adopted techniques, for example, Z-scan. However, verifying the quadratic dependence on irradiance at each wavelength considerably lengthens the experimental time.
--[p47]-- The fluorescence intensity is a measure of the population of the first excited state, which is proportional to the 2PA coeficient. Since the 2PA is proportional to the square of the input irradiance I, the resulting fluorescence signal is also proportional to $I ^ { 2 } ,$ . This dependence is thus a standard criterion to distinguish 1PF from 2PF, which was first used by Kaiser and Garrett in 1961 [71], just 1 month after second harmonic generation was reported [72].
--[p47]-- Standard reference molecules with known 2PA spectra are often used to measure relative optical fluorescence signals [73].When using 2PF to determine 2PA spectra, as shown in Figure 2.23, one needs to minimize strong reabsorption of the fluorescence signal for high sample solution concentrations by focusing the excitation beam near the wall of the cuvette close to the collecting lens.
--[p48]-- The selection of a reference molecule is based on the spectral range of the fluorescence ofthe sample. A large overlap ofthe fluorescence spectra will help minimize the measurement error induced by the spectral sensitivity of the detector. Several 2PF standards have been published with emission wavelengths ranging from 390 to 1000 nm and excitation wavelength from 500 to 1600 nm [73, 77, 78]. However, there are still spectral ranges where new/more standards are needed.
--[p49]-- ESA, which can follow 2PA because ESA does not contribute to the fluorescence. The sensitivity of 2PF can be very high, thanks to the high responsivity of photomultiplier tube (PMT) detectors and the fact that 2PF is a zero-background signal technique. A 2PA cross section of∼1 GM can be measured given a sample solution with large fluorescence quantum yield at high concentration.
However, there are molecules whose fluorescent quantum yield is too low to measure by 2PF.

--[p25]-- 2.2.1
Two-Photon Absorption and Bound-Electronic Nonlinear Refraction
The second term after the linear optics term in Bloembergen’s [8] expansion of the electric polarization
$$
P \sim \varepsilon _ { 0 } ( \chi ^ { ( 1 ) } E + \chi ^ { ( 2 ) } E ^ { 2 } + \chi ^ { ( 3 ) } E ^ { 3 } + \ldots ) ,\tag{2.1}

is the third-order polarization, which is characterized by the third-order nonlinear susceptibility $\chi ^ { ( 3 ) }$ .

===== [p261-263] Fabrication of Waveguides and Other Optical Elements by Multiphoton Lithography > a) No data available. (8块) =====
--[p262]-- Coumarin 6 was also added to the system to improve the contrast for fluorescence microscopy. An interesting application of MPL was pioneered by the group of Cojoc, who fabricated axicon lenses, convergent lenses, and ring-shaped phase masks on top of an optical fiber (Figure 10.13) [52]. NOA 63 was used as the building material because of its good adhesion to glass, easy processing, a suitable refractive index of1.56, and low cost.
--[p261]-- Following MPL, the development of the microstructures is performed with standard solvents including ethanol [50], ethyl acetate [29], and methanol. Thiol–ene resins have been used for several MPL experiments to build optical elements. Already in 1999, Prasad et al. used a mixture of NOA 72, the epoxy glue EPO-TEK 301 (Polytek PT), and the 2P initiator AF 183 to build optical elements such as beam splitters, grating couplers, and waveguides in bulk [49].
--[p261]-- a) No data available.
All NOA thiol–ene resins exhibit very good transparency in the visible and NIR regions. Multiphoton polymerization was generally initiated by an undisclosed initiator, which is also applicable to MPL. In one case, curing was induced with a 465 nm laser using an additional NIR two-photon absorbing fluorophore AF183 (6-benzothiazol-2-yl-2-naphtyl diphenylamine) to sensitize crosslinking of the NOA 72 resin [49].
--[p261]-- EPO-TEK 301 here acts as a hardener, which causes a rubber-like consistency of the NOA 72 film prior to MPL writing after thermal crosslinking. A refractive index diference of 0.0234 between the nonilluminated clad material and the illuminated waveguide core was suficient for waveguiding without an additional development step. NOA 72 was also used by Kirkpatrick et al. to construct periodic grid structures with a holographic MPL technique [51].
--[p263]-- To avoid phase separation, a siloxane dithiol monomer was synthesized and used with a commercial siloxane triallyl monomer. The siloxane thiol–ene system could be soaked in PDMS to give a swollen gel and then selectively crosslinked via MPL. An alternate strategy was to gel triacetoxy vinyl silane in the presence of thiol. In this case, the thiol and ene groups survived the tin-catalyzed condensation and could later be selectively patterned by MPL.
--[p261]-- In this case, the 2P initiator AF 380 was dissolved in NOA 72 in a benzene solution and spin-coated to give a film for MPL. Also using NOA 61, Sun et al. fabricated a Fresnel zone plate and multistep difractive phase lenses (Figure 10.12) [50].
In another report, NOA 63 was used to place line and point defects within ORMOCER replica PhC structures using isopropyl thioxanthone (ITX) as photoinitiator [29]. The PhC was first infiltrated with NOA 63 thinned with acetone.
--[p262]-- NOA 63 has also been used to fabricate optical rotors, which can be manipulated with the assistance oflaser tweezers [53]. In a diferent paper, microscopic difractive optical elements, amenable to laser-based optical trapping, were fabricated with NOA 81 [54].
Thiol–ene chemistry has been applied by our own research group as a tool for selectively templating waveguide structures within a siloxane prepolymer [55].
--[p263]-- This caused a refractive index increase of 0.013, which was suficient for waveguiding [56]. In another work, Griesser et al. selectively photocrosslinked ring-opening metathesis polymerization (ROMP)- derived polyenes with thiol monomers via MPL to fabricate lenses [57].

===== [p124-125] Photoinitiators for Multiphoton Absorption Lithography > 6.1.2.2 Property of Two-Photon Initiators (9块) =====
--[p125]-- Initiation: $\mathbb { S } \xrightarrow { \ h \mathrm { v } , \ h \mathrm { v } } \mathbb { S } ^ { \star } . . . \xrightarrow { \mathsf { I } } \mathbb { \Sigma } ^ { \star } \xrightarrow { } \mathbb { R }$
Propagation: $\mathsf { R } ^ { \cdot } + \mathsf { M } \longrightarrow \mathsf { R M } ^ { \cdot } \longrightarrow \mathsf { R M M } \cdots \longrightarrow \mathsf { R M } _ { n }$
--[p124]-- Generally, photoinitiators can be selected from any chemical compounds that can decompose into active species, such as free radicals and photoacid generators (PAGs) when exposed to light. Photoinitiators exist in nature and are widely used in the industry. In TPP, the photoinitiators play an important role as shown in Figure 6.1 [14]. Taking radical photopolymerization as an example, the photosensitizer in the photopolymerizable resin during the TPP process is excited by
--[p125]-- absorbing two photons simultaneously, and then fluorescence is emitted in the UV–vis region. Consequently, the fluorescence will be absorbed by the photoinitiators with good chemical reactivity and give rise to radicals. Then, the radicals react with monomers or oligomers, resulting in the production of monomer radicals, which would expand in a chain reaction until two radicals meet and undergo termination.
Finally, the low molecular weight monomers are polymerized into cross-linked, high molecular weight materials.
--[p124]-- Compared to liquid photoresists, solids and gels are more difficult and time consuming to prepare and process, but the complete restriction of motion in these media facilitates the fabrication of complex structures, such as those with free moving parts. There are two crucial components in a prepolymer photoresist for TPP: the photoinitiator and the monomers.
--[p124]-- Most of the applications for 2PA result from this intensity dependence. Two-photon absorption is a way of accessing a given excited state by using photons of half the energy (or twice the wavelength) of the corresponding one-photon transition, thus leading to other applications.
One ofthe most important considerations for developing photoresists for TPP is high transparency.
--[p125]-- Termination: ${ \mathsf { R M } } _ { n } \cdot { } + { \mathsf { R M } } _ { m } \cdot \ldots { \mathsf { R M } } _ { n + m } { \mathsf { R } }$
Figure 6.1 Schematic illustration of the TPP process. S stands for the photosensitizer, I denotes the photoinitiator, R<sup>•</sup> is the radical, and M is the monomer. S\* and I\* are
the excited states of the photosensitizer and photoinitiator, respectively, after absorbing the photon energy.
--[p124]-- Other components can include polymerization inhibitors to stabilize the photoresist and influence the feature size, photosensitizers, solvents to assist in casting, filler polymers to create gels or increase the viscosity of liquid resins, and other additives such as dye molecules that introduce new functions to the polymerized structures [13].
--[p124]-- 6.1.2.2 Property of Two-Photon Initiators
The main diference between one-photon absorption (1PA) and two-photon absorption (2PA) is that 2PA involves the simultaneous interaction of two photons, and so it increases with the square of the light intensity, whereas 1PA depends linearly on the intensity. This is the reason why 2PA is observed only under intense laser beams, particularly focused pulsed lasers, which generate a very high instantaneous photon density.
--[p124]-- On the other hand, the prepolymer photoresist used for MAL is usually a viscous liquid, an amorphous solid, or a gel, to prevent the escape ofthe reactive components during TPP. Liquid samples are easy to fabricate and process, but will also bring disadvantages, such as the undesired deformation of structures during fabrication.

===== [p60-63] Modeling of Polymerization Processes > Basic Laser Polymerization Chemistry and Kinetic Equations (28块) =====
--[p60]-- Basic Laser Polymerization Chemistry and Kinetic Equations
The chain-growth photopolymerization process [14] in the simplest case consists of three basic types of chemical reactions: photoinitiation, chain propagation, and chain termination. Light irradiation causes the destruction or excitement of the photoinitiator leading to the generation of molecules with active centers, such as free radicals, ions, and so on.
--[p61]-- In the following, we consider the free-radical polymerization. The simplest mathematical model of the polymerization kinetics has to describe the evolution of the number densities of the monomer $M ( \vec { r } , t )$ (or non-reacted moieties in multifunctional monomer species) and the active centers (within growing chains and crosslinked clusters) $R ( \vec { r } , t )$ .
--[p61]-- Because these gels are more stable relative to only physically connected polymer structures (e.g., by means of entanglement), the multifunctional monomers are very attractive for multiphoton lithography (MPL) applications.
The exact chemical reactions involved in the polymerization depend on the particular polymerizable resin. Typically, the monomer comprises an unsaturated bond or a cycle. The free radical or ion attaches to the bond or opens the cycle, thus propagating the chain.
--[p60]-- The lifetime of the active centers is limited by the chain termination reactions. The termination can occur, for example, because of the reaction of two radicals (quadratic termination), recombination of ions, or reaction of the active center with the inhibitor (also called the radical quencher for the case of the free-radical polymerization). The described reactions for the case of free-radical polymerization are shown in the following schematic.
Photoinitiation
$$
--[p60]-- { \mathrm { I n i t i a t o r } } { \xrightarrow { n \times h \nu } } A \bullet
$$
Chain propagation
$$
A \bullet + M  A - M \bullet
$$
$$
A - M \bullet + M  A - M - M \bullet
$$
$$
A - ( M ) _ { n } \bullet + M  A - ( M ) _ { n + 1 } \bullet
$$
Chain termination by
$$
A - ( M ) _ { n } \bullet + \bullet ( M ) _ { m } - A  A - ( M ) _ { n + m } - A
$$
recombination
disproportionation
$$
A - ( M ) _ { n } \bullet + \bullet ( M ) _ { m } - A  A - ( M ) _ { n } + ( M ) _ { m } - A
$$

Equation (3.6) can be used when the repetition time is much less than any characteristic reaction times (and also the times ofthe transfer ofheat and species). This is generally true for the laser systems working at a megahertz-scale repetition rate.
--[p61]-- This kind of reaction (also referred to as crosslinking) leads to the formation of branched polymer structures whose size significantly exceeds the average length of a single polymer chain. The process finally may result in the aggregation of distinct macromolecules into a single chemically bonded macroscopic network, the gel.
--[p61]-- This reflects the fact that localized initiation of the polymerization by the focused laser beam also results in localized chemical transformations.

--[p62]-- Alternately, a photoinitiator may be totally absent, relying on direct photoactivation of the monomer [20].

Another important parameter is the molar mass distribution (or molecular weight distribution) of the polymer chains. Equations (3.1)–(3.3) assume that the kinetic constants do not depend on the length of the macromolecules involved in the reactions. This assumption, known as Flory principle, is generally satisfied.
--[p63]-- In typical direct laser writing (DLW) conditions, however, the emerging heat is quickly removed from the polymerization domain as a result of thermal conductivity. That is, the time in which the heat dissipates from the laser-irradiated spot is estimated as $\tau _ { \mathrm { h e a t } } = { d _ { \mathrm { f o c u s } } } ^ { 2 } / \chi _ { \mathrm { r e s i n } }$ .
--[p60]-- If the monomer has several moieties that can undergo the chain propagation, then the active species can attach to the already reacted monomer molecules:

--[p62]-- The proper model [17] of this phenomenon considers diferent termination constants for long and short radicals.
Molar mass distribution of the growing radicals (see Section 3.5) is also important when considering their difusion [18], because of the dependence of the diffusivity on the size of a molecule.
The initiation kinetics depends on the particular photoinitiator employed in the resin. It may be a multistage process with complex dynamics [19].
--[p62]-- \frac { \partial Q } { \partial t } = - k _ { \mathrm { t R Q } } R Q .\tag{3.3}

In Eq. (3.1), $S ( \vec { r } , t )$ denotes the generation of the active species due to the photoinitiation; other terms describe the termination reactions. The kinetic constants for the chain propagation, the quadratic chain termination, and the chain termination due to inhibition are $k _ { \mathrm { p } } , k _ { \mathrm { t R R } }$ , and $k _ { \mathrm { t R Q } } ,$ respectively.
--[p60]-- Such active species become the starting elements of the macromolecular chains that further grow by adding many molecules of the monomer, which is the main component of the polymerizable resin. With each of the chain propagation steps, a monomer molecule is attached to the macromolecule’s active center in a way that it is transferred to the newly attached monomer. This allows for the growth of linear macromolecules.
--[p63]-- MPP is a photochemical process that does not rely on the temperature rise due to the laser irradiation. The highly energetic irradiation regimes can trigger unnecessary thermal processes, resulting in nanofabrication distortions and damage to the sample. Such regimes are typically avoided and thus are not considered here. In the modest irradiation regimes, the heating can possibly influence the kinetic constants ofthe polymerization process.
--[p63]-- In this case, the repetition time $R ^ { - 1 }$ is on the order of nanoseconds or less. Typical polymerization reaction and transfer times range from microseconds to milliseconds.
The consumption of the photoinitiator is given by
$$
\frac { \partial G ( \vec { r } , t ) } { \partial t } = - \frac { 1 } { 2 k _ { \mathrm { G } } } S ( \vec { r } , t ) ,\tag{3.7}
$$
where coeficient $k _ { \mathrm { G } }$ reflects the eficiency of the generation of the active species.
--[p62]-- S ( \vec { r } , t ) \approx \eta \tau _ { \mathrm { p u l s e } } R \frac { I _ { 0 } ^ { \ n } ( \vec { r } , t ) \sigma ^ { ( n ) } } { ( h \nu ) ^ { n } } G ( \vec { r } , t ) .\tag{3.6}
$$
Here, $\tau _ { \mathrm { p u l s e } }$ is the pulse length, R is the repetition timer, and $I _ { 0 } ( \vec { r } , t )$ is the peak pulse intensity distribution.
--[p62]-- When an active center reacts with an inhibitor molecule, both species are destroyed, as reflected in Eqs. (3.1) and (3.3). Equation (3.2) describes the consumption of the monomer due to the chain propagation.
The major parameter of the polymerization process is conversion, which is the fraction of reacted monomer species:
$$
p ( \vec { r } , t ) = \frac { M _ { 0 } - M ( \vec { r } , t ) } { M _ { 0 } } ,\tag{3.4}
$$
--[p62]-- An important deviation from this principle is concerned with autoacceleration efect (also known as gel efect, Trommsdorf–Norrish efect) [14], which are particularly important in the case of non-crosslinking (linear) radical polymerization. When reaching a certain conversion level, the quadratic termination of long radicals is hampered as a result of entanglements, thus resulting in a steep increase in the polymerization rate.
--[p61]-- Additionally, the inhibitor can be mixed with the resin to prevent occasional polymerization or to add the possibility to manipulate the polymerization process. In those cases, the evolution of the inhibitor number density $Q ( \vec { r } , t )$ has to be considered as well. It should be noted that the number densities depends not only on time t but also on the position $\vec { r }$ .

===== [p228-229] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Direct Writing of Continuous-Metal Microstructures (6块) =====
--[p228]-- This approach caused newly created NPs to aggregate, sinter, and become fused with the underlying layer (Figure 9.17). Lu et al. developed a medium for MPL of continuous gold metal based on ${ \mathrm { A u } } ^ { 3 + }$ and an ionic liquid. The ionic liquid helped the formation of gold NPs upon laser irradiation and encouraged their aggregation into continuous gold metal features [124].
Fourkas et $a l .$ reported a hybrid two-step approach for creating continuous metal features [97, 125].
--[p228]-- Direct Writing of Continuous-Metal Microstructures
When the goal is to create microstructures of continuous metal, then directly writing metallic features would appear to be the preferred route. However, this approach is not straightforward, because most chemical systems for forming metal in solution or viscous media tend to generate poly-nucleated and/or discontinuous NPs, rather than the bulk metal.
--[p228]-- Directly preparing metal microstructures from a liquid solution provides easier processing, but also pose new challenges in that NPs or other metal generated by MPL within a solution medium may not have a firm point of attachment on the substrate or other portions of the microstructure. Ishikawa et al. addressed this issue by scanning the focused laser at a slower rate and focusing at the surface of previously deposited metal [123].
--[p229]-- First, silver nitrate/PVP solutions were spin-coated onto substrates forming films. Then MPL was used to photo-reduce $\mathrm { A g ^ { + } }$ in the PVP films, forming silver lines [125]. This process could also be carried out on the surface of microstructures fabricated earlier by MPL using a metal-free photopolymer [97]. The silver lines were then enhanced with by copper EM, forming a microstructure with highly selective and conductive metallization.
--[p228]-- The first report of direct fabrication of metallized structures was presented by Stellacci et al. [9]. In their approach, a composite was formulated of poly(vinylcarbazole) (PVK), Ag NPs, $\mathrm { A g B F _ { 4 } }$ and a two-photon-excitable dye that could be spin-coated onto surfaces. Laser excitation of the dye activated electron transfer to the $\mathrm { A g ^ { + } }$ ions, which caused Ag NPs to grow further and fuse into continuous metal within the PVK matrix.
--[p228]-- The host polymer could then be dissolved using dichloromethane, leaving a freestanding metallic nanostructure. Maruo et al. also used a host matrix as a support to directly write silver lines and pyramids in a PVP matrix containing silver nitrate [121]. Metal–organic frameworks (MOFs) have also been used as a host matrix by Ameloot et al. to write micro-QR codes and microcircuits [122].
Although it is generally advantageous to use a host matrix, this may not always be possible.

===== [p63-69] Modeling of Polymerization Processes > Phenomenological Polymerization Threshold and Spatial Resolution (38块) =====
--[p66]-- D ^ { ( 2 ) } ( x , z , t ) = \frac { w \sqrt { \pi } } { V } \frac { \tau _ { \mathrm { p u l s e } } R I _ { \mathrm { m a x } } { } ^ { 2 } \sigma ^ { ( 2 ) } G _ { 0 } } { h \nu } \exp \left( - \frac { 4 x ^ { 2 } } { w _ { r } ^ { ~ 2 } } - \frac { 4 z ^ { 2 } } { w _ { z } ^ { ~ 2 } } \right) .\tag{3.15}

Then, in both cases of the voxel and the line, the dimension of the structure along the specified coordinate can be calculated.
--[p69]-- The growth of the conversion during the polymerization process also means an increase in the probability of the crosslinks. This results in the growth of the average mass of the crosslinked macromolecules containing many linear chains. Percolation theory [33] states that, at a certain conversion level, the average mass diverges and tends to infinity. At this point, the crosslinks result in the formation of the polymer gel, which is a macroscopic-sized network of covalently bonded chains.
--[p65]-- The high spatial resolution of MPP is provided by the sharp nonlinear response of the polymerizable resin to the laser radiation. The simplest phenomenological model for such a response relies on the polymerization threshold [25]. This is the minimum energy density that must be absorbed for the local transformation of the initial resin into the polymer that can withstand the development process.
Similar to Eq. (3.5), the multiphoton absorbed energy density is given by
$$

With formula (3.16), however, one cannot calculate the experimentally observed minimal size ofa polymer nanofeature.
--[p64]-- According to the above-mentioned estimations, such level of spatial resolution cannot be achieved only by sharp beam focusing. Even taking into account that in the case of the $n _ { \mathrm { p h } }$ th-order of multiphoton absorption the width ofthe absorbed intensity distribution is decreased by the factor of $\sqrt { n _ { \mathrm { p h } } }$ , the focusing is definitely not enough to provide sub-100-nm size √of a nanostructure.

--[p68]-- The difusion of species is considered in Section 3.5.
Another physical mechanism that explains the polymerization threshold is related to gelation. In the case of polymerization of multifunctional monomers, the crosslinking reaction results in random connections of distinct polymer chains by covalent bonds. The basic understanding of the behavior of such a system can be provided by the percolation theory [32].
--[p67]-- Another beam with vortex shape is used for the photodepletion of the radicals. The volume in which the polymerization is efectively carried out is narrowed, and thus the spatial resolution is improved. There are several variations of this scheme, which employ femtosecond and continuous wave (CW) lasers, as well as two-color and single-color setups. This technique is described in detail in Chapter 5. The theoretical model of SPIN is given in [28].
--[p67]-- There are at least two possibilities to improve the spatial resolution in this manner. One of them relies on the concept of super-resolution photoinducedinhibition nanolithography (SPIN), which is similar to stimulated emission depletion microscopy (STED) [10–13]. Basically, the writing is performed with two beams. One beam with a standard Gaussian-like shape is used for the photoinitiation of the polymerization, that is, generation of free radicals.
--[p66]-- For a Ti:sapphire laser operating at 800 nm, in the case of the two-photon absorption, $2 L _ { \mathrm { l } } \approx 1 8 2$ nm and $2 L _ { \mathrm { z } } \approx 6 7 5 \mathrm { n m }$ , taking into account the above estimations of the dimensions of the focal spot. Because of the difraction limit, the spatial resolution is diferent from the minimum feature size.
It is important to note that the polymerization threshold is a local approximation.
--[p66]-- For instance, the thickness of the line along the coordinate x is
$$

As with any phase transition, the formation of the gel sufers from fluctuations in a finite-size domain. Those fluctuations are not caused by the instabilities of the laser beam shape and intensity; instead, they are related to the inhomogeneities in the gel [26, 35]. Such inhomogeneities are one of the factors that determine the minimal feature size.
The basic study of this phenomenon is given in the following section.
--[p66]-- If $2 L _ { l } < w _ { r } / \sqrt { 2 } , D ^ { ( 2 ) } ( \vec { r } )$ has a single global maximum at $\vec { r } = 0$ (see Figure 3.3b). Thus, regardless of the actual level of the threshold, it is not possible to resolve two voxels at such a distance: a single polymer feature only in the center can be formed instead. Similarly, the longitudinal resolution limit of $2 L _ { z } > w _ { z } / \sqrt { 2 }$ can be derived.

--[p68]-- The difusion ofthe inhibitor is one ofthe factors that determines the duration of the dark polymerization (after the irradiation) at a point in the resin [31]. This factor limits the maximum conversion that can be reached with short-time irradiation schemes. One can expect, however, that the difusion may be efectively disabled by bonding the inhibitor molecules to a prebuilt matrix, for example, to the inorganic gel of a hybrid polymerizable material.
--[p63]-- Phenomenological Polymerization Threshold and Spatial Resolution
Writing of 3D structures requires far-field laser beam focusing. Thus, the minimum achievable size of the focal spot is limited by difraction. To reduce this size, objectives with high numerical aperture as well as the beams with complex polarization (e.g., radially polarized beams [21]) or spatial field structure (e.g., Bessel beams [22, 23]) are employed.
--[p68]-- As a starting point, it is important to understand the possible physical mechanisms behind the phenomenological polymerization threshold. At the larger spatial scale when the influence of the nonlocal phenomena becomes negligible, at least two such mechanisms are possible.
When the inhibitor (radical quencher) is involved in the polymerization, the radicals generated by the photoinitiator are first depleted by the inhibitor.

--[p64]-- For a typical MPP setup consisting of the Ti:sapphire laser operating at the wavelength 800 nm and the objective of $\mathrm { N A } = 1 . 4$ and $n = 1 . 5$ , the minimum focal spot dimensions are estimated as $w _ { r } \approx 2 5 7$ nm and $w _ { z } \approx 9 5 5 \mathrm { n m }$ . --[p67]-- Another approach [29, 30] relies on the depletion of the radicals by mobile quencher (inhibitor) molecules specially added in the resin. The irradiation is performed in a regime when the quencher consumption at the beam maximum is compensated by the difusion from the outside of the irradiated volume. As a result, a nonequilibrium, quasi-stationary spatial distribution of the quencher concentration is formed. The distribution profile has a flat zero minimum around the beam maximum.
--[p67]-- This means that the polymerization is considered independently in the neighboring subdomains of the medium. Any size-dependent nonlocal phenomena, such as transfer of molecules, finite length of polymer chains, and size-dependent fluctuations, are neglected. Thus, the spatial resolution limit can be possibly overcome when performing the laser writing in a regime that essentially deviates from the local threshold concept.
