===== [p232-233] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Structures Functionalized by NP Coating (5块) =====
--[p233]-- demonstrated an efective method for tuning the refractive index of ORMOCER 3D woodpile PCs coated with CdS NPs [138]. The PC surface was first pretreated with 3-aminopropyl-triethoxysilane solution in toluene to ensure NP attachment [138]. Then, CdS NPs were deposited using a modified chemical bath deposition (CBD) approach. As the thickness of CdS layers deposited onto the PC lattice increased, the stopband increased in amplitude and shifted to longer wavelength by as much as 375 nm.
--[p233]-- The shift in the stopband was attributed to an increase in both the rod thickness and the efective refractive index, whereas the increase in amplitude was attributed to the increase in efective refractive index.
--[p232]-- [136] and had a quantum eficiency of more than 85% [135]. The NPs were dissolved in a chloroform or toluene solution, and then drop-casted onto the PC, which dispersed them evenly throughout the structure. The stopband of the PC was observed to increase with concentration of NPs in the solution because of the increased fill factor and refractive index. The emission spectrum of the structure exhibited redistributed spontaneous emission [135].
--[p232]-- Structures Functionalized by NP Coating
Semiconductor NPs can also be integrated into microstructures by first fabricating them by MPL in a photopolymer and then chemically functionalizing the microstructure surface with the NPs [12, 135]. Three-dimensional woodpile PCs with a bandgap centered at a wavelength of 2.04 μm were fabricated using an ORMOCER and then infiltrated with PbSe NPs for the first time by Li et al. [128, 135]. PbSe NPs were prepared by the method described by Yu et al.
--[p232]-- Three-dimensional woodpile PCs with planar defects were fabricated in an ORMOCER and coated with PbSe/CdSe core–shell NPs [137]. A molecular linking method was used to ensure that the NPs were attached to the PC surface (Figure 9.19). The polymerized ORMOCER structure was functionalized with an amino group prior to immersing in the NP solution to obtain multiple layers. The emission peak overlapped with the stop gap of the PC, which enhanced the spontaneous emission.
Buso et al.

===== [p179-179] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > Thiol–ene photoaddition (3块) =====
--[p179]-- Alternating steps of propagation and chain transfer give rise to a 1 : 1 addition of thiol- and ene-containing monomers, provided that the ene functionality exhibits no homopolymerization. Thiol–ene reactions proceed most quickly for electron-rich (e.g., vinyl ethers) or strained (e.g., norbornene) monomers. Notably, thiol–ene polymerizations work well with type II initiators, as the monomeric thiol can also serve as the hydrogen-donating co-initiator [70].
--[p179]-- The reaction has been exploited for the formation of biomaterials derived from synthetic [71, 72] and natural polymers [73, 74], the formation of cyclic peptides [75], as well as in the biochemical decoration of ene-containing hydrogel networks [70, 76–78].
--[p179]-- Thiol–ene photoaddition
In this step-growth polymerization reaction between thiols and alkenes [62, 69], an active photoinitiator first abstracts a thiolic hydrogen to form a thiyl radical. The thiyl then propagates across the ene functional group to form a carbon-centered radical, which then abstracts a hydrogen from another thiol.

===== [p251-259] Fabrication of Waveguides and Other Optical Elements by Multiphoton Lithography > Acrylate Monomers for Multiphoton Lithography (40块) =====
--[p253]-- An innovative approach to fabricate a feedback laser resonator was realized by the group of Duan, who used a carbosilane dendrimer functionalized with an allyl derivative of fluorescein to structure SCR500 [17]. Every Si–H terminated branch of the dendrimer was linked to the allyl group of the dye via hydrosilylation.
--[p254]-- The monomer is a clear liquid with a viscosity of 520 cP at $2 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ exhibiting low volatility and fast cure response in free-radical polymerization [20]. The glass transition temperature ofthe homopolymer ofPETA is $1 0 3 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ . The refractive index of the uncured monomer (1.4801) is comparatively low for optical applications. Mixed acrylate films for MPL containing PETA are prepared by first dissolving the components in chloroform.
--[p253]-- Other benefits include high reactivity, endowing polymers with scratch, chemical, and heat resistance [20]. Thin uniform films of TITA are formed by casting followed by solvent evaporation or by using calibrated spacers between glass plates [21]. Fourkas et $a l .$ pretreated the glass substrate with methacryloxypropyltrimethoxysilane (MEMO) to enhance the adhesion with the photopolymer formulation [22].
--[p253]-- Another acrylate resin that is frequently employed for MPL of photonic elements is tris(2-hydroxyethyl)isocyanurate triacrylate (TITA in Figure 10.1). TITA is a solid at room temperature but exhibits a rather low viscosity above its melting temperature of $5 2 { - } 5 4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ [19]. The polar isocyanurate ring of this monomer provides films with excellent adhesion, low brittleness, high strength, and good weathering properties.
--[p256]-- MPL was then used to introduce nonperiodic defects. Removal of unreacted resin was unproblematic with ethanol [32].
In another paper from Braun and coworkers, 3D-defects were created within a silica colloidal crystal by means ofMPL. A preliminary high-index replication step followed by removal of the opal template allows well-defined defects within a 3D silicon PhC. This allows waveguiding of NIR radiation around sharp bends within a complete PBG material [31].
--[p258]-- Contrast is improved with high-refractive-index monomers such as for 2-phenylthioethyl acrylate [41].
An analogous process for fabricating optical waveguides was developed by the group of Kern [42]. They used phenyl and benzyl methacrylate as high-refractiveindex monomers, which do not interfere with hydrosilylation. This allows a process in which curing of the silicone rubber can be accomplished within the
MPL reactive monomer formulation.
--[p255]-- The authors then used an unconventional route for postprocessing: After MPL structuring of the waveguide cores, the surrounding unreacted resin, which consisted of oxetanes (cationic curable monomers) and residual uncured acrylate (radical monomers), was photopolymerized with UV light. Only under these conditions could the cationic initiator be activated eficiently.
--[p258]-- The extent of swelling was controlled via the ratio of acrylic acid isobornyl ester, which is compatible with PDMS, and 1,4-butanediol diacrylate crosslinker, which has poor PDMS compatibility. MPL structuring of waveguides was followed by removal ofunreacted monomers under reduced pressure. More recently, swelling has been minimized by mixing the monomer formulation and the liquid polysiloxane matrix material with orthogonal reactivity prior to selective matrix curing [40].
--[p256]-- This approach makes use of colloidal self-assembly to form large-scale periodic structures, which is a method that may not be so well suited for MPL due to its lower throughput. Nevertheless, the high resolution and 3D flexibility of MPL allow well-defined defects in the size range of the lattice period. This was detected by means of the fluorescent dye FITC1, which emits in the region of the PBG of the PhC.
--[p256]-- This trifunctional monomer has a low viscosity of106 cP at $2 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ and cures to give polymers with good resistance to water, chemicals, abrasion, and heat. Volatility of the monomer is low, and curing proceeds rapidly. The homopolymer has a glass transition temperature of $6 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ and a relatively low refractive index of1.4723. Braun et al. used TTA to structure waveguides within a PhC (Figure 10.7) [31–35].
--[p252]-- SCR500 from Japan Synthetic Rubber is an acrylate-based formulation described in the literature for MPL that consists of diferent urethane acrylate oligomers with molecular weights from 480 to 1200 Da. While the exact composition of SCR500 is not disclosed, the molecular weight of the monomers and the diluting agents is suficiently low to allow a viscosity of about 850 mPa s. SCR500 contains either Irgacure 369 or Irgacure 184 (BASF) as radical photoinitiators.
--[p252]-- The existence of a high-quality photonic bandgap (PBG) was confirmed using transmission spectroscopy.
Sun et al. studied the temperature dependence of PhC writing in SCR500 [16]. They predicted that the structuring resolution could be greatly improved by decreasing the process temperature. This hypothesis was based on the suppression of local photo-induced radicals and the reduction of Brownian motion.
--[p253]-- The monomer and other components such as binder and photoinitiator can be mixed in chloroform, in which case the solvent is subsequently removed after casting [21]. This method was applied by Dorkenoo et $a l .$ , who succeeded in connecting optical fibers with waveguide structures. Furthermore, they were able to fabricate Y-splitters and a March–Zehnder structure.
--[p253]-- The need for regulation of the refractive index contrast between the core and clad of a waveguide can make it necessary to prepolymerize the matrix with white light [21]. Dimethylformamide (DMF) [22], ethanol [23], and methanol [24] are all potential solvents for the development of TITA-based resins. Rinsing duration is on a time scale of seconds to minutes. TITA has been used in various formulations, which were modified for MPL with the intention of fabricating optical elements.
--[p252]-- To maximize the dispersion of titania within the system, titanium ethoxide was reacted with methacrylic acid and mixed with SCR500 using 2,2-diethoxy-acetophenone as initiator. After MPL processing of this novel resin, $\mathrm { T i O } _ { 2 }$ was formed via the hydrolysis of the $\mathrm { T i ^ { 4 + } }$ –methacrylate complexes. Finally, development with ethanol and thermal post-treatment were carried out.
--[p251]-- Linear aliphatic chains and polyether units (poly(ethylene glycol) and poly(propylene glycol)) or polycaprolactone units between the acrylate functionalities tend to decrease the glass transition temperature of the cured polymer while monomers with aromatic spacers give stif materials. By choosing the right set of monomers and reactive diluents, the mechanical properties can be tuned over a broad range from elastomer-like polymers to hard and stif materials.
--[p251]-- Low molecular weight reactive diluents such as TTA (trimethylpropane triacrylate) or hexane-1,6-diol diacrylate (HDDA) form tightly crosslinked networks with good resistance against solvent swelling. These monomers have generally low viscosity and high reactivity, but, due to the higher number of reactive groups per gram, greater polymerization shrinkage is exhibited. To account for this, higher molecular weight monomers such as ETA (Figure 10.1) can be applied.
--[p257]-- Another example of using MPL to create flexible waveguide structures is given by Krivec et al. In this case, low-refractive-index, organically modified silica monoliths were prepared, which were mechanically flexible and served as a host for polymeric, acrylate-based waveguides [36]. The porous monoliths were fabricated using a surfactant-based template (Figure 10.8).
--[p255]-- In other studies, PETA was applied by Ober and coworkers in a resin with the multiphoton initiator NOPD and the fluorescent dye FITC1 (Figure 10.5) [29]. NOPD, a novel two-photon photoacid generator (PAG), is able to initiate both radical and cationic polymerization reactions [30].
The same group also applied MPL to create 3D defects within resin-filled organic modified ceramic (ORMOCER) PhCs with inverse opal features.
--[p259]-- The method was used to produce optical interconnects for printed circuit boards. A refractive index diference of 0.02 between the illuminated core and the nonilluminated cladding material was achieved. The authors suggest that the optical damping value of $1 . 6 9 \mathrm { d B c m ^ { - 1 } }$ obtained from cut-back measurements can be improved by changing the shape of the waveguides.
--[p254]-- in a 50 : 50 wt% blend with the alkoxylated difunctional acrylate Sartomer 9008 along with 4,4<sup>′</sup>-bis(di-n-butylamino)biphenyl or E,E-1,4-bis[4-(di-n-butylamino)styryl]-2,5-dimethoxybenzene as initiator. Woodpile PhCs with 60 nm line widths were constructed (Figure 10.4), which exhibited stop bandgaps in the near-IR spectral region [24].
--[p253]-- In this case, poly(styrene-co-acrylonitrile) served as the binder and 1,13-bis-[4-(diethylamino)phenyl]-tri-deca-1,3,5,6,8,10,12- hexaen-7-one as initiator [25]. Fourkas et $a l .$ used TITA with dipentaerythritol pentaacrylate as co-monomer and Lucirin-TPO-L as initiator to create a high-performance micro-ring resonator (Figure 10.3) [23].
TITA was used by Perry et al.
--[p259]-- Waveguide patterns were produced with diferent laser powers, and it was found that contrast and optical loss of the waveguides were improved at higher power, although burning was observed beyond $2 6 0 \mu \mathrm { W } .$ With optimized process conditions, a bundle of seven waveguides with core diameters of approximately 45 μm was produced (Figure 10.10). Crosstalk between waveguide channels within the bundle was not observed.
--[p258]-- After removal of the template, the host material was infiltrated with highrefractive-index 2-phenoxyethyl acrylate (POEA) and the initiator NDPD (Figure 10.8) [37]. MPL yielded waveguide structures with good refractive index contrast (Figure 10.9).
Another concept is derived from photolocking: A low-refractive-index polydimethyl siloxane (PDMS) film was swollen with an acrylic mixture containing the initiator B3K [8, 38, 39].
--[p254]-- Another frequently used monomer for MPL is pentaerythritol triacrylate (PETA in Figure 10.1), a trifunctional monomer that is well known from applications including binder adhesives, coatings, and inks. PETA gives highly crosslinked networks because of its three acrylate moieties. The free hydroxyl group allows better adhesion, lower volatility, and the possibility for subsequent chemical modification [26].
--[p256]-- Scattering was avoided by matching the refractive index of the resin (1.5) with that of the ORMOCER material of the inverse opal PhC. Figure 10.6 gives an overview of the process starting from the ORMOCER inverse opal.
Another monomer that is applied for the introduction of defects in PhC structures is TTA, shown in Figure 10.1.
--[p255]-- The cationic component of the resin, 3-ethyl-3-ethyloxetane-3-yl methoxy methyl oxetane, remained stable throughout this step and was excluded from the core volume. The refractive index of the core material was in this way increased relative to the surrounding unreacted material. Radical-induced cationic polymerization was found to play only a minor role.
--[p251]-- Acrylate Monomers for Multiphoton Lithography
Acrylate monomers are the principal components of resins for traditional stereolithography (SLA) and, unsurprisingly, are widely used for MPL as well. A wide variety of (meth)acrylates ranging in molecular weights, structural moieties, and degree of functionality are commercially available.
--[p255]-- Infiltration of existing PhCs with the resin followed by MPL has been used to create well-controlled defects within the PhC [29]. Kaino et al. [27] used a resin with both radical and cationic monomers (PETA as crosslinking agent with tert-butyl-calix-8-arene acrylate) crosslinked via MPL to form an optical waveguide core. 3,2-Benzimidazolyl-7-diethylamino coumarin was used as a two-photon absorber and sensitizer for the initiator lauryl peroxide.
--[p251]-- The popularity of acrylates for lithography arises principally from the generally rapid rate of propagation with an eficient termination in the presence of oxygen, which allows excellent spatial and temporal control of the polymerization process [9, 10]. With viscosity, reactivity, and shrinkage all contributing to the performance of a resin, monomers must be selected carefully. Some common and commercially available acrylate monomers are shown in Figure 10.1.
--[p254]-- For thicker films, polymers such as polymethylmethacrylate (PMMA) can be added to increase the formulation’s viscosity [27]. Recently de Miguel et al. used a two-step lithographic process with PETA resin and isopropylthioxanthone (ITX) as photoinitiator to achieve �/20 axial control of features. The first step was MPL with high laser power, while the second step was to induce well-defined thermal expansion with lower laser power [28].
--[p256]-- First, a silicon–air PhC was created from an SU-8 template, which had been formed beforehand via interference lithography. The advantage of this method is that periodic structures are formed in a relatively high-throughput manner. In the next step, the PhC structures were infiltrated with a MPL-sensitive TTA-based resin. The authors used 0.1 wt% of AF-350 (tris[4- (7-benzothiazol-2-yl-9,9-diethylfluoren-2-yl)phenyl]amine) as initiator and a boron dipyromethene fluorescence dye for imaging.
--[p253]-- This indicates a possible method for minimizing unwanted efects such as floating or distortion during laser scanning. Conducting MPL at elevated temperature gave less crosslinked voxels because of enhanced chain termination and thus a lower polymerization rate. Finally, the PBGs of the PhCs were tailored via thermal processing. In some other studies, Xia and coworkers were able to manufacture 15-μm-scale micro-spherical and Fresnel lenses with SCR500 [13].
--[p253]-- The novel dye was then mixed with SCR500 in chloroform, enabling a higher doping concentration of fluorescein without self-aggregation, as was the case with conventional fluorescein doping. SCR500 was used more recently with 2,7- dibromo-3,6-bis(3,7-dimethyloctyloxy)9,9-spirobifluorenone as a two-photon sensitizer to test an autofocus method, which allowed voxel position precision of ±0.045 μm [18].
--[p259]-- The same group has more recently used a related technique to photocrosslink acrylate-terminated oligosiloxanes giving waveguides with low optical loss $( 0 . 6 5 \mathrm { d B c m ^ { - 1 } }$ at 1310 nm) [43].
--[p252]-- The glass slide provides the additional benefit of excluding moisture, which can hydrolyze $\mathrm { T i ^ { 4 + } }$ complexes that are sometimes added [15].
In 2004, Duan et al. reported the MPL fabrication of a diamond photonic crystal (PhC) using SCR500 as the building material (Figure 10.2) [15]. In order to increase the refractive index, $\mathrm { T i O } _ { 2 }$ nanoparticles were integrated in the photopolymer.
--[p252]-- The cured material exhibits a modulus of 160 MPa and a tensile strength of 6.0 MPa, along with a strain at failure of 10% and a glass transition temperature of $1 4 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ [12]. The refractive index before and after polymerization is given as 1.52 and 1.53, respectively [13]. Spin coating is used to provide thin, uniform films for MPL. Covering the film with glass slides prevents oxygen inhibition and maintains film thickness during the building process [14].
--[p253]-- On the contrary, the voxel size at $- 6 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ , which is significantly below the freezing point of SCR500, was found to be only slightly smaller than at ambient temperature. While the hypothesis was not proven, a phenomenon was discovered, in which the previously formed radicals in the frozen state did not polymerize until the resin was heated above $1 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ .
--[p256]-- Unreacted resin was removed from the sample by rinsing it with ethyl acetate for 30 s. To fabricate the PhC, PMMA colloidal particles were self-assembled to give an opal template, and then infiltrated with ORMOCER. In the next step, the ORMOCER was UV-polymerized, and then the PMMA template was removed with tetrahydrofuran (THF) [29].
--[p251]-- While such monomers do increase the overall viscosity of the resin significantly, reduction in shrinkage is perhaps more critical particularly for layer-by-layer lithographic techniques where delamination and curling must be avoided [11]. Aside from the molecular weight of the monomers, the selection of functional groups in the backbone is also important. Urethane groups provide interchain hydrogen bridges, allowing improved mechanical properties, but also an increase in viscosity.

===== [p49-49] 2 Characterization of 2PA Chromophores > 2.3.2.5 Fluorescence Anisotropy (5块) =====
--[p49]-- 2.3.2.5 Fluorescence Anisotropy
One-photon excitation fluorescence anisotropy measurements, especially when linked to quantum chemical calculations, can reveal the spectral positions and orientations of the transition dipole moments from the ground state $\mathrm { S } _ { 0 }$ to the first excited state $\mathrm { S } _ { 1 } ( \mu _ { 0 1 } )$ and to higher excited states $\mathrm { S } _ { n } ( \mu _ { 0 n } )$ relative to the orientation of the emission dipole moment $( \mu _ { 1 0 } )$ .
--[p49]-- Figure 2.25 shows the fluorescence anisotropy spectra for both 1PA and 2PA for a squaraine dye along with other data described in the next section [82].
--[p49]-- The angle $\beta$ between the absorption transition moment and the emission transition moment can be determined from [74]
$$
r = \frac { 2 } { 5 } \left( \frac { \cos ^ { 2 } \beta - 1 } { 2 } \right) .\tag{2.42}
$$
Similarly, 2PA anisotropy can be measured by two-photon excitation and measuring the ratio of parallel and perpendicular polarizations, which for a three-level system gives $r _ { \mathrm { 2 P A } }$ as [80]
$$
--[p49]-- This information cannot be fully obtained from linear absorption spectra. The excitation anisotropy spectrum r(�) is defined as
$$
r ( \lambda ) = [ I _ { | | } ( \lambda ) - I _ { \perp } ( \lambda ) ] / [ I _ { | | } ( \lambda ) + 2 I _ { \perp } ( \lambda ) ] ,\tag{2.41}
$$
where $I _ { | | }$ and $I | _ { \perp }$ ||<sub>are the fluorescence intensities polarized parallel and perpendic-</sub> ular to the excitation light, respectively [79].
--[p49]-- r _ { \mathrm { 2 P A } } = \frac { 1 8 \cos ( \gamma / 2 - \beta _ { 0 1 } ) \cos \beta _ { 0 1 } \cos \gamma / 2 - 7 \cos ^ { 2 } \gamma / 2 + 1 } { 7 ( 2 \cos ^ { 2 } \gamma / 2 + 1 ) } .\tag{2.43}
$$
Here, $\gamma$ is the angle between dipole moments participating in the 2PA process, and $\beta _ { 0 1 }$ is the angle between the bisector of $\dot { \gamma }$ and the emission transition moment, which can be found from one-photon anisotropy measurements in Eq. (2.42) [81].

===== [p167-167] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > 8.1 (1块) =====
--[p167]-- 8.1

===== [p297-298] Microfluidic Devices Produced by Two-Photon-Induced Polymerization > Introduction (9块) =====
--[p298]-- In the two-photon process, a photopolymer absorbs two near-infrared photons simultaneously in a single quantum event whose collective energy corresponds to the UV region of the spectrum. The rate of two-photon absorption is proportional to the square of the light intensity, so that the near-infrared light is strongly absorbed only at the focal point within the photopolymer.
--[p298]-- The quadratic intensity dependence of two-photon absorption allows the confinement of the solidification to submicrometer dimensions, beyond the difraction limit of light [10]. Two-photon microfabrication has widely been used for many applications including photonics, biology, lab-on-a-chip systems, and microelectromechanical systems (MEMS), as described elsewhere in this book [11]. Our group has proposed and developed several types of optically driven microfluidic devices.
--[p297]-- Additionally, several types of modular functional microfluidic devices such as micropumps, microvalves, and micromixers were developed and assembled into a micro total-analysis system on demand [8]. Although the concept of chemical IC chips is an innovative and eficient strategy, each modular microfluidic chip is made by the layer-by-layer process of stereolithography.
--[p298]-- In this chapter, we introduce optically driven microfluidic devices including micropumps and micromanipulators using movable microcomponents fabricated by two-photon microfabrication.
--[p297]-- For example, they succeeded in making 3D microstructures such as a microcoil of $7 0 \mu \mathrm { m }$ diameter with sub-10-μm resolution by the optimization of both the optical system and the sensitivity of the photopolymer. In addition, they utilized the microstereolithography for the production of 3D microfluidic devices called chemical IC chips [7].
--[p297]-- At that time, most of microfluidic devices were limited to two-dimensional microchannels, because they were made by photolithographic patterning on glass or silicon substrates and the subsequent bonding process. On the other hand, the chemical IC chips were constructed of 3D microchannels made by stereolithography. The 3D microchannels provide several advantages such as minimization of the total size of the microfluidic devices and improvement of mixing eficiency.
--[p297]-- Therefore, both the lateral and depth resolutions of stereolithography are still restricted to several tens of micrometers due to the layer-by-layer nature of the technique.
As an alternative ultrahigh-precision microstereolithography, Maruo et al. utilized two-photon-induced polymerization for producing 3D microstructures using a photopolymer [9]. This fabrication process is called two-photon microfabrication or two-photon microstereolithography.
--[p297]-- The original concept of stereolithography was first proposed and demonstrated as a form of stereolithography by Kodama in 1981 [5]. In his system, layer-by-layer photopolymerization according to the sliced data of the desired 3D structure was utilized to produce complex 3D objects. By using the layer-by-layer process, Ikuta and Hirowatari first developed a microstereolithography system that could produce sophisticated 3D microstructures whose sizes were in the range of 10–100 μm in 1993 [6].
--[p297]-- Introduction
Three-dimensional (3D) printing technologies have attracted much attention because of their ability to produce complex 3D structures with a wide variety of materials. There are several approaches for 3D printing, including fused deposition modeling, inkjet methods, laser sintering, and stereolithography [1–4]. In particular, stereolithography is one of the most precise 3D printing techniques using photopolymers as materials for 3D objects.

===== [p317-317] Microfluidic Devices Produced by Two-Photon-Induced Polymerization > 13 (1块) =====
--[p317]-- 13

===== [p193-194] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > Outlook (7块) =====
--[p193]-- The broad scope of amenable chemistries for biomaterial and hydrogel synthesis (Section 8.4) and the increasing accessibility of multiphoton technologies will allow MPL-fabricated biomaterials to impact the fields of tissue engineering and regenerative medicine. Specifically, highly defined tissue replacements can be formed from spatially tailored MPL biomaterials for organ therapy.
--[p193]-- Ideally, contributions will also be made to the toolbox of chemistries that enable multiphoton addition and cleavage reactions within fabricated biomaterials for advanced studies of how dynamic changes in the biophysical and biochemical nature of the ECM influence cell and tissue function. Expanding this toolbox will require interactions among polymer chemists, photochemists, materials scientists, and biologists to develop advanced techniques for MPL biomaterials.
--[p193]-- Outlook
Combining traditional photopolymerization techniques with advances in multiphoton lithography through MPL enables the fabrication of a useful class of photopolymerized biomaterials with defined geometry, architecture, and micrometer-scale features.
--[p194]-- Continued development on the access to multiphoton sources, which facilitate the necessary techniques for MPL and software that makes these approaches more user-friendly, will also increase use. As the 3D printing industry matures, many of the hardware and software approaches, in principle, can be adopted for advanced photopolymerization approaches via MPL. In total, the outlook for the use ofMPL photopolymerizations for the fabrication and modification of biomaterials and hydrogels is bright.
--[p194]-- Continued interactions at the interface of photopolymerizations, materials science, and biology will allow MPL biomaterials find broad use in the fields of tissue engineering, regenerative medicine, and stem cell biology.
--[p193]-- Water-soluble and biocompatible photoinitiators are particularly attractive for hydrogel manipulation via photopatterning in the presence of cells. Additionally, alternative chemistries for the crosslinking of polymer networks and hydrogels that are compatible with cell culture and especially those that facilitate dynamic or reversible crosslinking are desirable.
--[p193]-- Furthermore, modulatable hydrogels with dynamic subcellular biophysical and biochemical patterning will unlock new understanding in stem cell biology.
As these approaches see continued use and broader applications, there remains a need for an extended toolbox for MPL photopolymerizations. Users will benefit from novel biocompatible photoinitiators with eficient multiphoton cross sections that respond to unique wavelengths.

===== [p112-116] 5 STED-Inspired Approaches to Resolution Enhancement > Photoinhibition (19块) =====
--[p112]-- They were able to attain features as small as 64 nm in diameter with this technique, even though the excitation was based on linear absorption. Gu and coworkers developed a new photoresist formulation, which included the same photoinhibitor, and were able to achieve a 40-nm feature size with CW 488-nm excitation and 375-nm deactivation beams [52].
Gu and coworkers went on to apply the photoinhibition approach to resolution enhancement in multiphoton polymerization [53].
--[p116]-- Also, as with STED, RAPID also has the advantage that the photoinitiator is regenerated by the deactivation step.
The implementations of RAPID so far also have some distinct disadvantages. First, the surface roughness with the MGCB photoresist is greater than for similar resists with conventional photoinitiators, even when deactivation is not employed. Second, the best resolution in a STED-inspired fabrication technique should arise when the deactivation wavelength is as short as possible.
--[p116]-- For the MGCB photoresist, the deactivation eficiency decays quickly as the wavelength used moves to smaller than 800 nm. Finally, as noted above, there is a polymerization channel that cannot be deactivated with the MGCB photoresist. This channel has a significant influence on the ultimate feature pitch that will be able to be attained. Further materials will need to be tested to determine whether these issues can be addressed.
--[p114]-- The lifetime of the state that is deactivated must, instead, be considerably longer than the laser repetition time. This discovery led us to test whether deactivation could be accomplished using a CW laser. As shown in Figure 5.6c, CW radiation is indeed efective for deactivation, obviating the need for synchronized excitation and deactivation pulses.
Additional experiments revealed that at high enough excitation intensities, complete deactivation is not possible [56].
--[p115]-- Our previous kinetic analysis revealed a number ofcriteria that are desirable in a material for resolution enhancement through photodeactivation [12]: (i) the state that is deactivated should not be the state that is excited, but it should be reached as quickly as possible after the first state is excited; (ii) the state that is deactivated should have as long a lifetime as possible so that initiation does not compete with deactivation; and (iii) the state that is deactivated should have a large extinction coeficient so that deactivation can occur eficiently. These conditions cannot all be met simultaneously in a STED scheme.
--[p112]-- However, this property also means that both photoinitiator and photoinhibitor are used up every time the two beams are incident on a given area of the sample, instead of being regenerated, as in STED. Because of this efect, when fabricating close-packed features, the fabrication parameters must be adjusted based on the local density of features and the number of writing passes that have been performed already in that region [53].
--[p114]-- Ifthe solvated electron difuses away from the cation, then it will eventually initiate polymerization and cannot be deactivated. These ideas received additional support from experiments on other related species, which indicated that initiation does not occur directly from the state to which the molecule is excited [44]. Nevertheless, it has not yet been proven conclusively that this mechanism is responsible for the observed deactivation.
--[p114]-- Our proposed mechanism for initiation and deactivation of MGCB is shown in Figure 5.7. An electron that is driven out of an MGCB molecule can be solvated in the surrounding photoresist, where it can lead to the relatively slow initiation of polymerization. If the electron remains in the vicinity of the MGCB cation that ejected it, subsequent irradiation can lead to photoinduced backtransfer ofthe electron, regenerating the photoinitiator.
--[p116]-- It is clear that RAPID with MGCB meets criterion (ii) above. Although the initiation time has not been measured yet, a lower limit would be on the order of 100 ns based on available data. The intensities that are needed for deactivation indicate that the extinction coeficient is comparable to that of STED systems as well. It will require further experimentation to ascertain whether the first criterion is met by this system.
--[p114]-- We expected to be able to measure the excited state lifetime via the broadening of the lines with increasing delay time. Surprisingly, the eficiency of deactivation was completely independent of delay time over this window (Figure 5.6b) [56]. The lifetime of a strongly fluorescent excited state would be expected to be on the order of nanoseconds, so these results imply that MGCB is deactivated primarily through a mechanism that does not involve stimulated emission.
--[p112]-- They achieved a minimum size for an isolated feature of 9 nm, and were able to create adjacent lines of 20 nm width on a pitch of 52 nm.
One of the attractive features of the photoinduced deactivation approach is that the initiation of polymerization is completely decoupled from the inhibition of polymerization.
--[p114]-- Many of the light-driven processes observed in these materials have had multiple explanations proposed for them, and it is likely the case that the dominant events depend strongly on factors such as the viscosity, polarity, and temperature ofthe medium. Based upon the above observations and evidence from the literature [57], we have suggested that initiation by MGCB is driven by photoinduced electron ejection [44, 56].
--[p114]-- The use of CW light for deactivation allows considerably greater intensities to be employed than in the case of pulsed deactivation. Combining CW deactivation with the type of phase mask shown in Figure 5.2c, we were able to attain an axial resolution of 40 nm using 800-nm pulsed excitation and 800-nm, CW deactivation (Figure 5.8). We call this scheme resolution enhancement through photo-induced deactivation (RAPID) lithography.
--[p115]-- Any system that meets conditions (i) and (iii) is capable of fast writing speeds under most conditions, although it may be necessary to use phase masks that produce dark lines rather than dark central regions. A system that can also meet condition (ii) allows higher contrast to be attained. At the same time, practical constraints require that the initiation time to be not overly long, which would place a limit on the minimum delay time between write cycles in a given region.
--[p114]-- This observation indicates that there are at least two channels for polymerization and that only one of these channels can be deactivated with 800-nm light. We further found that the channel that cannot be deactivated grows in importance as the viscosity of the photoresist decreases.
The photochemistry and photophysics of triarylmethane dyes such as MGCB have been studied in great detail, and hundreds of papers have been published on the topic [57].
--[p112]-- Photoinhibition
Photoinhibitors are molecules that form weakly reactive radicals upon photoexcitation. These radicals tend to be more reactive with other radicals than they are with closed-shell species, and so can act to terminate radical chain reactions. Indeed, these materials were originally developed for photoquenching radical polymerization so that detailed kinetic measurements of the polymerization process could be performed [48, 49].
--[p112]-- The resolution enhancement declines substantially as a result of this efect. For instance, in creating 10 lines, Gu and coworkers found that the minimum pitch increased from 53 to 90 nm [53]. Additionally, although the photoinhibitor generates weakly reactive radicals, at high enough concentrations these radicals can still drive crosslinking reactions, which limits the maximum deactivation intensity that can be employed [50].
--[p112]-- McLeod and coworkers recognized that photoinhibitors could also provide a means of enhancing resolution in photopolymerization [50, 51]. They developed a methacrylic photoresist containing a photoinitiator that was excited at 473 nm and a photoinhibitor that was excited at 364 nm. Both the excitation and deactivation beams were CW, and the deactivation beam was phase-shaped as in STED microscopy.
--[p114]-- Our expectation based on the reported photophysics of MGCB was that the observed deactivation was the result of stimulated emission. To determine the lifetime of the state that is deactivated, we set the intensity of the deactivation beam such that deactivation was only partial at short delay times between excitation and deactivation. We then fabricated lines at diferent delay times, ranging up to the ∼13 ns repetition time of the Ti:sapphire lasers.

===== [p116-116] 5 STED-Inspired Approaches to Resolution Enhancement > Absorbance Modulation Lithography (4块) =====
--[p116]-- As with photoinhibitor schemes, AML decouples the excitation and inhibition steps. Indeed, the photoinitiators in AML are in an entirely diferent spatial region than the molecules that inhibit exposure. However, it is not clear so far whether such a scheme could be employed for 3D fabrication using multiphoton polymerization, because the overlayer efectively acts as a near-field mask.
--[p116]-- The photochromics used in AML can be switched between “open” and “closed” forms. The former absorbs light in the ultraviolet but is transparent to red light, whereas the latter absorbs red light but is relatively transparent in the ultraviolet. The key to AML is that the photoresist is exposed by ultraviolet light, but patterned red light is used to prevent the ultraviolet light from getting through the overlayer except at desired locations.
--[p116]-- Perhaps a photochromic scheme that could use multiphoton absorption at two diferent wavelengths for excitation and inhibition could be used in a bulk material. Even if such a scheme could be realized, current photochromics have relatively long switching times and can only be cycled a limited number of times. These issues must be addressed for AML to have a major impact in 3D fabrication.
--[p116]-- Absorbance Modulation Lithography
A discussion oftwo-color schemes for resolution enhancement would not be complete without considering absorbance modulation lithography (AML) [58, 59], even though this method has yet to be used with multiphoton polymerization or any other 3D fabrication scheme. In AML, a thin photochromic overlayer is deposited on top of a thin layer of photoresist. Photochromics are bistable, photoswitchable molecules [60].

===== [p321-321] Nanoreplication Printing and Nanosurface Processing > 13.4 (1块) =====
--[p321]-- 13.4

===== [p205-209] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Overview (29块) =====
--[p207]-- A major disadvantage is that the functional material is usually present only on the outside of the structure, and not throughout the bulk of the 3D form, which can compromise performance. An example might be an electronic function, where the deposition of a thin skin of conductive metal would have much lower conductivity that an idealized bulk-metal structure, or one that could potentially be created by a composite approach.
--[p207]-- This approach was demonstrated by depositing silicon onto a silicate inverted structure as a means for preparing 3D silicon PCs [22]. Overall, templated deposition also ofers the advantage that 3D patterning and targeted material deposition are decoupled, enabling both processes to be separately optimized, and it provides a means for creating bulk 3D structures from the second material.
--[p208]-- For example, if the MPL patterning and processing creates features with high surface roughness, then a second material deposited onto the polymeric template could have desirable properties, such as high adhesion, as well as undesirable properties, such as high optical scattering. So invariably, fabrication involving multistep processing requires careful co-optimization of the processing conditions to achieve good performance from the 3D microstructure.
--[p208]-- One early example in the field of MPL involves a hydrogel composite that was formulated to respond to UV radiation, such that the structure underwent reversible mechanical deformation when exposed [24]. A wide range of applications can be efected by active-response devices, and their advancement depends heavily on the progress in composite development and microstructure functionalization.
Over the past decade, many examples of the fabrication strategies described above have been reported.
--[p207]-- This might be viewed as an extreme limit of post-MPL functionalization, where the deposition is simply continued until a maximally thick layer is deposited. The back-filled medium is the structural inverse of the original template. This has been achieved by ALD of silica [22] and by galvanic deposition of metal around a structure fabricated on a conductive surface [23].
--[p207]-- The functional device could consist of the infiltrated template, or the final device could be obtained after using plasma etching or other approaches to remove the polymeric template, leaving the inverted structure in the second material. A second round of infiltration in another material can be used to reinvert the structure back to its original form in the final material.
--[p206]-- But it has the disadvantage that the additives must be present at low weight loading so as to not interfere with the multiphoton excitation and polymerization processes.
In post-MPL functionalization (Figure 9.1c), a conventional MPL medium is used to create the targeted 3D form, and post-fabrication chemistry or physical deposition processes are used to deposit a second material onto the 3D form.
--[p205]-- A good example is meta-structured materials, such as photonic crystals (PCs), for which the optical function is derived from the combination of sub-wavelengthscale structure as well as the bulk optical properties of the constituent material itself [1].
--[p205]-- Yet most applications require not only well-defined 3D forms but also functional properties that are manifest in controlled chemical, mechanical, optical, electronic, or thermal interactions with the environment. Indeed, for many micro-/nanodevices, the overall performance depends sensitively upon the synergistic combination of small-scale form and properties.
--[p208]-- It should also be noted that composite and surface functionalization can be used to create structures with either a passive or an active function. An actively functional device can be thought of as one that changes its form or physical properties in response to an environmental stimulus. These types of interactions often result from the combination of material, shape, or size of the device.
--[p207]-- For polymeric media, unreacted functional groups at the surface can be suficiently active so that surface functionalization can be carried out without the need to add additional components to the MPL medium [13, 20]. The post-MPL functionalization approach has the advantage that it decouples fabrication of the 3D structure from deposition of a targeted material, which eliminates potential complications that can arise in attempting to create a composite.
--[p205]-- Creating 3D micro-/nanoscale polymeric structures by MPL continues to serve many purposes, but a growing number of current applications require a wider range of materials to achieve targeted functions. In a limited number of situations, it has been possible to identify alternative materials that can be patterned in much the same way as a photopolymer.
--[p205]-- For example, thermally deposited arsenic sulfide [4] and other chalcogenides [5] can be directly photocrosslinked and then chemically etched, behaving like an inorganic analog of a photoresist that can be used to directly create functional 3D semiconductor nanostructures.
--[p208]-- Molecules of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { S }$ difused into the interstitial voids of the woodpile lattice as well as into the polymeric rods themselves, forming CdS nanocrystals throughout the structure. The resulting CdS-doped PC exhibited properties substantially diferent from those of a nonfunctionalized lattice.
Fabrication using composites, surface functionalization, or backfilling introduces a plethora of challenges.
--[p207]-- Conformal and nonconformal deposition approaches can be selected as needed for the targeted application. Deposition approaches such as sputtering and physical vapor deposition tend to be nonconformal, depositing material primarily on those surfaces of the 3D structure that are exposed by “line of sight” to the source [10, 11]. Conformal deposition, in which all surfaces of the 3D microstructure are coated uniformly, can be achieved using a variety of approaches.
--[p209]-- The remainder of this chapter reviews methods for fabricating and functionalizing composite structures in terms of the material classes, rather than processing method, as applications are typically dependent upon the class of material needed for a targeted function, and the means by which it is deposited are a secondary concern.
--[p207]-- The primary disadvantage remains, again, that multiple fabrication steps are needed to produce the final structure, including alternate substrate bonding/removal steps.
Some methods for fabricating 3D micro/nanostructures in novel materials have employed processes that are essentially a combination of both the composite and surface functionalization approaches.
--[p208]-- In addition to the general advantages and disadvantages listed above, the materials involved must be compatible with previous and subsequent steps, else the material function and 3D form can be compromised. Particular attention must be paid to chemical reactivity, solubility, swellability, and thermal tolerances associated with the materials and processing steps, as well as how these can disrupt the function or 3D form of the structure [20].
--[p205]-- But such materials are by far the exception, and in most cases it has been necessary to engineer new materials and processes for MPL that provide access to the wider range of properties and functions needed for a given application.
The imperative to create functional micro-/nanoscale structures has led many investigators to develop complementary approaches for multiphoton processing of composite materials and functionalization of 3D structures.
--[p205]-- Overview
Three-dimensional (3D) multiphoton lithography (MPL) is highly appealing as a fabrication and manufacturing technology because it provides a means for creating previously inaccessible structures that can have transformative applications in optics, sensing, imaging, consumer electronics, microelectromechanical systems (MEMS), and medicine.
--[p208]-- Adhesion and material compatibility are particularly important for interfaces created by surface functionalization or secondary deposition. Properties of the 3D form can also significantly afect those of the overall structure.
--[p206]-- In the first approach (Figure 9.1b), a composite material [6] is formulated that is both suitable for direct fabrication of the targeted microscale form by MPL and contains one or more additives that endow the final microstructure with the targeted functionality. Such composites are typically formulated so that a large fraction of the total mass is comprised of a polymerizable monomer and/or oligomer and a multiphoton-activatable initiator that are known to be suitable for MPL.
--[p209]-- Each of these has substantially extended the technological impact of MPL by opening new routes to functional 3D micro/nanoscale structures that can have tailored function for a given application. Several reviews of MPL and extensions of the method have been published over the past decade [25–40], providing a wealth of information concerning methods for fabricating functional micro/nanoscale devices using a range of materials.
--[p208]-- For example, Kawata and coworkers reported a route to high-refractive-index PCs based on a polymeric “woodpile” lattice loaded with CdS nanoparticles [21]. The approach involved first patterning the woodpile lattice by MPL using a composite of a photopolymer doped with $\mathrm { C d ^ { 2 + } }$ salts. After MPL, the woodpile structure was functionalized by exposing it to an atmosphere of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } S _ { }$ .
--[p206]-- The approaches that have been reported for integrating specific properties or function into 3D microstructures can be divided into three classes, as illustrated in Figure 9.1: (i) MPL using composites, (ii) post-MPL functionalization, and (iii) templated deposition or back-filling.
--[p207]-- Chemical-bath-based methods, such as electroless deposition [12, 13], can be used to deposit conductive metal conformally onto the 3D form. Other approaches, such as chemical vapor deposition (CVD) [14] and atomic layer deposition (ALD) [15] have also been used for conformal deposition. In some cases, the MPL medium may be modified slightly by incorporating an additive that makes the 3D structure active toward subsequent surface functionalization [16–19].
--[p207]-- Bulk functionalization can occur when a surface functionalization agent is able to difuse into the bulk of the crosslinked polymeric structure [20, 21].
With templated deposition, or backfilling (Figure 9.1d), MPL in a conventional photopolymer blend is used to create a free-standing 3D form upon which a second material is deposited to completely (or nearly completely) fill the void space between features of the 3D structure.
--[p206]-- A wide range of functional structures can be accessed by this approach. For example, optically functional microstructures can readily be created by blending a molecular chromophore into a polymeric MPL medium [7]. Composites containing photo-reducible metal salts and/or metal nanoparticles have been formulated and used to create electrically conductive structures by MPL [8, 9]. The composite approach has the advantage that the functional structure can be fabricated in a single MPL step.
--[p205]-- Microfluidics provide another good example, as these can be engineered to have peculiar and useful flow and mixing characteristics that derive from the combination of the channel size, shape, and cross-section with the chemical nature at the interface formed by the microfluidic walls and the fluid [2, 3].
The laser-induced change most commonly leveraged for MPL is photo-induced polymerization or cross-linking of an organic monomer or oligomer.

===== [p96-98] Light Sources and Systems for Multiphoton Lithography > Further Processing Considerations (18块) =====
--[p96]-- For example, linear stages in the line-by-line mode usually require appropriate acceleration tracks; galvanometer scanners need special attention in synchronization, and some piezo systems can perform learning cycles before processing to achieve better fidelity. The result of the data analysis and processing is a program that can be executed by the system controller.
--[p96]-- For instance, processing of micro lenses can be done with a standard rectangular grid hatching, but in many cases the surface quality of the resulting structure will be disappointing, as it still exhibits traces of the rectangular grid. A strategy based on a concentric writing algorithm can be much more successful in this case. As soon as freeform surfaces like aspheric lenses are targeted, it is worthwhile considering which processing approach is the most promising.
--[p98]-- Setups with an SLM require a very dedicated and extended optical system, as shown in Figure 4.4. For example, LC-based SLMs very much depend on the correct polarization and require a well-polarized light beam. The design and calculation of computer-generated holograms include assumptions on beam shape and wavefront properties, which require special attention. Finally, it is useful to be able to verify the exposure image on a reference camera to identify unexpected hologram design deviations.
--[p96]-- In such a situation, it is essential to have full control of the hardware and to program the processing strategy in detail oneself, for example, in CNC code.
DMDs, SLMs, and related devices require images that are shown on their display. In the most basic case, these images are slices of the 3D model as described earlier, which are subsequently displayed on a DMD. With SLM, usually phase modulation with holographic images is used to minimize the loss of laser power.
--[p97]-- An advanced setup typically includes a piezoelectric stage or a precision motorized XYZ stage operating considerably in the sub-micrometer range, a commercial ultrashort laser with < 250 fs pulse duration, megahertz repetition rate and a pulse energy not less than 2 nJ, a high-quality high NA 100× objective, polarizing optics for power control, and a fast shutter.
--[p97]-- Instead of aligning each substrate at the corresponding accuracy, this is often compensated by measuring three positions on the sample surface that are far enough apart from each other. Subsequently, the nanostructures are built onto a virtual plane that is defined by these positions.
Diferent technological approaches for two-photon polymerization described earlier result in a wide range of optical setups (Figure 4.6).
--[p97]-- In addition, an electromechanical shutter, a standard microscope objective, a camera module, and some optomechanical components for beam steering, laser power control, and sample illumination are required. This low-budget system will be of relatively low performance and require some development eforts and attention of the experimenter; however, it would work, and for certain tasks like material studies it can be suficient.
--[p96]-- Further Processing Considerations
The production of a micro and nanostructure starts with a 3D sketch or model of the structure to be manufactured. This data has to be analyzed, converted, and prepared for the processing unit of the MPP setup. For stages and SLM, totally diferent approaches are required.
Linear stages, piezo systems, and galvanometer scanners usually require that the model is converted into movement trajectories and the corresponding movement commands.
--[p97]-- A powerful 2PP system is based on a dedicated nano-positioning system with high dynamics and/or a laser scanner, an ultrashort laser with megahertz repetition rate, a high-quality, high-NA optical microscope objective, a fast optical shutter like an acoustooptic modulator, automated laser power control, and dedicated optics for beam shaping and sample illumination, and tools for identifying the resin interface [25].
--[p98]-- In conclusion, a wide spectrum of ultrafast light sources and precise positioning systems is available in the literature and used for MPP. Low-budget laboratory systems can be self-built from standard laboratory equipment, but this requires much efort to characterize, develop, and optimize the performance of optomechanical components together with material, process, and software.
--[p98]-- Commercially available systems are much more than the sum of their optical and mechanical components: These systems would have been optimized with respect to their performance, and they are based on much experience in lasers, optical design, performance, and control of nanopositioning stages and laser scanners and laser–matter interaction.
--[p96]-- Depending on the controller manufacturer, it might be in a standard programming language like computerized numerical control (CNC) code or in a device-specific, proprietary programming language.
Quite often, users of 2PP systems like to have very detailed control of the processing strategy to ensure essential properties of the fabricated structure.
--[p97]-- Diferent algorithms have been proposed and are used to calculate holographic images, ofwhich the most popular is the Gerchberg–Saxton algorithm [24], which is used to iteratively create holograms for each image slice. However, similar to dedicated user programming of stages, calculation of holograms can be based on an analytic mathematical approach instead of an iterative numerical approach, particularly when single-shot 3D polymerization is intended.
--[p98]-- It is worthwhile considering whether the system should be extendable, as the progress in the field of MPP is very fast and the state of the art changes every year with new technologies coming up.
--[p96]-- In most instances, the user starts with a 3D model from a computeraided design (CAD) software that is exported to an STL file. The STL file format has become a standard in additive manufacturing, as it is an open standard, clear, and easy to implement. It is not only widely used in MPP but also in stereolithography and for 3D printers as well. In an STL file, only surfaces of an object are described, and this is done by segmenting the surface into many small triangles.
--[p97]-- Regardless of the positioning system or beam deflection system, an important aspect of an MPP setup is the precise alignment of the incident laser beam with respect to the sample substrate surface. For example, for a nanostructure of 100 μm width and an intended axial size voxel of 1 μm, an angular error of only 0.05<sup>∘</sup> already corresponds to about 100 nm, which is 10% of the voxel size.
--[p97]-- A very simple setup that allows dot-by-dot exposure can be built with a standard motorized XYZ positioning system. Most femtosecond or even picosecond lasers that might be already available in a laboratory can be used as a light source if they provide nanojoule pulse energy and good beam quality.
--[p96]-- To generate movement trajectories from STL files, algorithms are used that divide the model into slices and identify the edges of the model in each slice. To produce not only a surface but a solid 3D object, the algorithm has to identify the inside part of the object and add movement trajectories that fill the inner volume of the object, a method that is often referred to as hatching.
In addition, the algorithm has to consider requirements that are specific for the positioning system.

===== [p309-312] Microfluidic Devices Produced by Two-Photon-Induced Polymerization > Microfluidic Devices Driven by a Focused Laser Beam (12块) =====
--[p312]-- The pumping performance of the viscous micropump with the twin spiral microrotor depends on several parameters including the width of the U-shaped microchannel and the diameter and position of the microrotor, like a viscous micropump using a single rotating disk. We examined the pressure field and streamlines of the micropump by changing the width of the microchannel via simulation using COMSOL Multiphysics software.
--[p309]-- Microfluidic Devices Driven by a Focused Laser Beam
In the microscale, viscous drag force is dominant compared to inertia force due to the scale efect. Using this unique property, we have proposed and developed a viscous micropump using a single-disk microrotor, as shown in Figure 12.17 [33]. In this case, we designed a U-shaped microchannel to generate laminar flow around the single-disk microrotor.
--[p310]-- When a laser beam is focused on the center of the twin spiral microrotor, the optical torque induced by the radiation pressure and exerted on the spiral blades is in the same direction, because the spiral microrotors are mounted in opposite directions. Therefore, the net optical torque generated by the twin spiral microrotor can exceed that of a single spiral microrotor.
Figure 12.19 shows the SEM images of the twin spiral microrotor with an outer cylinder.
--[p311]-- To drive the twin spiral microrotor, we also used the optical system of twophoton microfabrication, while operating the Ti:sapphire laser in the continuous wave (CW) mode. The surrounding liquid was glycol ether ester, which we used for washing out the liquid photopolymer. The rotation speed of the twin spiral microrotor was proportional to the intensity of the induced laser beam. The maximum speed reached 300 rpm at a laser power of 500 mW.
--[p312]-- Figure 12.20 shows that the viscous micropump with a twin spiral microrotor can be used for pumping a fluid and transporting microobjects. The sequence of images was taken at 2-s intervals, showing a tracer particle being pumped through the channel when the microrotor was rotating at a speed of 300 rpm.
--[p309]-- When the twin spiral microrotor is optically trapped by focusing a laser beam at the corner of the U-shaped microchannel, it is stably rotated at a high speed of several hundred micrometers per minute while the microrotor is trapped in three dimensions at the focus. As a result, the microrotor rotation provides a continuous, unidirectional flow without pulsation like the micropump using a rotating single disk.
--[p310]-- We employ the phenomenon of the reversed rotation of the single spiral microrotor to create a novel microrotor that has higher rotational speed than a single spiral microrotor. As shown in Figure 12.18, in our twin spiral microrotor, two spiral blades with reversed directions are connected along the optical axis inside an outer cylinder.
--[p310]-- The optically induced rotation ofa microrotor with a single spiral blade was first reported by Galajda and Ormos [35]. In their experiments, they demonstrated that the rotation direction of the spiral microrotor is reversed when the trapped position of the microrotor is changed from below the focus to beyond the focus along the optical axis.
--[p309]-- Although we have successfully demonstrated that a microparticle was transported along the U-shaped channel, the rotational speed of the disk is low. This is because the disk is rotated by time-shared scanning of a single laser beam. To overcome the limitation of the rotational speed, a spinning microrotor with twin spiral blades was utilized as a microrotor for the viscous micropump.
--[p309]-- Figure 12.18 shows an optically driven viscous micropump using a spinning microrotor with twin spiral blades [34]. The outer cylinder attached to the twin spiral microrotor plays an important role in that it generates a continuous unidirectional flow without flow separation. The twin spiral microrotor is confined to a U-shaped microchannel to generate a laminar flow like the previous viscous micropump using a single-disk microrotor.
--[p312]-- As a result, a steady laminar flow was generated in the linear regions of the U-shaped microchannels by selecting proper parameters. In this simulation, the flow rate was saturated at a microchannel width of 10 μm with a 4-μm-diameter microrotor.
A prototype of the viscous micropump containing a twin spiral microrotor (diameter: 4 μm) was fabricated by two-photon microfabrication.
--[p311]-- The diameter and height of the microrotor were 4 and 9 μm, respectively. The opposite facing spiral blades were fabricated by two-photon microfabrication. When extracting the microrotor from unsolidified photopolymer, we used a supercritical $\mathrm { C O } _ { 2 }$ drying process to reduce harmful deformation caused by the surface tension of the rinse solution [12].

===== [p118-118] 5 STED-Inspired Approaches to Resolution Enhancement > Conclusions (2块) =====
--[p118]-- With further advances, two-color techniques may lead to tabletop tools that can be used to perform 3D lithography with a resolution that rivals that of electron-beam lithography.
--[p118]-- Conclusions
Two-color approaches have already enabled substantial resolution enhancement for two-photon lithography, and hold great promise for eventually pushing the resolution down to the 10–20 nm level. Success in this endeavor will require the pursuit of new materials and approaches that can address the challenges discussed above.

--[p28]-- 2.3.1.2 Z-Scan
Introduced in 1989 [25], the originalZ-scan consists of translating a thin sample through the focus of a laser beam while monitoring the light transmitted through an aperture [26]. -is gives a measure ofthe NLR ifthere is no NLA present. Gaussian spatial beams are preferred since modeling the results is greatly simpli“ed, but not necessary. For measuring ultrafast nonlinearities, pulsed sources are required, and the signal is proportional to the temporally integrated nonlinearity.
--[p29]-- The open-aperture Z-scan is sensitive only to NLA as long as the sample is thin as previously defined, and the curve can be fittted with a parameter describing the NLA (assuming there is a single NLA process!). The closed-aperture Z-scan is afected by both NLR and NLA and, given that the NLA has previously been fitted, can be fitted with a single parameter for the NLR (again under the assumption that there is a single NLR process!).

--[p34]-- If the Z-scans are identical for the same irradiance but with diferent pulse widths, the response time of the nonlinearity is shorter than the temporal width of the pulses used. For very short pulses (e.g., femtosecond), this would indicate that the mechanism is 2PA. If the open-aperture $Z .$ scans are identical for the same fluence, then they may be due to ESA.
--[p34]-- Similarly, if the closedaperture Z-scans are the same for the same fluence rather than irradiance, then the NLR may be due to the redistribution of the populations.

Thus, the irradiance changes by a factor of 2 while the signals for both NLA and NLR are nearly identical.

===== [p192-193] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > Biologic Questions (7块) =====
--[p193]-- Excitingly, the ability to manipulate 3D hydrogel biomaterials with MPL (see Section 8.6) uncovers a unique ability to address questions related to how dynamic changes in the ECM influence cell function and tissue assembly. New directions in the field of 4D biology (3D scafolds that are dynamic in time) rely on userprogrammable and externally tunable chemistries to introduce or remove specific cues from the culture microenvironment in real time [15].
--[p192]-- Biologic Questions
As advances in MPL fabrication of biocompatible materials are achieved, the class of biomaterials presents unique opportunities to address several questions in biology and regenerative medicine. Both fields are beginning to appreciate the utility of 3D scafolds for cell culture, as opposed to 2D tissue-culture polystyrene (TCPS), that can better recapitulate critical aspects of the native ECM [198].
--[p193]-- Combining these dynamic and user-tunable chemistries with MPL allows the user to control the biophysical and biochemical nature of the cellular environment on the micrometer scale. Similar approaches allow biologists to study how changes in material stifness influence cell proliferation and diferentiation [137] or to investigate how adhesive ligand presentation or removal influences tissue development and function on the cellular level [133].
--[p192]-- Several reticulated materials are already being employed for 3D cell culture [172], and, uniquely, MPL allows rapid fabrication of geometrically defined scafolds as tissue replacements for regenerative medicine. For example, patient-specific devices can be fabricated utilizing medical imaging to assess the geometric need, which can be translated into a digitally rastered structure and printed in a material of interest using MPL.
--[p193]-- Materials scientists and MPL experts must interface and interact with cell and developmental biologists to employ these burgeoning techniques to address critical questions in these fields.
--[p193]-- Additionally, selective photochemistries now allow patterning and removal of bioactive ligands or full proteins to investigate how subcellular localization of specific biochemical moieties may direct intracellular signaling. Moreover, the ability to control these reactions in space and time opens the door to the creation ofanisotropic materials and synthetic tissues to study heterogeneous tissue formation and function.
--[p192]-- Three-dimensional systems facilitate improved tissue morphogenesis and more physiologic culture environments for many cell types of the body [171]. MPL-fabricated materials complement this need for 3D scafolds, as they can be patterned with micrometer-scale resolution (sub-cellular length scales) that enable researchers to answer questions related to how 3D geometry, topography, and structure afect cell function and tissue organization [199–201].

===== [p307-308] Microfluidic Devices Produced by Two-Photon-Induced Polymerization > Microfluidic Devices Driven by a Scanning Laser Beam (11块) =====
--[p308]-- When the rotation direction was reversed, the tracer particle was pushed to the outlet. Figure 12.16 shows the dependence of the velocity of the tracer particle on the rotational speed of the rotors. It is clear that the velocity of the tracer particle is proportional to the rotational speed of the rotors. The flow rate was estimated at less than $1 \mathrm { p l } \mathrm { m i n } ^ { - 1 }$ .
--[p307]-- As a promising method of addressing the above issues, we have developed an optically driven micropump by using two-photon microstereolithography [32]. The micropump consist of two lobed rotors that are incorporated into a microchannel. The lobed rotors are individually confined to a microchannel by their own shaft, thus preventing the rotors from moving unless continuously irradiated with light.
--[p307]-- Microfluidic Devices Driven by a Scanning Laser Beam
In the development of functional lab-on-a-chip devices, built-in micropumps based on pressure-driven flow are necessary for the further extension of application fields.
--[p308]-- A tracer particle was also fabricated in the microchannel using two-photon microstereolithography. The tracer particle was optically trapped to prevent it from moving around while unsolidified photocurable resin was being washed out. After the washing process, the tracer particle was released, after which the rotors were interlocked using the time-divided laser scanning technique. As a result, the tracer particle was successfully brought into the microchamber by the pumped flow.
--[p307]-- This allows the micropump to be easily integrated into a mass-produced biochips made from plastics and glass.
Figure 12.13a shows a schematic diagram of the optically driven lobed micropump. This micropump is driven by means of radiation pressure generated by focusing a laser beam.
--[p307]-- Although various kinds of pressure-driven micropumps have been developed using a range of micromachining techniques [30, 31], most of the previously developed micropumps are diaphragm types, in which piezoelectric actuators or pneumatic actuation are utilized to deform an elastic membrane that drives fluid transport. To obtain adequate displacement of the membrane, the diameter of the membrane must range from 100 to 500 μm.
--[p307]-- When the laser beam is focused on the side of the rotor, the net radiation pressure applied to the rotor points toward the focus, allowing the rotor to be controlled by scanning the laser beam along two circular trajectories alternately. Figure 12.13b shows the time-divided laser scanning method to control the two rotors simultaneously. The black points shown in Figure 12.13b are trapping points for each status.
--[p308]-- Ultralow flow rates such as this give our mechanism the potential for use in micro/nanofluidic devices.
--[p307]-- The laser beam is divided and scanned along two circular trajectories in opposite directions. In this case, on–of control of the laser beam is not performed when the laser beam alternates between the two trajectories. While the rotors are tightly engaged and rotated, fluid is trapped in the spaces between the rotors and the cage, and carried around in them as shown in Figure 12.13b.
Figure 12.14 shows an SEM image of a prototype of the lobed micropump.
--[p307]-- As a result, the overall dimensions of the micropumps are much larger than the microchannels. This prevents the further miniaturization and integration of microfluidic components. In addition, the use of built-in high-precision microactuators makes biochips expensive. For these reasons, most current disposable microfluidic chips for chemical synthesis and cell analysis still utilize external pumps such as syringe types rather than built-in micropumps.
--[p308]-- The length of the major axis of each rotor is $9 \mu \mathrm { m }$ . The thickness of the rotor is 2.5 μm. The lobed rotors were incorporated into a microchannel whose inlet and outlet are in 5 μm wide and 7 μm high. The microchannel was also fabricated by using two-photon microstereolithography.
We demonstrated that fluid was transported with the optically driven micropump. Figure 12.15a,b shows sequential images of the micropump being driven.

===== [p117-118] 5 STED-Inspired Approaches to Resolution Enhancement > Challenges for Two-Color, Two-Photon Lithography (10块) =====
--[p118]-- Controllable phase masks that allow the voxel dimensions to be changed on the fly may also assist in the most rapid possible fabrication of 3D structures with features on a range of diferent size scales.
--[p117]-- Challenges for Two-Color, Two-Photon Lithography
Two-color approaches hold great promise for enabling vast improvements in the resolution of multiphoton lithography. At the same time, there are many hurdles to be overcome before this promise can be fully realized.
--[p118]-- Addressing this issue may require applying two-color approaches to other materials, such as hybrid organic/inorganics that have been used in two-photon polymerization [64].
Finally, as voxels become ever smaller, throughput becomes a major concern. Smaller voxels mean an increased number of scans to perform patterning over the same volume. It is therefore essential to develop highly sensitive materials that allow high writing speeds.
--[p117]-- Figure 5.9 considers a two-dimensional case, and this problem is substantially worse when fabrication is performed in three dimensions. This problem does not arise in STED microscopy, in the sense that fluorescence regenerates ground-state molecules. In contrast, in lithography, once an initiation event has occurred, the
--[p117]-- Every approach to twocolor resolution enhancement would benefit from the development of optimized materials, and this development must go hand in hand with a deeper understanding ofthe photochemistry and photophysics that underlie the methods. Until such knowledge is available, it will be dificult to determine which approach is best for a given application.
--[p118]-- Another important challenge is the creation ofmaterials that can be deactivated at the shortest possible wavelength, which will allow the dark region in the focus of the deactivation beam to be as small as possible. However, this advantage must be balanced against the fact that the deactivation PSF should envelop the entire excitation PSF to minimize background polymerization.
--[p117]-- of exposed photoinitiators that cause chemistry increases greatly with the fabrication of subsequent lines, raising the overall exposure in a given maximum well above the threshold for a single pass, thereby decreasing the resolution and dynamic range.
photoinitiator is used up. Undesired background polymerization is probably the greatest challenge to improving the resolution of two-color materials.
--[p118]-- As voxels and structures created with two-photon polymerization become smaller, there is a nonlinear change in their mechanical properties [61–63]. Even though two-color approaches allow for a greater degree of crosslinking in a voxel of a given size, mechanical integrity may end up to be a major issue in pushing the resolution of 3D structures to its limit.
--[p117]-- All two-color approaches that have been developed to date sufer from the common shortcoming that the state that leads to initiation is the same state that must be deactivated. Thus, there is in inherent competition between initiation and deactivation, and this competition requires that deactivation occur on as fast a timescale as possible. Ultimately, not every molecule that is excited in a region in which crosslinking is desired can be deactivated before initiation occurs.
--[p117]-- Because a key goal of two-color approaches is to improve resolution, it is desirable to be able to fabricate structures on as tight a pitch as possible. However, every fabrication pass that is within the focal volume of the excitation beam contributes to a buildup of photoinitiation in areas in which it is not desired. As shown in Figure 5.9, this phenomenon can lead to a strong polymerization background, even when the degree of excitation in undesired regions is quite small.
