===== [p177-178] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > 8.4.3.1 Macromer Chemistries (10块) =====
--[p177]-- Decades of research on the use of hydrogels as biomaterials have provided a wealth of information on backbone chemistries for this application, all of which equally applies to MPL hydrogels [64]. In general, hydrophilic polymers can be segregated into two main classes: synthetic polymers and natural polymers. Synthetic polymers for hydrogel synthesis include poly(ethylene glycol) (PEG), poly(vinyl alcohol) (PVA), poly(acrylic acid) (PAA), and poly(2-hydroxyethylmethacrylate) (PHEMA) [65].
--[p178]-- In each material class – resins, networks, or hydrogels – the backbone chemistry often defines the bulk chemical properties of the material, which influence protein fouling, biocompatibility, cell adhesion, degradation, and mechanical integrity [12]. The linking chemistry defines the kinetics of formation, the microarchitecture, and crosslinking density [49].
--[p177]-- This section serves to introduce a subset of the available toolboxes even though the specific chemistries have not yet been explicitly utilized with MPL. A brief overview of MPL biomaterials is presented in Section 8.5.
Typical non-hydrogel photopolymerized biomaterials employ a polymeric resin-like precursor solution that is composed of functional monomers, oligomers, or macromers and a suitable photoinitiator that can be polymerized rapidly into a solid.
--[p177]-- Such hydrophobic backbone chemistries include esters [59], carbamates [60], and carbonates [61] that are end-functionalized with polymerizable moieties such as vinyl groups or complementary thiol and ene functionalities [62]. Included in this class of materials are the biophotopolymers that have been used to fabricate microstructured biomaterials using MPL [63].
For the fabrication of hydrogels, the main constraint is that the backbone chemistry be hydrophilic.
--[p177]-- Common resins that have been applied to fabricate photopolymerized biomaterials include (meth)acrylates, epoxides, urethanes, styrenes, n-vinylpyrrolidone, and other vinylic small molecules [57]. In these systems, polymerization induces the formation of high molecular weight, insoluble, and entangled polymers that comprise a solid material. In parallel, functional oligomers or macromers are also used to make biomaterial based on nonhydrophilic backbone polymers.
--[p177]-- Of these, PEG or poly(ethylene oxide) (PEO) have found prolific use on account oftheir non-fouling behavior and ability to mimic the elasticity and transport of native tissue. Natural polymers for hydrogel fabrication include alginate, collagen/gelatin, hyaluronic acid, fibrin, chondroitin sulfate, and cellulose derivatives [66].
--[p177]-- In either case, synthetic or natural, the polymers can be functionalized with a variety of polymerizable moieties (such as (meth)acrylates, (meth)acrylamides, or thiol–ene pairs) to render the backbone polymers amenable to photopolymerization. Once crosslinked via photopolymerization, the insoluble polymer network imbibes water and swells, resulting in a functional hydrogel [66].
--[p178]-- Given the large toolbox, the orthogonal selection of backbone chemistry and linking chemistry spans a large parameter space of material properties. Additional information on the specific linking chemistries amenable to photopolymerization is provided in the following section.
--[p177]-- 8.4.3.1 Macromer Chemistries
The photopolymerization precursor solution also requires the selection of appropriate macromer chemistries for material fabrication. The macromer selection can be approached in two stages: (i) deciding on a backbone chemistry, and (ii) choosing a linking chemistry for network formation.
--[p177]-- Fortunately, single-photon photopolymerization of biomaterials and hydrogels has been employed extensively over the last few decades and has identified a broad range of backbone and linking chemistries for the formation of biocompatible polymer networks and hydrogels. In principle, the same toolbox can be adapted to MPL fabrication given that the photoinitiator and light source chosen provide a suficient source of radicals to drive polymerization.

===== [p155-157] Hybrid Materials for Multiphoton Polymerization > Silicate Hybrid Materials (8块) =====
--[p155]-- In particular, it has been widely utilized for waveguide-coupled woodpile structures to investigate superprism phenomena [33, 34] and for the fabrication ofdifractive optical elements [35] and optically active microdisks (Figure 7.3). It has also been used to fabricate woodpile structures and infiltrate them with quantum dots [37]. In addition, it has been shown that the maximum resolution ofthe laser-fabricated structures can be increased by post-treating ORMOCER structures with heat [38].
--[p155]-- Silicate Hybrid Materials
Silicate-based photopolymers have proven to be a very popular choice in microfabrication using MPP [14, 17–19] mainly due to the high reactivity they exhibit. They combine the properties of silicate glasses such as hardness, chemical and thermal stability, and optical transparency with the low-temperature laser processing of organic polymers – properties impossible to achieve with just inorganic or pure polymeric materials.
--[p155]-- More recently, a low-threshold organic-semiconductor distributed-feedback laser was fabricated based on a surface grating structured in an ORMOCER material using MPP [39].
The commercially available ORMOCER is a viscous liquid that does not require any pretreatment before structuring, which makes its handling straightforward and easy. However, it also has certain drawbacks.
--[p157]-- MPP allows not only the miniaturization of such devices but also the adoption of more than one needle design in a single array, thereby optimizing the efect of geometry on mechanical and puncturing ability.
--[p155]-- The ORMO-CER widely used in two-photon polymerization (2PP) was first developed by Ruth Houbertz [14, 15, 30, 31] and was used to fabricate photonic crystal woodpile structures, like the one shown in Figure 7.2 [32], proving its successful structuring ability.
The good mechanical, optical, and processing properties that ORMOCER possesses, combined with the fact that it is commercially available, make it a very attractive option for the fabrication of photonic crystal structures and devices.
--[p155]-- The most widely used hybrid material is the commercially available photopolymer ORMOCER. This material comprises an inorganic (–Si–O–Si–) backbone which can be functionalized with a range of organic functionalities. ORMOCERs are often used as dental composites [20–23], as electrolytes for lithium batteries [24, 25], as membrane materials for fuel cell applications [26, 27], and in optical interconnect technology [28, 29].
--[p157]-- The biocompatibility and the chemical inertness that ORMOCER exhibits over and above its already mentioned properties have widened the range of ORMO-CER 3D microstructural applications to biomedical microdevices and medical implants [41–48]. For instance, Figure 7.4 shows a micro-needle array that can be used in drug delivery devices, enabling the transdermal delivery of diferent pharmacological substances.
--[p156]-- As the liquid transforms into a solid during photopolymerization, shrinkage occurs [40], which results in the distortion of the photopolymerized structure and deviation from the original design. This drawback of laser-induced fabrication can be corrected using a compensation factor [41].

===== [p270-274] Fabrication of Waveguides and Other Optical Elements by Multiphoton Lithography > Materials Based on Multiphoton Photochromism (14块) =====
--[p272]-- Bisazobenzene systems, in contrast to other azo-containing polymers, have some advantages including better nonlinear optical and photoresponsive properties and enhanced birefringence as well as thermal stability. Forming a copolymer and thus attaching the dye covalently to the polymer matrix is an efective measure against dye aggregation, which is generally problematic in guest–host systems.
Azo isomerism is not only useful a in case of 3D data storage.
--[p272]-- Importantly, however, the driver is able to switch the dye’s fluorescence intensity, which depends on whether the driver is in the “read” or “write” state. The reading wavelength of 660 nm is suficiently diferent from the erasing wavelength of 400 nm, so unwanted erasing does not occur during the reading mode of a typical device.
Another creative approach for optical data storage was developed by Belfield et al. [98].
--[p272]-- The molecule consists ofa driver part and a dye part, in which the driver part is a fulgimide, the ring moiety (write form) of which can be opened with light of 530 nm to the read form. The reaction is reversible, with light of 400 nm inducing the backward reaction (erasing). Regardless of the state of the driver, the benzophenoxazine dye’s absorption wavelength remains unchanged at 660 nm.
--[p272]-- Ambrosio and coworkers presented a method for MPA patterning based on photo-induced mass migration [108]. Using a polyurethane, which was obtained via condensation reaction with an azo-containing, Y-shaped chromophore, they were able to create structures including a difraction gratings. Rentzepis et al. solved the problem of nondestructive read out of 3D optical stored data in an elegant way [99].
--[p270]-- Prior to two-photon illumination, the chromophores are introduced into a carrier polymer – usually PMMA – via uniform doping in organic solvent, copolymerization, or grafting reactions. Films were created from polymer solutions via drop coating, spin coating, or drop casting.
In an interesting contribution, Zhang et al. developed an optical polarization storage technology based on two-photon isomerization (TPI) of a bisazobenzenecontaining MMA copolymer [101].
--[p271]-- Under MPA conditions, azogroups undergo a cis–trans isomerization, which causes anisotropy and birefringence within the material. This allows the encoding of two diferent patterns in the same layer and region, which can be read out separately by changing the polarization of the readout beam by $4 5 ^ { \circ }$ (Figure 10.19).
--[p270]-- Materials Based on Multiphoton Photochromism
Multiphoton absorption (MPA) can induce changes in the optical properties of a material by numerous mechanisms. Change in fluorescence, absorption, or refractive index can be induced not just by polymerization but also via chemical events such as photochromic transformations (isomerism, dimerizations, cyclizations, etc.) [97–101], photobleaching [102–104], or protonation by means of a PAG [105, 106].
--[p273]-- To realize multiphoton fabrication, 4-(diphenylamino)phenylbenzoate was attached to polymer networks such as polystyrene and PDMS. The polymer underwent two-photon-induced decarboxylation with a yield of 40%.
Sun et al. recently demonstrated two-photon structuring via cis–trans isomerization within a viscous fluorescent polymer DPO-PPV (poly(p-phenylene vinylene)) [107].
--[p270]-- This allows a better response of functional nanodevices to external stimuli [107]. Furthermore, elaborate processing procedures including post-baking or development steps are avoided. Some of the more commonly studied photochromic moieties include fulgides, spiropyranes, diarylethenes, polycylic aromatic compounds, and azobased chromophores [99]. Generally, the two-photon absorbing molecules are embedded in transparent polymer matrices.
--[p270]-- These single-molecule transformations are not catalytic processes such as the case with MPL, where one photoinitiation event causes a chain reaction. Reactions are thus more volumetrically confined, making photochromism highly appropriate for 3D optical data storage. Multiphoton photochromism has another benefit in contrast to the more established technique of MPL: higher concentrations of the active ingredients within resins are possible.
--[p274]-- In contrast to the cis conformation, the trans isomer is not or only weakly soluble in organic solvent, which made selective removal of unreacted polymer after TPI possible (Figure 10.23). In this manner, a PhC and a Fresnel zone plate were written with a femtosecond laser. Moreover, the structured areas could be loaded by nearly 100% of fluorescent material.
10.7
--[p272]-- Most previously proposed photochromic molecules had the same absorption bands for the read-out and erasing states, meaning that some information would be erased during each read-out cycle. To solve this problem, “read,” “write,” and “erase” operations were each assigned their own separate wavelength. The idea of this invention is shown in Figure 10.20.
--[p273]-- In 2007, Kern and coworkers showed that at the laser wavelength of 610 nm, refractive index change can be accomplished via two-photoninduced photo-Fries rearrangement and decarboxylation (Figure 10.21) [109].
The latter photoreaction can also be triggered by MPA, which has been exploited by the same group to write waveguide structures that could potentially serve as optical interconnects in flexible printed circuit boards (Figure 10.22) [110].
--[p273]-- In this system, a fluorinated diarylethene derivative was employed, which reversibly ring-closed under UV exposure and opened with visible light. The photo-reversible molecule served exclusively in its closed conformation as an energy-transfer quencher for a the fluorescent dye 2,7-bis[4-(9,9-didecylfluoren-2-yl)vinyl]phenylbenzothiazole. Based on this principle, the fluorescence of the dye could be modulated. This system allowed a 10 000-fold readout, with only a small extent oferasing.

===== [p24-25] 2 Characterization of 2PA Chromophores > 2.1 (8块) =====
--[p25]-- light simply missing the detector and incorrect identification of the physical processes, have led to reported values ofnonlinear coeficients that vary over orders of magnitude from one publication to the next [4–7].

--[p24]-- Additionally, NLR accompanies NLA and transforms into a spatial redistribution of energy upon propagation. -us, in any experiment to monitor transmittance changes, considerable caution needs to be taken to ensure that the entire beam is collected by the detector for all input irradiances. Otherwise, the NLR may be easily mistaken for NLA.
--[p24]-- For example, if NLA is irradiance-dependent (as for ultrafast nonlinearities), errors come from energy calibration (e.g.,±5%), temporal measurements (e.g.,±10%), and spatial energy distribution measurements (e.g.,±10% for Gaussian beams but for two dimensions). -ese errors add along with “tting errors (e.g.,±10%) to give an overall con“dence error for the third-order nonlinear response.
--[p24]-- 2.1
Introduction
In this chapter, we describe several techniques for measuring nonlinear absorption (NLA) with special emphasis on the instantaneous nonlinear response of twophoton absorption (2PA) [1] due to its extensive use in microscopic imaging and 3D stereolithography/additive manufacturing [2]. We also give some information on nonlinear refraction (NLR) measurements since NLR almost always accompanies NLA signals.
--[p25]-- Although such large discrepancies are becoming rarer due in part to more reliable, stable, and well-characterized optical sources and to our more complete understanding of the nonlinearities themselves along with the potential measurement problems, obtaining reliable coeficients with small confidence errors is still challenging.
--[p24]-- Also, in order to be able to separate NLA and NLR, the conditions for external self-action, that is, the thin-sample approximation, need to be satis“ed, placing stringent requirements on the experimental parameters [3]. -is will be discussed in more detail later. Even when NLA and NLR can be separated, obtaining reliable values for nonlinear coecients can be dicult.
--[p24]-- In general, multiple experimental techniques are needed to fully unravel the various physical processes involved in the NLA for a given material, as it is rare to “nd only a single dominant nonlinear response. For example, 2PA not only causes a change in transmission but also results in the generation of excited states. -ese excited states can also change the absorption spectrum of the material, which can easily be confused with the 2PA itself.

===== [p263-267] Fabrication of Waveguides and Other Optical Elements by Multiphoton Lithography > Sol–Gel-Derived Resins (26块) =====
--[p267]-- Thus condensation is accelerated compared to other sol–gel-derived resins, which is another advantage of this technology. Films for MPL are formed via drop casting or spin coating. 1-Propanol is a suitable solvent to remove the unreacted resin after MPL structuring. The described Zr-based resin has been applied to fabricate PhC with a clear bandgap in the NIR region (Figure 10.16) [76–78]. More recently, Emons et al.
used a 4 : 1 MEMO/ZPO resin with a dipentaerythritol hexa acrylate crosslinker to create PhCs with feature widths down to 45 nm [79].
--[p264]-- As photo-curable ORMOCER resins do not require any solvents, shrinkage is comparatively moderate. High thermal stability up to $2 7 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ is necessary for mass fabrication processes of, for example, printed circuit boards, which are laminated at a similar temperature.
--[p265]-- The refractive index of the resin will change upon polymerization, which has to be taken into account in the optical setup. Online monitoring of the fabrication process is thus simplified, which is an advantage over cationic resins such as SU-8, where polymerization occurs only during the post-baking step.
--[p265]-- Although the post-processing of ORMOCERs is only incidentally mentioned in most MPL-related literature, post thermal treatment can provide significant improvement in the spatial resolution of the fabricated structures, as demonstrated by Gu and coworkers [71]. During the post thermal treatment step, further polymerization of methacrylate groups and partial decomposition of organic parts were detected.
--[p267]-- Synthesis of these Zr resins is carried out by chelating zirconium n-propoxide (ZPO) with methyl methacrylic acid and adding a hydrolyzed product of MEMO [77]. Afterward, water is added and an interconnected porous cluster structure is obtained. 4,4-Bis(diethylamino)benzophenone is used as the initiator. The gelation step involves the removal of solvents and is typically carried out at $1 0 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ , where the formation of Si–O–Zr linkages is kinetically favored.
--[p266]-- Addressing the problem of not being able to create a full PBG with existent resins (ORMOCER, SU-8, acrylates, etc.), Houbertz et al. invented a Ticontaining ORMOCER-based MPL resin named ORMOCER PD92. This was then used to fabricate PhCs with a PBG in the NIR region. Shrinkage during MPL, which is typical to Ti-containing resins, remains a problem [73].
In addition to ORMOCERs, there are other sol–gel materials that are used in MPL technology for photonics.
--[p265]-- As shown in a study on PhCs [71], it is possible to enhance the resolution ofPhC microstructures via postbaking at temperatures between 150 and $3 0 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ after the solvent development step [68]. Final curing ensures that the maximum crosslinking degree is reached.
In the literature, there are several diferent applications where ORMOCERs were successfully employed.
--[p264 [表]]-- The very low root-mean-squared (RMS) roughness indicates an almost perfect planarization behavior, which is important for substrates and makes the material interesting for highly sophisticated optical applications (Table 10.2) [61].
Table 10.2 Properties of three ORMOCER resins for optical applications.

--[p263]-- This sol–gel process is carried out without water, which improves the yield [60]. Alkoxylation is favored here because of the bulky phenyl rings which sterically prevent self-condensation of DPD. Thus silanol groups are hardly present in the final polysiloxane network. The byproduct methanol or ethanol can be removed by evaporation [61].
--[p265]-- MPL methodology tends to be streamlined relative to traditional UV methods: pre- and post-exposure baking steps, as well as solvent dilution, are not necessary as with conventional UV curing, because the polymerization takes place within the resin volume [61]. Post-baking can, however, be useful because the adhesion of the microstructures to the substrate is improved [66].
--[p263]-- ORMOCER resins can be cured with UV radiation, in which case two striking benefits distinguish this technology from conventional UV-curable resins. Precursor siloxane oligomers organize with sizes of 1–5 nm to allow very high resolution in UV lithography. Resolution is further enhanced by high crosslink density, which leads to rapid termination and minimizes unwanted polymerization in nonilluminated regions [58].
--[p265]-- In addition to increased feature resolution, an enhancement in refractive index was also observed. Reinhardt et al. presented MPL-fabricated nanostructures showing efective excitation and focusing of surface plasmon polaritons (SPPs). In this case, the ORMOCER with a suficiently high dielectric constant was covered with a 50-nm gold film by electron sputtering. In Figure 10.15, an SPP excitation event at a 3D ramp structure is shown [67].
--[p265]-- A distributed-feedback resonator, which might be used for optical lab-on-a-chip applications, was fabricated via MPL by Woggon et al. using the Ormocomp resin [66]. The resonator was based on a 400-nm periodic grating with 40 nm height modulation. Partial MPL structuring within the substrate, which was caused accidently by slight tilting of the cover slip, was circumvented by fabricating the resonator on a stage with a thickness of a voxel.
--[p264]-- ORMOCERs gain hardness, chemical resistance, and transparency from their glassy component. Durability, ease of processing, and range of functionality arise from their partially organic character. Silicone portions ensure flexibility and adequate surface properties [63]. By applying trialkoxysilanes and trifunctional metal alkoxides, which form tight crosslinks, Young’s moduli greater than 10 GPa are obtained.
--[p263]-- Sol–Gel-Derived Resins
Composites, in particular nanocomposites, are intensively studied for optical applications because of the great breadth in material properties. ORMOCERs are a popular class of nanocomposites, known both for their excellent material properties and for ease of processing [58]. ORMOCER photosensitive resins consist of sol–gel-erived oligomers containing organic and inorganic groups, purely organic monomers, and other additives including a photoinitiator.
--[p266]-- Finally, the extensive work at the Fraunhofer Institute, where ORMOCER materials were actually developed, must be mentioned. In particular, Houbertz and coworkers have successfully used ORMOCER resins for MPL structuring of optical elements [59, 61, 69, 73, 74]. In one work, waveguides were fabricated to connect a photodiode and a laser diode on a printed circuit board using only one material. The MPL structured areas served as the core, while the surrounding material served as the clad.
--[p263]-- Other silanes, such as, for example, diphenylsilanediol (DPD), are employed as network modifiers [58]. According to the literature, low light absorption – a key property for photonic materials – can be achieved by lowering the content of C–H and silanol moieties. A synthetic method for modifying silanol groups is depicted in Figure 10.14.
DPD is reacted with MEMO with a condensation catalyst at temperatures between 60 and 100 <sup>∘</sup>C.
--[p263]-- Typical ORMOCER formulations for optical applications include Ormocore (DPD + MEMO), Ormoclad (DPD + MEMO + TTS (3,3,3-trifluoropropyl trimethoxysilane)), and Ormocomp (TTS + TTASS) [62]. For many applications, it is desirable to integrate highly refractive elements such as sulfur (Ormocomp) or titanium in the resins. Typical Ti-containing formulations consist of 17 mol% MEMO, 50 mol% DPD, and 33 mol% Ti(OEt) . Processing viscosity can be lowered with solvents such as THF or cyclopentanone.
--[p264]-- While some of these materials have ambient-temperature glass transitions, in other materials no transition is observed at all. Difunctional monomers form linear or cyclic oligomers with the behavior of elastomers: low Young’s moduli of around 5–100 MPa and glass transitions below room temperature.
--[p266]-- During polymerization, organic polymerizable moieties difuse toward the polymerizing region and cause the desired refractive index change. Importantly, the solvent development step is eliminated. Instead, only a post-curing thermal step is required. PhCs have also been fabricated by the Fraunhofer group using ORMOCERs. Shrinkage problems are addressed by scaling computer-aided design (CAD) structures with a shrinkage constant prior to building [61].
--[p265]-- For the active material layer, the organic semiconductor tris-8-hydroxyquinoline aluminum doped with the laser dye 4-(dicyanomethylene)-2-methyl-6-(4- dimethylaminostyryl)-4H-pyrane was applied.
Leyrer et al. introduced waveguide defects via MPL into polymer opals using ORMOCER B59. This well demonstrates the applicability of MPL for creating nonperiodic structures, which are more complicated with other nanostructuring techniques (e.g., interference lithography) [68, 72].
--[p267]-- As an example, Zr-based sol–gel resins exhibit advantages such as good control of refractive index via the Zr content, improved mechanical stability and – perhaps most importantly – very low volume shrinkage [75]. With suficient laser power, PhC structures from Zr resins with no measurable shrinkage are even possible [76]. This fundamental benefit is thought to arise from the combined inorganic and organic polymerization reactions occurring in the resin.
--[p264]-- Mixing diferent precursors such as tri- or difunctional alkoxysilanes, metaloxides, and macromonomers enables control of the mechanical properties from the range of soft and flexible polymers to rigid and sometimes brittle glasses [58]. A crucial optical property, namely optical attenuation, can be as low as 0.2–0.3 dB $\mathrm { c m ^ { - 1 } }$ at 1320 nm. Refractive indices can be tuned from 1.47 to 1.58 (at 633 nm), which allows suficient contrast for waveguides.
--[p265]-- Prior to MPL microfabrication of optical elements, ORMOCER resins may be prebaked for a few minutes at $8 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ to increase resin viscosity. It is also useful to carefully clean the (glass) substrate and thus ensure good adhesion of the resin. Films can be prepared by spin coating but also by simply casting the resin into the cavity between two cover slips [65–67]. As radical initiator, Irgacure 369 is frequently used [68–70].
--[p263]-- The inorganic element of the ORMOCER is most commonly silicon but may also be Al, Zr, or Ti, which tend to be bound via alkoxyl bridges to organic moieties including acrylates. Thanks to the hydrolytic stability of the Si–C bond, it is possible to introduce various functional groups to modify properties including flexibility, refractive index, and so on. If the organic moiety is amenable to polymerization, it will contribute to network formation.

===== [p275-275] Fabrication of Waveguides and Other Optical Elements by Multiphoton Lithography > Conclusions (3块) =====
--[p275]-- Photo-curable polymers based on acrylates and epoxides are the most commonly applied materials, although thiol–ene resins are also commercially available and increasingly popular. Photo-curable nanocomposite resins (principally monomers with Si, Ti, or Zr and covalently bound acrylate moieties) permit direct structuring of microscopic structures with lower optical loss and higher refractive indices than allowed by traditional organic resins.
--[p275]-- Multiphoton-induced chemical transformations, aside from polymerization, are also applied in the field of photonics. In particular, MP photochromism is intensively studied for ultra-compact optical data storage. In the coming years, the use of microphotonics is expected to become more widespread. If so, MPL will serve as an enabling technology certainly in the development and possibly the production stages of these next-generation photonic devices.
--[p275]-- Conclusions
Photons have some inherent advantages to electrons in data transmission, storage, and processing, although the manipulation of light at length scales below the difraction limit is not very straightforward. MPL is a powerful tool for constructing well-defined 3D microstructures with feature widths well below 100 nm. Some of the more common optical elements fabricated by MPL include PhCs, waveguides, lenses, and thermooptic switches.

===== [p103-107] 5 STED-Inspired Approaches to Resolution Enhancement > Stimulated Emission Depletion Fluorescence Microscopy (26块) =====
--[p103]-- Stimulated Emission Depletion Fluorescence Microscopy
Traditional fluorescence microscopy faces some of the same challenges, as does photolithography. The Abbe criterion also dictates the minimum distance between resolvable features in imaging, for instance. However, in fluorescence microscopy, photodamage limits the shortest wavelength of radiation that can be employed, particularly in the case of live-cell imaging.
--[p104]-- It is also possible to employ two deactivation beams, each with its own phase mask, to improve the resolution in the transverse and axial dimensions simultaneously [24].
The dimensions of the dark region of the PSF of the deactivation beam are limited by difraction. However, operating at an intensity that is in the saturation regime causes the efective dimensions ofthe bright region ofa PSF to be enlarged.
--[p106]-- Second, having the stimulated emission wavelength far to the red of the origin of the absorption spectrum prevents the deactivation beam from exciting molecules, which is especially important if there are Franck–Condon-active, low-frequency vibrations that lead to the presence of hot bands. Third, the farther to the red that $\lambda _ { \mathrm { S E } }$ lies, the smaller the probability of absorption to higher lying excited states occurring.
--[p105]-- Thus, the majority of fluorescence only occurs after IVR, and so can be considered to arise from the vibrationless level of $\mathrm { S } _ { 1 }$
Excitation and emission transitions are typically vertical, which is to say that the nuclear positions to not change during the electronic transition.
--[p103]-- The inability to use shorter wavelengths for biological imaging has been a driving force in the development of new, super-resolved techniques for optical microscopy, which include STED [15, 16] and others [18–20].
In a typical implementation of laser fluorescence microscopy, fluorescent molecules are excited by a tightly focused laser beam at wavelength $\lambda _ { \mathrm { e x } }$ , and fluorescence is detected at wavelengths that are longer than $\lambda _ { \mathrm { e x } } .$ .
--[p106]-- However, highly excited molecules also usually have a higher probability of undergoing intersystem crossing to a long-lived triplet state, or even of undergoing irreversible photochemistry.
For the above reasons, EEA should be avoided if possible in STED microscopy.
--[p104]-- If the PSFs of the excitation and deactivation beams were to overlap completely, then no improvement in resolution would be realized. A key feature of STED microscopy is that a spatial phase mask in the path of the deactivation beam is used to engineer the PSF to contain a dark region in which stimulated emission will not occur.
--[p107]-- For this reason, it is optimal for the deactivation pulse in STED to have a duration that is significantly longer than the IVR time in the ground electronic state, which allows IVR to depopulate the lower state in the stimulated emission transition continuously. It is also desirable for the deactivation pulse to be much shorter than the fluorescence lifetime. Meeting both of these conditions allows the most eficient possible deactivation.
--[p105]-- The excitation beam takes a fluorophore from the ground electronic state $( \mathrm { S } _ { 0 } )$ to a vibrationally excited level of the first excited electronic state $( \mathsf { S } _ { 1 } )$ . Intramolecular vibrational redistribution (IVR) then causes the molecule to relax to the vibrationless level of $\mathrm { S } _ { 1 }$ . IVR typically occurs on a timescale of a few picoseconds [26], which is much shorter than the fluorescence lifetime of a good fluorophore.
--[p104]-- For instance, a spiral phase element [23] (Figure 5.2a) leads to a PSF with a dark region along its axis (Figure 5.2b), and a phase plate with a halfwave delay in its central region (Figure 5.2c) leads to a PSF with a dark region that is confined along the beam axis (Figure 5.2d). The former phase mask can be used to improve transverse resolution, whereas the latter can be used to improve axial resolution.
--[p106]-- A sum rule dictates that the sum of the oscillator strengths out of a given electronic state must be a fixed quantity [29]. Typical fluorophores have large extinction coeficients so that they can be excited eficiently. This condition implies that the oscillator strength from $\mathrm { S } _ { 0 }$ to $\mathrm { S } _ { 1 }$ is large.
--[p106]-- However, all fluorophores have higher lying excited states, and the energy gap between $\mathrm { S } _ { 1 }$ and the lowest of these states is typically less than the energy gap between $\mathrm { S } _ { 0 }$ and $\mathrm { S } _ { 1 }$ . It is therefore dificult to prevent EEA through control of $\lambda _ { \mathrm { S E } }$ alone. Luckily, the quantum mechanical properties of fluorophores come to the rescue in this case.
--[p106]-- Because the minimum of the $\mathrm { S } _ { 1 }$ potential energy surface is usually shifted from that of the $\mathrm { S } _ { 0 }$ surface, the emission transition is generally red-shifted as compared to the excitation transition.
--[p105]-- In the case of the excitation beam, this phenomenon is undesirable, but in the case of the deactivation beam the saturation efect can in principle be harnessed to make the efective dark region as small as desired [15]. In practice, there are limits to the ultimate resolution that can be attained through this technique, but resolutions that are far below the Abbe limit are readily achieved.
--[p103]-- The resolution in the transverse dimension is determined largely by how tightly the excitation beam can be focused (which depends upon $\lambda _ { \mathrm { e x } } )$ . Two features that are separated by a distance that is significantly less that $\lambda _ { \mathrm { e x } }$ will be excited simultaneously, and therefore cannot be resolved.
--[p106]-- A large Stokes’ shift is desirable in fluorescence microscopy, as it causes the excitation and emission to occur at substantially diferent wavelengths.
The stimulated emission pulse, which is timed to arrive after IVR is complete in $\mathrm { S } _ { 1 } ,$ drives molecules from the vibrationless excited electronic state to a vibrationally excited level of $\mathrm { S } _ { 0 }$ .
--[p103]-- Thus, if one laser pulse is used to excite a molecule at wavelength $\lambda _ { \mathrm { e x } }$ and another laser pulse stimulates emission from the molecule at wavelength $\lambda _ { \mathrm { S E } }$ before the molecule has had time to fluoresce, then no emission will be detected from that molecule. We will here refer to the first laser as the “excitation laser” and the second laser as the “deactivation laser”.
--[p103]-- Super-resolved techniques for optical microscopy were enabled by the realization that if one can use photochemical or photophysical means to prevent nearby fluorophores from being excited simultaneously, then there is no reason why the resolution of fluorescence microscopy cannot be far better than what is predicted by the Abbe criterion. Indeed, methods that achieve superresolution in this manner were the subject of the 2014 Nobel Prize in chemistry.
--[p103]-- STED takes advantage of the fact that whereas fluorescence in a sample being imaged is usually emitted isotropically, stimulated emission always follows the direction of propagation of the stimulation source [22].
--[p107]-- One important practical reason for this limit is that full saturation of the deactivation transition leads to degradation of the resolution. In the saturation limit, the populations of the lower and upper states involved in a transition become equal, and the ability to depopulate the upper state through stimulated emission is therefore lost.
--[p106]-- In this case, the oscillator strength from $\mathrm { S } _ { 1 }$ to $\mathrm { S } _ { 0 }$ must also be large, which leads to a high fluorescence quantum yield. Ifthe $\mathrm { S } _ { 1 } - \mathrm { S } _ { 0 }$ oscillator strength is large, then the oscillator strength from $\mathrm { S } _ { 1 }$ to higher lying excited states must be small, which reduces the likelihood of EEA occurring.
In practice, there is a limit to the degree to which STED can enhance resolution.
--[p106]-- This Stokes’ shift can become substantially larger if the magnitude and/or direction of the molecular dipole moment difer in $\mathrm { S } _ { 0 }$ and $\mathrm { S } _ { 1 } ,$ in which case the surrounding medium will react to the excitation by reorganizing to stabilize the newly formed electronic excited state (which in turn destabilizes the ground electronic state) [27].
--[p103]-- In this scheme, fluorescence is collected from excited molecules everywhere along the beam path. By employing confocal detection, fluorescence generated outside of the center of the focal region can be rejected [21]. This efect can increase the resolution substantially, particularly in the axial direction. Confocal detection ofers only limited resolution improvement in the transverse plane, however.
--[p106]-- For a number of reasons, it is usually ideal if $\lambda _ { \mathrm { S E } }$ can be considerably longer than the wavelength of the maximum of the fluorescence spectrum. First, this separation allows scattered deactivation light to be filtered out from the fluorescence signal.
--[p105]-- In one recent, highly favorable case, 2.4-nm resolution was achieved using 532-nm excitation and 775-nm deactivation in a non-biological sample [25].
To understand how similar concepts can be applied to photolithography, it is instructive to consider first the molecular photophysics of STED (Figure 5.3).
--[p106]-- For all of these reasons, it is desirable to perform STED microscopy using dyes that have large Stokes’ shifts [17].
The potential for disruption of the STED process by higher lying excited states through excited-state/excited-state absorption (EEA) is worth considering in greater detail. A molecule that is excited to a higher excited state typically still fluoresces from $\mathrm { S } _ { 1 }$ [28], so in the best case EEA simply does not lead to deactivation.

===== [p318-319] Nanoreplication Printing and Nanosurface Processing > Micro-transfer Molding (�TM) (10块) =====
--[p318]-- So while the mask used in conventional lithography is expensive and slow to make itself, it can be used over and over again to quickly produce millions of devices. This ability to replicate devices is at the core of the inexpensive electronic devices available today. A similar parallel strategy can be applied to multiphoton lithography, but instead of using a 2D mask to reproduce features, a 3D mold can be used.
The technique of μTM was popularized by Prof.
--[p318]-- George Whitesides as part of a suite of tools known as Soft Lithography [9]. In Soft Lithography, an elastomeric polymer, polydimethylsiloxane (PDMS), is poured over a lithographed master and cured. The cured PDMS can then be used as a stamp, a mold, or as a functional device itself. When it is used as a mold, the technique is called microtransfer molding (Figure 13.1).
--[p318]-- The low surface energy of PDMS enables it to wet surfaces very well, resulting in feature resolutions that are less than 100 nm. The early examples of this technique predated multiphoton lithography and focused on conventionally lithographed masters, but it was not long after that masters made by TPIP were used with μTM.
--[p319]-- There are two molding techniques: nanoimprint lithography (NIL) [10–12] and particle replication in nonwetting templates (PRINT) [13, 14], which are closely related to μTM but result in parts without flash. These techniques have not yet been used with TPIP-created masters. NIL is similar to μTM except that pressure is applied before curing to ensure the layer of flash is extremely thin, usually ∼100 nm.
--[p319]-- Since structures made by TPIP can have any arbitrary geometry, some ofthe first demonstrations of nanoreplication using μTM showed the ability to reproduce undercut and high-aspect-ratio features, which are nontrivial geometries to fabricate by conventional photolithography. For example, the set of cantilevers shown in Figure 13.2 demonstrates that large overhanging features can be reproducibly molded and released using PDMS molds.
--[p318]-- Alternative methods have been shown to increase fabrication speed, such as projection lithography [5, 6] and beam splitting [7, 8], but these optical setups can be somewhat complicated. Conventional photolithography, which uses masks, does not sufer from this serial writing problem because millions of features are patterned simultaneously in parallel.
--[p318]-- Micro-transfer Molding (�TM)
TPIP is a slow way to create a device not only because each object is made one at a time but also because each point within that object is individually created and connected to the previous one. This means that, despite fabrication speeds now approaching centimeter per second, it can still be rather time consuming to create a single device [4].
--[p319]-- PDMS is able to mold such geometries because of its low modulus, 2 N mm<sup>−2</sup>, which makes it very flexible. PDMS has also been used to mold some very high-aspect-ratio structures, such as the tower shown in Figure 13.2. This tower is 10 μm × 10 μm in cross section and 500 μm tall. The replicas can also be used to create new molds, and masters can be reused to create new molds repeatedly.<sup>1</sup>
--[p319]-- One issue with μTM is that, usually, the molded part is not able to be isolated as a free piece because it is connected to a replica of the substrate. Even though only the molded piece is filled and the mold is cleaned of liquid resin, there is inevitably some “flash” remaining around the new part. Macroscopically molded parts are usually deflashed by tumbling or grinding, but this is not feasible with microscopic parts.
--[p319]-- After curing, this thin layer is removed by reactive ion etching, leaving clean parts on a substrate that is not made of the same material. In PRINT, the molding elastomer is not PDMS, but instead a fluorinated silane that is highly nonwetting, which causes only the part to be filled with monomer and results in essentially flash-free particles. Liquidia Technologies Inc. has already scaled PRINT up for the mass production of microscopic particles using a reel-to-reel format for the mold.
These two techniques are poised for interesting future application of multiphoton nanoreplication, since both of these methods eliminate flash.

===== [p184-184] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > o-Nitrobenzyl esters (2块) =====
--[p184]-- o-Nitrobenzyl esters
As one of the most heavily utilized photocleavable linkers, the use of o-nitrobenzyl esters for light-based molecular cleavage extends back to the 1970s [122]. Upon photoexcitation, the nitrobenzyl group forms an aci-nitro intermediate through either a singlet or triplet excited state. This intermediate further decays to generate both a nitroso and an acid/amide byproduct (depending on parent substitution) [123].
--[p184]-- Cleavage rates and photoactive wavelengths (250 < � < 400 nm) can be tuned by varying molecular substituents [124–126]. These reactions have been used for the uncaging of proteins [127, 128], controlling cell adhesions [129, 130], photodegrading polymers and biomaterials [70, 131–134], and probing the efects of physical material cues on cell function [135–137].

===== [p286-286] Fabricating Nano and Microstructures Made by Narrow Bandgap Semiconductors and Metals using Multiphoton Lithography > Confirmation of PbSe Formation (3块) =====
--[p286]-- The slightly larger amount of Pb can be explained as a Pb-rich structure surface due to the low utilization rate of Se in the photoresin [22]. There are also tiny amounts of carbon and oxygen elements identified. These are residual organic molecules attached to the surface of the fabricated PbSe structure. To show the crystal structure of the fabricated PbSe structures, a high-resolution transmission electron microscopy (HRTEM) image is shown in Figure 11.4b.
--[p286]-- The image confirms the formation of PbSe with a rocksalt phase.
The EDS measurement was performed at a fabricated PbSe block at an area of $2 0 \times 2 0 \mu \mathrm { m } ^ { 2 }$ to reduce random error. Because Pb and Se are the main heavy elements of the fabricated structure, the atomic ratio measured by the EDS is quantitative. HRTEM was performed on a FEI Tecnai F30 transmission electron microscope.
--[p286]-- Confirmation of PbSe Formation
It is important to confirm the formation of the PbSe structure by two-photon lithography. The energy-dispersive spectrum (EDS) ofthe fabricated structure was measured, which is shown in Figure 11.4a. From the EDS measurement, Pb and Se elements can be identified with an atomic ratio of Pb $/ \mathrm { S e } = 1 . 0 7 : 1$ . This almost 1 : 1 ratio of Pb and Se confirms that the formed structure is mainly constituted with nearly equal amounts of Pb and Se elements.

===== [p355-355] Three-Dimensional Microstructures for Biological Applications > Final Remarks (3块) =====
--[p355]-- The applications of these unmodified or (chemically, biologically, etc.) modified 3D structures span from cell studies to scafolds for tissue regeneration, platforms for bacterial and cell growth investigation, as well as platforms for drug delivery, with great impact in areas such as medicine, biology, and chemistry.
--[p355]-- Final Remarks
Two-photon polymerization has proven to be a powerful technique for designing 3D micro and nanostructures for biological applications. Several polymers and modified ceramics have been demonstrated biocompatible, allowing applications in biological environments. Additionally, a range of molecules have been investigated to play the role of photoinitiators in 2PP.
--[p355]-- Although such microstructures are usually fabricated with femtosecond laser apparatus, by chemically designing specific resins and photoinitiators, less expensive laser systems can be employed in this fabrication process, which is a fundamental aspect for making this fabrication technology available to a wider community.

===== [p236-237] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Structures Fabricated in ChG Film (10块) =====
--[p236]-- Raman spectroscopy has been used to show that thermally deposited $\mathrm { A s } _ { 2 } \mathrm { S } _ { 3 }$ is highly photosensitive when it contains a high fraction of ${ \mathrm { A s } } _ { 4 } {  { S _ { 6 } } }$ and clusters [4].
--[p237]-- This system produced features as small as 180 nm (Figure 9.22) [149]. Thermal deposition conditions were observed to influence the morphology, size, and appearance of nanostructures in thin ${ \mathrm { A s } } _ { 2 } { \mathrm { S } } _ { 3 }$ films. Schwarz et al.
--[p236]-- The shape, size, and optical properties of features produced by MPL in deposited ${ \mathrm { A s } } _ { 2 } { \mathrm { S } } _ { 3 }$ films have been shown to be sensitive to the conditions used for thermal deposition, photoexposure, and etching [4, 5, 145, 147–153].

--[p236]-- Light exposure crosslinks the material, transforming the ${ \mathrm { A s } } _ { 4 } {  { S _ { 6 } } }$ cages and other clusters back to ${ \mathrm { A s } } _ { 2 } { \mathrm { S } } _ { 3 }$ network glass, red-shifting the band edge, and increasing the local refractive index [145]. MPL can be used to photo-pattern thin films of thermally deposited $\mathrm { A s } _ { 2 } \mathrm { S } _ { 3 } ,$ and then a chemical etchant can be used to remove the unexposed, non-cross-linked regions.
--[p237]-- ChGs were also found to be sensitive to the repetition rate of the laser beam [151]. By investigating the heat accumulation at the laser focal spot and the material’s response, an optimal condition for high-resolution fabrication in ${ \mathrm { A s } } _ { 2 } { \mathrm { S } } _ { 3 }$ was found [151]. Using these MPL conditions, nanorods with dimensions of68 nm (�/12) and good mechanical properties could be created as well as 3D woodpile PCs with a stopgap in the NIR [151]. Cumming et al.
--[p236]-- Structures Fabricated in ChG Film
Certain ChGs can be thermally deposited as thin glassy films (200 nm to $2 0 \mu \mathrm { m } )$ of photosensitive molecular clusters. ${ \mathrm { A s } } _ { 2 } { \mathrm { S } } _ { 3 }$ is an attractive material because it has high transparency in the IR, high refractive index $\left( n = 2 . 4 5 - 2 . 5 3 \right)$ , and a lowenergy band edge (530 nm), which makes it a good candidate for applications in sensing, waveguiding, and acoustooptics [4, 5].
--[p237]-- fabricated functional 3D PCs in ${ \mathrm { A s } } _ { 2 } { \mathrm { S } } _ { 3 }$ [146]. Three-dimensional woodpile PCs with a complete PBG were fabricated in ${ \mathrm { A s } } _ { 2 } { \mathrm { S } } _ { 3 }$ and etched using increasing concentrations of diisopentylamine (DIPA) in dimethylsulfoxide (DMSO) [4]. Higher etch selectivity was obtained by using etchants based on novel hindered amines, such as N-(4-methoxybenzyl)-(pyren-1-yl)amine.
--[p237]-- used a spatial light modulator to remove spherical aberration, which was found to decrease the size of written features with fabrication depth. This system was used to create high-quality PCs and chiral gyroid PCs in lithium niobate and ${ \mathrm { A s } } _ { 2 } { \mathrm { S } } _ { 3 }$ films [155, 156].
--[p236]-- Thermally deposited ${ \mathrm { A s } } _ { 2 } { \mathrm { S } } _ { 3 }$ can be thought of as an inorganic analog of a negativetone photopolymer. ChGs have the advantage over photopolymers that they do not exhibit swelling or shrinking during the etching process [146].

===== [p302-306] Microfluidic Devices Produced by Two-Photon-Induced Polymerization > Optically Driven Micromachines (22块) =====
--[p302]-- The history and previous work on optical tweezers are available in earlier reviews [19–22].
Recently, optical manipulation techniques have been applied to remotely controlled microfluidic devices. For example, synchronized manipulation of microparticles inside a microchannel was applied to create microfluidic components such as micropumps and microvalves [23, 24].
--[p306]-- The targeted microparticle was also fabricated by two-photon microfabrication for the demonstration of the performance of the manipulators. It was demonstrated that the targeted microparticle could be held away from the substrate. In the above experiments on the evaluation of the following velocity of the arm, the trapping point was 2.4 μm in diameter. However, the holding force at the probe tips was not suficient to grasp a 5-μm-diameter microparticle.
--[p302]-- By controlling the 3D microstructures according to the desired motions, several types of micromachines could be produced [13–16, 29–33]. The optically driven micromachines ofer unique, remotely controlled microdevices such as micropumps and micromanipulators applicable for microfluidic devices.
Figure 12.7 shows optical microscope images of optically driven micromanipulators. The micromanipulators were synchronously controlled by a time-shared optical trapping technique [15].
--[p303]-- If the probes attached to the manipulator arms are designed to grasp a microobject tightly, it can be caught between the probes while the arms are synchronously rotated by use of time-shared scanning of a laser beam.
A unique feature of the 3D micromanipulators is the rotational motion of the manipulator arm.
--[p304]-- A microparticle (diameter: 2.4 μm) is attached to the top of each handle. The handle with the microparticle was fabricated along the optical axis, while the supporting stage is lowered stepwise at a constant pitch of 0.5 μm. The length is changed from 5 to 15 μm. The power and the scanning speed of the laser beam are 200 mW and $5 0 \mu \mathrm { m } \mathrm { s } ^ { - 1 }$ , respectively.
--[p302]-- However, in the above examples, the target samples manipulated by optical tweezers are merely microparticles. Therefore, the structure and performance of the microfluidic devices are limited by the shape and size of the microparticles.
By contrast, we have utilized sophisticated 3D microstructures produced by two-photon microfabrication instead of target microparticles.
--[p302]-- By scanning the laser beam at high speed, multiple micromanipulators can be driven simultaneously. In this demonstration, a glass microbead whose diameter was 5 μm was grasped by the manipulator in a liquid. Using three micromanipulators, another type of micromanipulators could be constructed, as shown in Figure 12.8. In this case, a glass microbead could be tightly fixed at the center of the manipulators.
--[p304]-- On the other hand, the micromanipulators with rotating arms allow the handling of a microobject separated from the substrate. The 3D manipulation of microobjects will be useful for the manipulation of floating biological samples such as blood cells and microbes and for holding a biological sample released from a substrate.
--[p302]-- For this reason, electrostatic actuators are not suitable for biological applications such as microfluidic devices and micromanipulation tools for cells.
On the other hand, radiation pressure from a tightly focused laser beam can be used as optical tweezers to confine, position, and transport microparticles in liquid. Ashkin’s group first demonstrated this technique in 1986 [18].
--[p304]-- To evaluate the driving performance of the manipulators, several manipulator arms with handles of diferent lengths were fabricated through the direct writing process of a femtosecond laser beam. Figure 12.10 shows the top and the side views of the manipulator arms (length: 22.5 μm, width: 2 μm) with a handle (length: 5 μm, diameter: 2 μm). For the simplification of the experiments, the manipulator arms do not have probe tips for grasping a microobject.
--[p306]-- Therefore, in the experiments, the diameter of the trapping point was increased to 6 μm in order to increase the holding force. As a result, the targeted microparticle was grasped tightly with the two rotating arms. In the experiment, the two arms with probes were cooperatively controlled by time-shared scanning of a laser beam. The power and scanning speed of the laser beam were 500 mW and $3 \mu \mathrm { m } \mathrm { s } ^ { - 1 }$ , respectively.
--[p303]-- In Figure 12.9b, when a laser beam is focused on the handle below the attached microparticle and scanned perpendicular to the arm in the focal plane, the handle is inclined toward the focus because of the net radiation pressure exerted on the handle and the particle. At that time, since the base of the manipulator arm is restricted to the housing, the manipulator arm is rotated on its long axis. The rotational mechanism allows the manipulatation of a microobject in three dimensions.
--[p302]-- Optically Driven Micromachines
In the late 1980s, MEMS such as microgears and microactuators were developed by using silicon-based micromachining techniques [17]. Most MEMS utilize electrostatic actuators as the major driving source. Electrostatic actuators have been used in practical microdevices, including digital micro-mirror devices (DMD)s and RF MEMS. However, it is dificult to utilize electrostatic force in liquids such as electrolytes.
--[p302]-- Use of the spin angular momentum carried by circularly polarized optical tweezers was also made to construct a micropump with birefringent microparticles [25]. In addition, a Laguerre–Gaussian (LG) beam carrying orbital angular momentum was used for revolving multiple microparticles along its ring-like intensity distribution, called optical vortex [26, 27]. The rotation of microparticles using optical vortices was applied to microfluidic devices [28].
--[p303]-- In addition, we demonstrated a novel type of 3D micromanipulators for holding of a microobject in three dimensions [29]. Figure 12.9a shows a schematic diagram and the driving principle of optically controlled micromanipulators with rotating arms. The micromanipulator arm has a handle to rotate the arm about its long axis. A microparticle is attached to the tip of the handle as a trapping point. Figure 12.9b shows a cross section of the micromanipulators.
--[p306]-- The repetition rate of time-shared laser scanning for trapping each handle is 200 Hz. Figure 12.12b shows the transportation of a microparticle using a single arm with two probes. In this case, the targeted microparticle is pushed and slid on the glass substrate.
Optically controlled micromanipulators will be applied for noninvasive manipulation of living cells.
--[p305]-- When the handle is rotated at the maximum following velocity, the net radiation pressure is equal to the viscous drag exerted on the handle and the attached microparticle.
Figure 12.11 shows the dependence of the following velocity of the handle on its length. When the microparticle as a trapping point is not attached to the handle, the following velocities are almost constant at ${ \sim } 7 \mu \mathrm { m } s ^ { - 1 }$ .
--[p305]-- On the other hand, the following velocities of the handles with trapping points are twice as fast as those of the cases without trapping points. This result indicates that the trapping point plays an important role to tilt the handle.
Manipulation of a microparticle using the micromanipulators was demonstrated. Figure 12.12a shows the handling of a 5-μm-diameter microparticle with two manipulator arms.
--[p304]-- In most optical trapping techniques using galvano scanners, since a single laser beam is scanned in the focal plane, the motion of the trapped particle is restricted to translational or rotational motions in the focal plane. For this reason, the optically controlled manipulators that we have already developed [14] were driven in the focal plane. Therefore, the targeted microparticle is merely held in two dimensions on the substrate.
--[p304]-- In 3D micromanipulators, the manipulator arm is rotated about its long axis because of the radiation pressure exerted on both the handle and the particle attached to the handle, as shown in Figure 12.9b. The net radiation pressure depends on some parameters such as the length of the handle, diameter of the particle, and focusing position along the handle.
--[p302]-- Optical tweezers provide unique features such as remote manipulation of micro/nanoparticles in liquid, noninvasive manipulation of biological samples, precise manipulation in a sealed environment, and extremely small torque on the order of $1 0 ^ { - 1 2 }$ Nm. For these reasons, optical tweezers and the related techniques have been widely applied to studies on biological cells and DNA molecules, and microchemistry with microdroplets and microbeads.
--[p305]-- As shown in Figure 12.9b, the manipulator arm is rolled by slanting the handle by scanning the focused laser beam linearly in the direction perpendicular to the long axis of the arm. In our experiments, the magnitude of the net radiation pressure to roll the arm is evaluated by measuring the maximum following velocity of the handle while the scanning speed of the laser beam is increased.

===== [p321-324] Nanoreplication Printing and Nanosurface Processing > Nano-replication of Other Materials (9块) =====
--[p322]-- A sol–gel reaction proceeded following exposure to moist air, forming nanoporous $\mathrm { T i O } _ { 2 }$ . The SU-8 master was then removed by calcination at $6 0 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ . The final photonic crystal showed a bandgap in the near-infrared.
A chiral photonic crystal was also made by Gansel et al. using multiphoton lithography, but this time by using a positive photoresist on a transparent conductive indium-tin oxide (ITO) substrate [24].
--[p324]-- Unfortunately, this may not be possible given the complex geometry of a photonic crystal, but it has been done with simpler shapes such as a pyramid.
SiC-based ceramics have been patterned by μTM of urethane acrylate masters. The molds were filled with polyvinylsilazane, which, upon heating to $8 0 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ becomes amorphous SiC ceramic with >50% shrinkage [25].
--[p321]-- Nano-replication of Other Materials
The vast majority of objects made using multiphoton lithography are acrylates, epoxides, or organically modified ceramics, ORMOCERS, which are hybrid materials having a –O–Si–O– backbone and acrylic or epoxy groups for crosslinking [17, 18]. Chalcogenide resins [19] and resins containing titanium and zirconium [20, 21] have also been used in TPIP (see Chapter 3 for more examples).
--[p321]-- But another way to create structures out of diferent material is by making a master structure out of a photopolymer, molding it, and then filling the mold with a diferent material, such as silicon. This can be done in a conservative way where the mold can be reused, as in conventional injection molding, or in a sacrificial way where the master and mold are consumed for each part made, as in sand casting.
--[p324]-- Carbon microstructures were also molded by Maruo and coworkers using specially developed resins that carbonize eficiently [26]. These resins contain an epoxy, resorcinol diglycidyl ether, which has a high carbon content, and shrinks <30% upon carbonization. Figure 13.7 shows SiC pyramids and an approximately 5-μm-wide rabbit made of carbon.
--[p323]-- After development of the exposed photoresist, the unexposed resist acted like a mold for the electrodeposition of gold. This method of galvanoforming has been used in the past as part of a set of techniques known by the German acronym LIGA (Lithographi, Galvanoformung, Abformung), but this was the first demonstration of LIGA using a multiphoton process to create the initial pattern. The initial pattern was of spirals having varying numbers of turns.
--[p322]-- The original polymer material is then vaporized at high temperature, and the void is filled with Si, which is also deposited by CVD. Their final log-pile structure showed a bandgap near 2.3 μm.
Chichkov and coworkers also made photonic crystals by inverting an SU-8 master into titania [23]. Following fabrication of their woodpile structure, it was soaked in a solution of Ti(IV) isopropoxide in ethanol and allowed to react under an inert atmosphere.
--[p323]-- It was found the gold photonic crystal had very diferent transmission spectra between 3 and 8 μm for left- and right-handed circularly polarized light.
Both these methods to create photonic crystals out ofSi and Au are very impressive, but both processes only use the master and mold structure once and are inevitably wasteful and slow. A preferable method would be to reuse the mold for each replica.
--[p322]-- Wegener and coworkers were the first to create a 3D photonic crystal out of silicon by first creating a pattern by multiphoton lithography and then “double inverting” it into a Si structure [22]. The schematic in Figure 13.6 shows the steps involved. Briefly, the polymer microstructure made by TPIP is coated with $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ using chemical vapor deposition (CVD) to create a mold.

===== [p157-157] Hybrid Materials for Multiphoton Polymerization > 7.4 (1块) =====

===== [p211-212] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Organic Composites for Electrically Conductive Microstructures (7块) =====
--[p212]-- An electrically conductive all-polymer 3D microstructure was fabricated by MPL and shown to have a conductivity of $0 . 0 4 \mathrm { S c m ^ { - 1 } }$ (Figure 9.4) [53]. A blend of diferent amounts of 3,4-ethylenedioxythiophene (EDOT) dissolved in polyethylene glycol diacrylate (PEG-DA) was stirred for 30 min and degassed in a $1 \times 1 0 ^ { - 2 }$ mbar vacuum chamber for 1 h.
--[p211]-- Organic Composites for Electrically Conductive Microstructures
Electrically conductive 3D microstructures have many applications, including ion-exchange media, chemical sensors, organic photovoltaics, MEMS, microactuators, and lab-on-a-chip devices. A microstructure was fabricated in a polymer composite containing single-wall carbon nanotubes (SWCNTs) at 0.01 wt% via MPL at $\lambda = 8 0 0 \mathrm { n m }$ [52].
--[p212]-- The free-radical photoinitiator 2-hydroxy-4-(2<sup>′</sup>-hydroxyethoxy)-2-methyl-propiophenone (5 wt%) was added to diferent concentrations of the PEG-DA/EDOT blend, syringed through a 0.2-μm pore filter, and degassed for 5 min at $1 \times 1 0 ^ { - 2 }$ mbar. The PEG-DA/EDOT blend was then used to fabricate microstructures by MPL.
--[p211]-- Aggregated SWCNTs linearly absorb at 800 nm and compete with the photoinitiator for the absorption of photons. This competition afects the MPL threshold, causing portions of the fabricated structure to shrink and appear distorted.
--[p212]-- The free-standing microstructures were subjected to oxidative polymerization in a bath of iron(III)chloride hexahydrate melt, which converted EDOT to poly-3,4-ethylenedioxythiophene (PEDOT) and increased electrical conductivity. The conductivity was found to be directly limited by the concentration of the EDOT and reached a maximum of $0 . 0 4 \mathrm { S c m } ^ { - 1 }$ at 17 vol% EDOT.
--[p211]-- To limit excess absorption, avoid damage to the SWCNTs, and improve structural integrity, the wavelength for MPL was shifted to $\lambda = 7 8 0 \mathrm { n m }$ , which corresponds to a valley between absorption peaks of the SWCNTs while still being suitable for two-photon polymerization.
--[p211]-- With this adjustment, robust 3D structures, including nanowires with lateral spatial resolutions as low as 200 nm, were fabricated in the composite, and the SWCNTs were observed to be uniformly embedded throughout the structure [52]. The SWCNTs were also found to be partially aligned within the nanowires, and it was suggested that this alignment could be exploited to create devices with improved mechanical and electrical properties.

===== [p7-8] Rapid Laser Optical Printing in 3D at a Nanoscale > Introduction (9块) =====
--[p7]-- Now it is emerging as the most precise and true 3D printing technology in both scientific and industrial fields [9–12]. In order to achieve the resolution of structuring required for PhC structures operational at the visible spectral range, the feature sizes of 3D structures should be smaller than ∼100 nm in all cross sections.
--[p7]-- The photosensitization and initiation of nonlinear photopolymerization are fundamentally diferent from the one-photon processes in the case of ultrashort laser pulses at longer wavelengths. The excitation of the electronic subsystem occurs faster than the absorption of energy into ionic subsystem proceeding via electron–ion equilibration, recombination, and thermal difusion in the case of ultrashort sub-picosecond laser pulses.
--[p8]-- Since thermal processes are very eficient in chemical modifications of materials, by tuning photoexcitation with controlled thermal activation, the processing of materials is done by acquiring new functionalities when femtosecond laser pulses with repetition rates of tens of megahertz are used [17–24].
In this chapter, we demonstrate how optical and thermal resist exposure protocols at high scanning speeds can be used to ensure high resolution as well as a high throughput of fabrication.
--[p8]-- Discussion is focused on 3D microfabrication using two of the most popular resists, SU8 and SZ2080. We show that avalanche ionization plays an important part in photopolymerization at tight focusing conditions even though a seeding stage of bond-breaking and radical generation might occur via a nonlinear two-photon absorption (TPA). The role of photoinitiators in the achievable resolution in 3D structuring is revealed based on two-photon nonlinearities [25].
--[p7]-- The surface quality has to be even higher in terms of surface roughness, and in some applications the volume fraction of polymer has to be only 30% in a 3D PhC, which are very demanding requirements [13–15].
--[p8]-- In resists photosensitized by TPA, there is a wider processing window when 3D structures of high quality and fidelity can be prototyped. Thermal control of conditions at the focus are of paramount importance and are explicitly discussed.
--[p7]-- The most popular materials in the case of 3D laser photopolymerization are acrylate- and epoxy-based resins, which were developed decades ago before the era of tabletop femtosecond lasers for one-photon stereolithography. The photopolymers are photosensitized for the wavelength of an eximer laser emitting at 308 nm or the i-line of a Hg light source at 360 nm.
--[p7]-- Introduction
Three-dimensional (3D) laser structuring ofmaterials is widely used in photopolymer prototyping applications including micro-optical elements, parts of optically actuated micromachines in microfluidics, scafolds for cell growth, templates for plasmonic and metamaterials, and photonic crystals (PhCs) [1–7]. The technique originated from nonlinear microscopy, providing 3D confined recording inside UV-sensitive polymers [8].
--[p7]-- For this aim, the optical means of light beam delivery as well as the material’s response should be precisely engineered to control the resolution, but without compromising the fabrication throughput in cubic micrometers per hour (in three dimensions) or square micrometers per hour(in two dimensions). The mask writing by electron-beam lithography (EBL) has a throughput of $\sim 1 0 ^ { 5 } \mu \mathrm { m } ^ { 2 } \mathrm { h } ^ { - 1 }$ for a current 22-nm node in microelectronics [16].

===== [p153-154] Hybrid Materials for Multiphoton Polymerization > Introduction (9块) =====
--[p153]-- Indeed, it is the material properties that ineluctably and crucially determine the success of the printing process, the resolution of the laser-fabricated structures, and the potentiality of their bulk and surface functionalization. The majority of the applications involve the use of commercially available photoresists. There have been examples of both negative and positive photoresists used [1, 3, 9], with the final fabricated structure telling them apart.
--[p154]-- Here, we describe the recent developments in the technology of hybrid materials for MPP microfabrication, and the recent progress in the surface and bulk functionalization of the 3D fabricated structures; also, we discuss the applications of the technology.
--[p154]-- In parallel, they are characterized by high optical quality, as well as post-processing chemical and electrochemical inertness. These features, along with their good mechanical and chemical stability, have set of the organic–inorganic hybrid materials’ involvement in photonics, metamaterials, microelectromechanical systems (MEMS), microfluidics, and biomedical applications.
--[p153]-- That is to say, it is the reverse structure that is written on the sample. In any case, negative photopolymers are the most commonly used. However, besides the good structural results that these photosensitive ensure, they exhibit limited flexibility concerning the bulk and surface functionalization ofthe laser-fabricated structures.
--[p153]-- In the case of negative photoresists, the two-photon exposure results in the cross-linking of the polymeric chains, making the exposed area insoluble in the solvent used to remove the nonpolymerized material. In this way, the structures to be fabricated in the sample are directly written. On the other hand, in the case of positive photoresists, the two-photon absorption leads to the cleavage of the polymeric chains, which are no longer difused by the development solvent.
--[p153]-- Introduction
Multiphoton lithography (MPL) based on multiphoton polymerization (MPP) of photosensitive materials is a printing technique that enables the fabrication of three-dimensional (3D) structures with subwavelength resolution [1–5]. The polymerization is based on the phenomenon of multiphoton absorption.
--[p153]-- When an ultrafast laser beam is tightly focused into the volume of a transparent photosensitive material, the polymerization process can be initiated by nonlinear absorption within the focal volume. By moving the laser focus three dimensionally through the material, 3D structures can be fabricated.
--[p153]-- The technique has been implemented with a variety of acrylate and epoxy materials, and several components and devices have been fabricated such as photonic crystal templates [6], mechanical devices [7], and microscopic models [8].
A decisive parameter for the accurate fabrication of structures by MPL, besides the irradiation one, is the choice of the structuring material.
--[p154]-- In order to overcome this obstacle, a great deal of research has recently focused on the development of organic–inorganic hybrid materials synthesized via the sol–gel route tailor-made for MPP [10]. Sol–gel technology provides a very powerful tool for the development of hybrid compounds [11] relying on the incorporation of inorganic compounds into organic molecules. These hybrid materials benefit from straightforward preparation, modification, and processing.

===== [p143-148] Photoinitiators for Multiphoton Absorption Lithography > Application of Photoinitiators in Multiphoton Absorption Lithography (23块) =====
--[p148]-- intramolecular charge transfer (ICT) conjugate system was introduced for high � and a π-methoxybenzyl group for radical quenching. The authors investigated the optical properties and photoinitiating properties of BNMBC in comparison to its analogous 3,6-bis-[2-(4-nitrophenyl)-ethynyl]-9-benzyl-carbazole (BNBC, Figure 6.28), which was reported as a highly eficient TPP photoinitiator [7].
--[p143]-- The asymmetric shrinkage of the voxel in the axial and lateral directions plays a significant role in obtaining features of low AR, which is a critical requirement for construction of micro/nanodevices by TPP. In TPP, the center of the focus was set on the surface of the substrate, which led to the cured portion of the resin fixed on the substrate surface to avoid shrinkage in the lateral direction.
--[p145]-- [59] have designed photomonomers by considering the induction of the quenching moiety in the molecular design. The material used for the fabrication of the 3D structures is an organic–inorganic hybrid, while the quencher is a photopolymerizable amine-based monomer that is bound to the polymer backbone upon fabrication of the structures [60]. It was produced by the addition of zirconium propoxide (Figure 6.25a, ZPO, 70% in propanol) to methacryloxypropyl trimethoxysilane (Figure 6.25b, MAPTMS).
--[p145]-- The monomer 2-(dimethylamino)ethyl methacrylate (Figure 6.25c, DMAEMA) was added as a quencher. Michler’s ketone, 4,4-bis(diethylamino) benzophenone (Figure 6.25e, BIS), was used as the photoinitiator.
--[p148]-- The density of two-photon-induced radicals was distributed depending on the intensity of the laser focused beam when the photoresist was exposed to light. --[p144]-- Recently, reducing the radical difusion region in the polymerization process has been found to be a promising route to improving the resolution of TPP. The deactivation of radicals in the polymerization of photoresists mainly comes from radical termination by the combination of radicals and quenching molecules. TPP regions were well confined by the employment of the radical quenchers to a resin SCR500 [57, 58].
--[p148]-- In the normal photoinitiating system such as that used in BNBC, the radicals were terminated by their combination during the chain-growth reactions after some average distance of radical difusion. Once the radical quencher PhOMe was independently dispersed in the photoresist with BNBC, the radical difusion distances would be limited as a result of the deactivation of radicals by quenchers, resulting in the resolution improvement of TPIP.
--[p147]-- [58] Together with the photoinitiator P-Flu-TP2 (molecular structure shown in Figure 6.27), the radical quencher DBMP was added to the photoresist for TPP. The quenching efect as a function of the amount of radical quencher was experimentally investigated. Employing the proposed method, the lateral resolution of the line patterns was improved almost to 100 nm.
If the quenching moiety is connected in the photoinitiator molecule, the resolution for TPP would be further improved.
--[p145]-- Correspondingly, the TPP technique was called “difusion-assisted highresolution direct femtosecond laser writing”. This method relies on the combination of a mobile quenching molecule with a low laser scanning speed, allowing the difusion of the quencher in the scanned area and the depletion of the multiphoton generated radicals.
