===== [p280-281] Fabricating Nano and Microstructures Made by Narrow Bandgap Semiconductors and Metals using Multiphoton Lithography > 11.1 (9块) =====
--[p280]-- Although a long-wavelength lithography laser can be used for narrow electronic bandgap semiconductors, it obviously lowers the lithography resolution. In addition, compared with wide electronic bandgap materials, narrow electronic bandgap semiconductors normally have much higher refractive indices [13]. This also challenges multiphoton lithography technique with narrow electronic bandgap semiconductors because of the refractive index mismatch.
--[p280]-- However, as conventional multiphoton lithography is based on the top-down approach, the materials suitable for multiphoton lithography are mainly wide electronic bandgap materials, which can be categorized into inorganic material such as ${ \mathrm { A s } } _ { 2 } { \mathrm { S } } _ { 3 }$ chalcogenide glass [9, 10] and $\mathrm { L i N b O } _ { 3 }$ crystal [12], and organic photoresins such as SU8 [4], Ormocer [5], and IP-L 780 (Nanoscribe, GmbH) [6] which absorb the incident laser energy via a two-photon or multiphoton process.
When multiphoton lithography is applied to fabricate 3D structures inside narrow electronic bandgap semiconductors via the top-down approach, it encounters the problem of single-photon absorption, which disables the 3D fabrication ability.
--[p281]-- Even though narrow electronic bandgap semiconductors and metals have unique optical and electrical properties that are highly desired for a wide range of applications, the fabrication of their 3D structures by multiphoton lithography technique has not been well demonstrated.
--[p281]-- This avoids the problems arising from the refractive index mismatch and single-photon absorption within the top-down fabrication approach for narrow bandgap semiconductors and also the problem of focusing laser into a metal in the top-down fabrication approach [15].
--[p280]-- Copyright 2013, AIP Publishing LLC; and from
Zongsong Gan, Yaoyu Cao and Min Gu, Direct laser writing of three-dimensional narrow bandgap and high refractive-index PbSe structures in a solution, Opt. Express 21, 11202–11208 (2013), DOI: 10.1364/OE.21.011202.
--[p280]-- This technique has enabled a wide range of applications [1–8] from photonic crystals [1] to metamaterials [2], and from micromechanics [3] to all-on-chip optical processing [7] in the recent decade.
To fabricate functional nano and microstructures with unique new features via multiphoton lithography technique, diferent materials have been developed with intrinsic properties including high refractive index, high nonlinearity [9, 10], and high mechanical strength [6, 11].
--[p280]-- 11.1
Introduction<sup>1)</sup>
Multiphoton lithography has become a widely implemented and enabling technique for the fabrication of three-dimensional (3D) complex structures at the micrometer or nanometer scale [1–6]. It has the exceptional advantages of noninvasive and maskless features based on a nonlinear process induced by a focused laser beam. A number of important advances made in optics and photonics are based on this unique technique.
--[p281]-- In this chapter, we show the fabrication of 3D structures made of lead selenide (PbSe) as a typical example of the narrow electronic bandgap semiconductors and silver as a typical example of metals by conducting multiphoton lithography technique in a solution based on the bottom-up approach [14].
--[p280]-- For metals, it is fundamentally impossible to fabricate 3D nano and microstructures in metals using multiphoton lithography technique with the top-down approach because a laser beam cannot be focused inside a metal.

===== [p173-175] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > Photopolymerization (17块) =====
--[p174]-- In either case, polymerization results in high molecular weight species that form an entangled polymeric solid $\mathbf { o r } ,$ when multifunctional monomers/macromers are in solution, a crosslinked polymer network. During this process, radicals can also terminate as opposed to initiate or propagate the reaction, which decreases the concentration of radicals in solution and afects the kinetic rate of polymerization.
--[p173]-- where $\delta _ { \mathrm { u } } \Phi _ { \mathrm { u } }$ is the two-photon cross section (generally a function of �), T is the period of the laser pulses, $\tau _ { \mathrm { p } }$ is the duration of the laser pulses, � is the wavelength of the laser light, h is Planck’s constant, c is the speed of light, $w _ { x y }$ is the lateral focal radius of the laser, $P _ { \mathrm { a v g } }$ is the average laser power, VF is a volume factor (taken as 0.63 for an axial cylinder), and [PI] is the local concentration of the photoinitiator. The prefactor of 2 assumes that two radicals are generated for each dissociated photoinitiator molecule. Similar rate expressions can be derived for other multiphoton initiation processes.
--[p174]-- In contrast, a classic, chain-growth (or addition) radical-initiated polymerization proceeds when a radical activates a functional group (such as a (meth)acrylate) on one of the surrounding monomers, which then activates and adds to another monomer in solution. This proceeds iteratively through monomer addition to form long chains of monomers from each initiation event.
--[p174]-- Once radicals are generated within the precursor solution, they begin to propagate through the functional groups of the monomers or macromers, assuming the radicals do not immediately recombine within the solvent cage. In principle, polymerization reactions proceed through a chain or step-growth mechanism [43].
--[p175]-- Understanding the kinetics ofthe photopolymerization is critical in the fabrication ofnetworks and hydrogels to be used as biomaterials, as the extent of reaction afects the final material properties and biological function. For instance, in hydrogels, the crosslinking density influences not only the mechanical properties of the gel but also the water swelling ratio, the transport of nutrients and waste, as well as the degradation kinetics [49].
--[p174]-- In a classic, radical-initiated, step-growth (or condensation) polymerization, a radical activates a functional group (such as a thiol) on one of the surrounding monomers, which then adds to another functional group (such as a vinyl) on a second monomer while the radical transfers to another functional group in solution. In this manner, dimers are formed. Dimers can then react with monomers or other dimers to form trimers or tetramers, which sequentially build up in mass in a stepwise manner.
--[p173]-- Photopolymerization
Photopolymerizations comprise a particularly attractive mechanism for the fabrication of biomaterials via MPL, as they rapidly convert a liquid precursor solution into a solid material only within the regions ofirradiation. This allows easy removal ofthe unwanted material and recovery ofthe desired microstructured biomaterial. The initial step in a radical-mediated photopolymerization reaction is photoinitiation.
--[p174]-- This chapter is focused on the formation ofcrosslinked networks and hydrogels. In these systems, $k _ { \mathrm { p } }$ and $k _ { \mathrm { t } }$ vary as a function of macromer conversion or polymerization time on account of the changes in the difusion constants of the reacting species caused by the reaction of the precursor solution [46].
--[p174]-- Termination can occur through several mechanisms, including bimolecular combination between two active chain ends or an active chain end and an initiating radical; disproportionation, in which a hydrogen atom is abstracted from one active chain by another; interaction with inhibitors such as oxygen; and chain transfer to nonpropagating species [43].
--[p174]-- The kinetic rate of polymerization, $R _ { \mathrm { p } } ,$ for chain growth polymerizations accounts for both propagation and termination as follows [43]:
$$
R _ { \mathrm { p } } = k _ { \mathrm { p } } [ M ] \left( \frac { R _ { \mathrm { i } } } { 2 k _ { \mathrm { t } } } \right) ^ { 1 / 2 }\tag{8.2}
$$
where $k _ { \mathrm { p } }$ is the rate constant for propagation, [M] is the macromer functional group concentration, and $k _ { \mathrm { t } }$ is the rate constant for termination.
--[p173]-- The rate of initiation, $R _ { \mathrm { i } } ,$ for a Type I PI undergoing two photon photoinitiation from a typical pulsed laser is given by [44, 45]
$$
R _ { \mathrm { i } } = 2 { \cdot } 1 . 1 7 \delta _ { \mathrm { u } } \Phi _ { \mathrm { u } } \frac { T } { \tau _ { \mathrm { p } } } \Bigg ( \frac { \lambda } { \pi h c w _ { x y } ^ { 2 } } \Bigg ) ^ { 2 } P _ { \mathrm { a v g } } ^ { 2 } \mathrm { V F [ \mathrm { P I } ] }\tag{8.1}
$$
--[p174]-- At early macromer conversion, the viscosity of the precursor solution increases, limiting the radical difusion rate and, thus, the rate constant for termination $k _ { \mathrm { t } } .$ . The viscosity-mediated decrease in $k _ { \mathrm { t } }$ leads to an increase in the rate of polymerization termed autoacceleration.
--[p175]-- The kinetics of photopolymerization reactions are more completely described elsewhere with extra considerations for polymer networks and hydrogels [6, 17, 47, 48]. In general, by accounting for multiphoton photoinitiation kinetics, these same principles can be applied to MPL photopolymerizations.
--[p174]-- $\mathrm { A } s$ the macromer conversion increases and a crosslinked network begins to form, the macromer functional groups also become difusion-limited, causing a decrease in $k _ { \mathrm { p } }$ and the an overall decrease in $R _ { \mathrm { p } }$ . This phenomenon is referred to as autodeceleration and, combined with a consumption of functional groups, leads to a cessation of the polymerization.
--[p173]-- Photoinitiation occurs when a photoinitiator molecule (PI) absorbs a photon of light – or in the case of MPL multiple photons nearly simultaneously – and enters an excited state. The excited-state photoinitiator (PI\*) is capable of following one of two pathways: (i) dissociate directly into one or multiple primary radicals (Type I PI), or (ii) undergo a bimolecular reaction wherein the excited PI reacts with a second molecule to generate free radicals (Type II PI) [43].
--[p173]-- As shown in Eq. (8.1), the rate of initiation can be controlled by tailoring the PI concentration, selecting a PI with a favorable two-photon cross section, tuning the wavelength of light, or adjusting the laser power. As opposed to single-photon initiation, the rate of two-photon initiation varies quadratically with the input laser power.
--[p173]-- Most single-photon photopolymerizations are hindered by light attenuation, which creates a spatial gradient in light intensity, as well as out-of-plane initiation. Owing to the limited scattering and absorption of IR light in most media, the light intensity or average power is assumed uniform over the spatial domain of the polymerization. Quantifying the rate of initiation is critical, as the rate of initiation afects the rate of polymerization and, thus, the final material properties.

===== [p352-354] Three-Dimensional Microstructures for Biological Applications > Microstructures for Drug Delivery (12块) =====
--[p353]-- The production of microneedle arrays may allow the development of novel, portable medical devices and patches.
The development of efective microneedles has to fulfill some requirements: (i) they must penetrate at least 15 μm [38] into the skin; (ii) microneedles must not fracture during skin penetration; and (iii) the medicine should difuse into the dermis blood vessels.
--[p352]-- Microstructures for Drug Delivery
In many treatments, the drug or vaccine of interest cannot be administered by oral means because it might be metabolized by the liver, kidneys, or intestines before reaching the circulatory system [35]. Therefore, in these cases intravenous administration can be used by applying hypodermic needles. However, the use of a needle cause some unwanted efects, such as pain to the patient and local damage at the injection site.
--[p353]-- The aspect ratio of microneedles was modified by changing the size ofthe structure base, which ranged from 150 to 300 μm. Another geometry modification was made by adjusting the position of the channel relative to the center of the structure. Channel diameter was also the same for all structures.
Microneedles are subjected to many kinds of forces during insertion, such as compressive forces, bending forces, and shear forces.
--[p353]-- In addition, in the production of microneedle arrays, 2PP can achieve higher microneedle density than other conventional fabrications methods. Ovsianikov et al. [40] have fabricated hollow microneedle arrays with centered and out-of-center channels with diferent geometrical aspects. Figure 14.14 shows the SEM images of these structures.
All microneedles in Figure 14.14 are 800 μm long, so they can be used for drug delivery and/or drawing blood.
--[p353]-- Also, applications using microneedles require no expertise, and hollow devices may allow for difusiondriven drug administration over an extended period. In this way, microneedles provide a unique method for transdermal or intravenous drug delivery of many kinds of pharmacologic agents with sustained or controlled administration. In addition, an array of microneedles may allow injection or extraction over a larger area and at higher rates than with a single hypodermic needle.
--[p354]-- Therefore, microneedles with higher aspect ratios and smaller tip radii are best suited for applications since they will meet with lower resistance during skin penetration [42]. However, a compromise must be made since microneedles with higher base diameter can resist high loads.
2PP allows the production of microneedles with a wide range of geometrical features, which allows the tailoring of needles depending on the application.
--[p352]-- In addition, this kind ofapplication also requires medical expertise and it is not suited for cases that demand sustained delivery of the medicine.
A good alternative for hypodermic needles is an array of microneedles [36]. By using needles with reduced dimensions, it is possible to greatly reduce both pain to the patient [37] and damage at the injection site.
--[p354]-- Ormocer microneedle arrays were able to penetrate porcine tissues without fracture, reveling that these devices might be suited for medical applications [18, 40, 43].
--[p353]-- The minimum penetration depth requirement refers to the stratum corneum layer, which contains dead cells and represents a barrier for drug transportation through the skin. Another requirement is that the needles be made of a biocompatible material.
2PP fabrication can be applied to organically modified ceramics, also known by its trade name Ormocer. This organic/inorganic hybrid material presents interesting properties and is suited for the production of microneedles.
--[p354]-- During these tests, the tip of the microneedles showed bending, which might increase the resistance to skin penetration. Also, needles with higher base diameter resisted higher load values. This result suggests that structures with larger bases have greater mechanical stability. The needles with a 20.4-μm of-center channel presented the sharpest tip and the lowest penetration resistance.
--[p353]-- During the fabrication process, the crosslinking of the organic and inorganic components yields a 3D network that prevents the phase separation ofthe material. This results in a material with good chemical and thermal stability. In addition, Ormocer has passed through biological assays, including ISO 10933-5 cytotoxicity assay, showing that this material is nontoxic and biologically inert [39].
Because of its versatility, 2PP process allows the production of microneedles with diferent designs.
--[p353]-- Microneedle arrays are less prone to fracture compared to single microneedles when subjected to shear force because the force will be distributed over a wider area [41]. Also, this kind of configuration would allow higher rates of blood drawal and drug administration.
Arrays containing needles of each type of geometry were produced and tested against polytetrafluoroethylene and cadaveric porcine adipose tissue for penetration and fracture properties [40].

===== [p326-331] Nanoreplication Printing and Nanosurface Processing > Nanosurface Structuring via Ablation (21块) =====
--[p328]-- This micrograph highlights one of the flaws with ablation: while most of the material is vaporized and leaves the sample in the form of a gas, some parts may remain on the substrate or on the microstructure that can be dificult to remove.
Lim et al. also reported ablation as a means to create a cantilever by ablating a bridge, and they described another problem of ablation: surface roughness [34].
--[p329]-- For example, a 3D microfluidic network can be very dificult and slow to create by traditional TPIP, because great care would be needed to polymerize lines that overlap suficiently to not leak in some areas but are spaced appropriately apart in other areas to create channels. However, if an entire block is polymerized first, and then a 3D channel is ablated afterward, the structure can be made much more quickly. Xiong et al.
--[p328]-- They ablated lines from thin films of the acrylate resin SCR500, and found that the surface roughness of the lines depended on both the thickness of the film and the location of the voxel relative to the surface. Thinner (300-nm-thick) films gave smoother sidewalls compared to thicker (500-nm-thick) films. They also found that if the focal point was placed at the surface of the film, the sidewalls were smoother than if it was placed at the bottom of the film.
--[p331]-- Midorikawa and coworkers have also published on ablation-enhanced electroless deposition. They have shown that multiphoton-ablated glass, either fused silica or FORTAN, will selectively grow copper only in the ablated regions. The reason for the selectivity, they explain, is surface roughening due to ablation, which then causes particles of copper to stick and become anchored to the surface. These seed particles then catalyze the growth of a thin film of copper.
--[p328]-- Typically, fine features sufer from low crosslinking density since they are made with a low exposure dosage. Small features made by ablation should be mechanically stronger, having been fabricated with more photons, and subsequently made smaller by ablation.
A subtractive technique, such as ablation, also enables some structures to be made significantly faster than additive structures.
--[p330]-- These researchers have also pioneered the technique of using multiphoton polymerization inside these FLAE channels to pattern structures such as filters and mixers out ofSU-8. They equate this fabrication method to creating a “ship-insidea-bottle” (Figure 13.13).
Another promising application of ablation is for the chemical activation of surfaces. Work by Chen et al. used the fourth harmonic of a Nd:YAG laser at 266 nm to ablate a film of polyimide (Kapton) [38].
--[p327]-- Exactly the same optical system can be used for both fabrication and ablation, but usually the laser power required is approximately 5–10 times higher for ablation than for polymerization.
Unlike multiphoton polymerization, ablation is a subtractive fabrication method. Since the process uses an ultrafast laser, it has the same spatial confinement in three dimensions as TPIP, but it can be used to remove material from a structure rather than add on to it. Why might removing material be useful?
--[p330]-- They used near-IR pulses ∼100 fs in duration with microjoule per pulse energies to ablate both fused silica and the photosensitive glass FORTAN. The photochemical reaction within the glass was multiphoton and could be localized in three dimensions. After patterning the glass, the exposed regions were wet-etched using dilute HF or KOH. Channels with arbitrary cross sections could be created, having length-to-width aspect ratios upwards of 200.
--[p331]-- The ablated ceramics were analyzed by X-ray difraction and Raman spectroscopy, and it was found that there were minor increases in the calcium aluminum borate peaks and the lanthanum borate peaks. They also found that the Raman spectrum significantly broadened, which suggested the formation of new chemical bonds. The researchers were able to create a miniaturized RLC circuit on the surface of their LTCC using their ablation technique.
--[p329]-- showed exactly that process [35], a micrograph of which is shown in Figure 13.12. For this sample, the researchers used the IP-L resin from Nanoscribe Inc. They mention that the UV–vis absorbance spectrum of polymerized IP-L showed that it did not significantly absorb 390 nm, but that it did absorb well at 260 nm. They infer from this spectrum that the ablation process must be a three-photon nonlinear process and not a two-photon process, since their laser is of 780 nm.
--[p328]-- For instance, the cantilever in Figure 13.10b collapsed during development. To overcome this problem, a modified structure was created that resembled a bridge. After development, this bridge structure did not collapse. A portion of the bridge was then ablated, leaving the intended cantilever behind (Figure 13.10c) [1].
The image in Figure 13.10c shows some remnants of the removed material on the substrate.
--[p327]-- There are several practical reasons, such as (i) the ability to overcome structural fragility
(b)
(c)
during developing, (ii) reducing feature sizes, (iii) creating patterns faster, and (iv) as a means to generate a novel surface for chemical reactions.
One issue with some microstructures is that they can deform during development due to the surface tension of the developer solvent as it dries. This surface tension can cause some fragile structures to collapse.
--[p329]-- They show in their report that the ablated voxel is approximately 200 nm in diameter. They also demonstrate that fiber Bragg gratings can be made by ablating a periodic pattern in a waveguide, but they did not optically characterize the waveguides they made.
Midorikawa and coworkers were also able to create 3D microfluidic channels using femtosecond laser-assisted etching (FLAE) [36, 37]. While this process is not ablation, the results are very similar.
--[p331]-- There is reason to believe that acrylates, epoxies, or organically modified ceramics may also have the ability to be activated by laser ablation. If so, ablation-enhanced metallization could become a new route to 3D electronic devices.
--[p331]-- They ablated low-temperature co-fired ceramics (LTCCs), which are mostly used in electronics packaging, using a Q-switched Nd:YAG laser at 1064 nm, with 150- ns pulses having 2 mJ per pulse. Ablated patterns could then be coated in copper, silver, or gold using electroless plating. They found that both ablation and annealing took place near the focal point and that annealing was more favorable for metal deposition.
--[p328]-- By simply ablating a portion of a line, a smaller line-width feature can be created (Figure 13.11). While the line sizes can be made significantly smaller this way, it should be noted that this does not prove that the resolution can be improved. Since resolution is defined by how close two lines can be to one another, making one line narrower is not equivalent to making two lines closer together. This could be an important method, however, in making small structures stronger.
--[p328]-- These surface roughness results highlight the importance of characterizing the ablation process, which has not been reported in the lithography literature. While ablation of bulk materials from their surface has been performed for years, there is a significant amount of experimental and theoretical work remaining to be done for ablation as it applies to multiphoton lithography.
The paper by Lim et al. also demonstrates a novel way to decrease feature size using ablation [34].
--[p326]-- Nanosurface Structuring via Ablation
A relatively new method of fabrication for multiphoton lithography is via 3D laser ablation. Laser ablation is the process whereby a focused laser beam is used to vaporize a target material. Ablation has been used for decades in pulsed laser deposition to create high-quality crystalline thin films of ceramic materials by removing material from a bulk sample and exposing a substrate to the resulting plume.
--[p331]-- They have used this technique to metalize the walls of microfluidic channels to create electrodes capable of manipulating Euglena cells via dielectrophoresis.
While these examples of metallization are impressive, it should be noted that they have not yet been used with conventional materials used in multiphoton lithography. This provides an opportunity for the TPIP community.
--[p326]-- As it relates to multiphoton lithography, this ablation method has only been reported by a few groups, but it is poised to become an important tool in the microfabrication arsenal.
The basic idea ofmultiphoton ablation is very simple and is shown schematically in Figure 13.10a. After creation of a master structure by TPIP, it is developed, and then exposed to the same ultrafast laser for ablation. By scanning the beam with respect to the structure, parts of the structure can be vaporized.
--[p330]-- The patterned polyimide film was then exposed to aqueous solutions of ammoniacal silver, resulting in silver seed particles that adhered only to the ablated areas. The film was then exposed to a copper electroless plating bath to deposit copper wires. The wires were approximately 100 μm wide and 1 μm thick.
Kordas et al. also used laser ablation to enhance the metallization reaction [39].

===== [p331-332] Nanoreplication Printing and Nanosurface Processing > Conclusion and Future Directions (10块) =====
--[p331]-- One possible future direction is the use of ablation in conjunction with μTM to ease mold-filling and the release of parts. Many parts made by conventional injection molding are filled from a central runner that feeds molten polymer to the mold cavities. After the parts have hardened, they are broken free from the runner. This is not possible with microscale parts because of their size, but this could be accomplished via laser ablation, with its pinpoint ability to remove material.
--[p332]-- If masters made by TPIP were used instead to create the mold, more complex particles could be mass-produced using the PRINT process.
PRINT also exemplifies an exciting prospect for nanoreplication technologies – replicating unconventional materials. It may not be possible for a drug or a semiconductor to be patterned by TPIP, but these diferent materials can be cast. This area of casting functional nanomaterials is still in its infancy, and only a few examples have been shown thus far.
--[p331]-- This new method would be an adaptation of a soft lithographic method known as microinjection molding in capillaries (MIMIC). Typically, in MIMIC the features are two dimensional, but by using TPIP, 3D structures can be produced in large numbers this way and then isolated from one another by ablation.
Another future direction that is exciting for replication is the use of fluoropolymer molds, such as those used in PRINT, which help eliminate mold flash.
--[p332]-- These are but two examples of the novel ways to cast microscopic metal, but there are numerous applications in optics, electronics, and medicine if semiconductors, ionic conductors, biopolymers, or magnetic nanostructured materials could be made by replication.
Another exciting possibility is the use of ablation to generate chemically reactive surfaces.
--[p332]-- At present, the PRINT process has been commercialized and focuses predominately on simple geometries for drug delivery. Typical shapes for particles made by the PRINT process include hexagonal nut-shaped pieces, rectangular prisms, triangular prisms, and toroids, all having dimensions between ∼1 and 10 μm. The mold is made using a proprietary process based on conventional photolithography and is made reel to reel, enabling mass production of particles.
--[p332]-- Functional materials such as biocompatible polymers, semiconductors, metals, and magnetic materials have not yet been molded from TPIP-created masters. Whitesides and coworkers have developed what they call “microsolidics”, in which traditional PDMS-based microfluidic channels are filled with low-melting alloys of In, Ga, and Sn and then solidified, leaving behind complex metal patterns [40].
--[p332]-- These patterns may be able to extend one or two orders of magnitude smaller using TPIP to create the molds. Metals can also be cast from nanoparticle-based solutions to create metallic nanoparts. The Grzybowski group showed that a solution of Au nanoparticles having “sticky” capping groups could be cast and polymerized to yield microscopic Au parts [41]. Even alloy nanoparticles could be used to create mesoscopic metallic alloy parts.
--[p331]-- Conclusion and Future Directions
While multiphoton lithography is somewhat limited in the speed with which objects can be made and the materials that can be used, many researchers have made progress to work around these issues. Using μTM, time-consuming devices can be quickly replicated even if they have quite complex geometries. MA-μTM allows the creation of daughter structures that can contain closed loops, such as rings, bridges, or coils.
--[p332]-- After WW II, the plastics industry was revolutionized by the invention of screw injection molding, because it enabled complex parts to be mass-produced inexpensively; nanoreplication by μTM may ofer an analogous advancement to nanomanufacturing. At present, TPIP is used to create devices that simply cannot be created any other way, but it is time consuming to create multiple devices. Nanoreplication ofers a solution around this problem.
These exciting solutions will continue to evolve and are sure to increase the reach of multiphoton lithography into a more widespread technology.
--[p332]-- Several examples were discussed previously, but this could become an important area of research as new materials are discovered to have this property. By combining molding and ablation, one can imagine the use of ablation of replicated parts made out of materials not suitable for TPIP. Ablation of this other material can then be used for patterning new surface chemistry.

===== [p19-20] Rapid Laser Optical Printing in 3D at a Nanoscale > Discussion (6块) =====
--[p20]-- In the field of micro-optical devices, DLW can be used to make lenses, prisms, gratings, and their hybrids with surface quality, which does not require any post-processing and can be incorporated into microfluidic chips and fabricated on waveguides [69].
--[p19]-- Mechanical constraints of fabrication speed are defined by the mass and acceleration of stages at high velocity of scan and direction changes. With the use of light-weight scanning mirrors combined with �-lenses that maintain the same focal plane height when a beam is scanned, in combination with a collinear stage scan, is a promising direction for fabrication approaching 1 m $\mathbf { s } ^ { - 1 }$ linear scan speeds and is practical for fabrication over larger areas.
--[p19]-- Heat localization in photoresists, glasses, and polymers is facilitated by lowtemperature conductivity, while precise energy delivery via light absorption can be tailored using pulse duration and spectral or spatial chirp of ultrashort laser pulses. Reaching temperatures of modification (polymerization, glass transition, etc.) that are in the range of $1 5 0 - 2 5 0 ~ ^ { \circ } C$ and localized on scale of few micrometers inside polymers is a promising fabrication tool.
--[p19]-- When photoinitiators are not used inside photopolymers such as silicones popular in biomedical applications, a wider range of applications can be opened. Feasibility to tailor thermal curing that is launched by avalanche ionization of undoped pure silicone for its 3D structuring has been demonstrated [68].
Figure 1.8 shows an example of microfabrication that produces micro-optical elements in a single processing step.
--[p19]-- Discussion
Light delivery into micrometer-sized focal spots is also possible with dry objective lenses still maintaining a high $N A = 0 . 9$ focusing. This allows higher scan speeds since there is no oil immersion, which makes the fabrication less practical. Speeds of 10 cm min<sup>−1</sup> (1.7 mm s<sup>−1</sup>) on large-scale industrial laser welding lines are easily exceeded for the fabrication on the microscale due to small inertia of the workpiece.
--[p19]-- This makes even raster scan a productive approach in 3D additive manufacturing on the    scaling [16].
Relying on linear and thermal absorption rather than optically nonlinear energy delivery DLW becomes appealing for new applications. As is shown here, thermal accumulation at high laser repetition rate and fast scan can precisely control thermal conditions and heat accumulation at the focus inside the resists.

===== [p267-270] Fabrication of Waveguides and Other Optical Elements by Multiphoton Lithography > Cationic Polymerization and Stereolithography (22块) =====
--[p270]-- were able to place a correctly aligned Mach–Zehnder interferometer structure consisting of linear waveguide motives via MPL within a PhC that was created by means of holographic lithography (Figure 10.18) [95].
Lee et al. applied a two-photon laser-scanning microscope to produce 2D PhC structures in SU-8 polymer films with circular, elliptical, rectangular, or diamondshaped unit cells within a hexagonal or square lattice [96]. An aspect ratio of 6.9 with 250 nm line width was possible.
Specific patterns, for example, lines, dots, and Y-splitters, were placed in the otherwise perfect PhCs.
--[p268]-- Gamma butyrolactone was initially employed as solvent for SU-8, but it has been recently replaced with cyclopentanone because of its improved wettability of low-surface-energy substrates and faster processing times [83].
Upon exposure to light of an appropriate wavelength, a PAG will decompose and react with the solvent or monomer to give a Bronsted acid, which catalyzes the ring-opening polymerization of epoxide monomers.
--[p269]-- In a conventional UV lithographic process and the majority of MPL patterning experiments reported up to now, the subsequent post-bake process provides the activation energy for cationic crosslinking, which is inhibited at room temperature. Recently, however, certain MPL processing parameters were found that allow suficient crosslinking without postbaking. Here, the laser itself provides suficient heat to fully crosslink the resin [89].
--[p269 [表]]-- The sample is then developed with solvents such as ethyl lactate.

--[p270]-- As further advantages, they mention a lack of oxygen inhibition and the fact that SU-8 is solid during MPL, which eliminated the need to interconnect written features immediately. As shown by Serbin et al., SU-8 scafolds for PhCs can be readily removed after infiltration and subsequent sol–gel crosslinking with titanium(IV)-isopropoxide [85]. Seet et al. presented an SU-8 double inversion process, which provided structures with feature sizes below 100 nm [92].
--[p267]-- As the absorption of these initiators generally tails out at ∼300 nm, extended conjugation derivatives (such as Cyracure UVI-6976) or sensitizers can be employed to allow polymerization at higher wavelengths. Iodonium PAGs tend to be more easily sensitized (by compounds such as anthracenes, perylenes, phenothiazines, xanthones, or thioxanthones). To reduce the brittleness of the cured material, chain-transfer agents based on polyols can be included at concentrations up to 20 wt%.
--[p270]-- Another interesting experiment was conducted by Sun et al. using MPL with SU-8 to fabricate a microlens with 100% filling factor [93]. SU-8 was used here because of its high transmission of light from the visible to the NIR, low volumetric shrinkage, and its good mechanical properties.
--[p267]-- The most commonly used monomers for photo-induced cationic polymerization are epoxies (in particular 3,4-epoxycyclohexylmethyl-3<sup>′</sup>4<sup>′</sup>-epoxycyclohexane carboxylate (ECC) and bisphenol-A diglycidylether (BADGE) in Figure 10.17), although oxetanes are also found in commercial SLA resins [81]. Photo-induced cationic polymerization is induced with PAGs, which are typically aryl-iodonium or sulfonium salts with fluorinated counterions.
--[p269]-- For one, processing of SU-8 is slightly complicated. Substrate pretreatment is followed by coating, soft baking, and exposure. Postprocessing involves postbaking, development, rinsing, drying, and hard baking. Another shortcoming is shrinkage of ∼7% depending on the thickness and area size of the structure [88]. Film thicknesses from 2 to 300 μm are achievable by controlling the spin-coating parameters (time, speed, viscosity).
--[p269]-- In order to remove the solvent and densify the film, Microchem Corp. recommends a softbake step at $9 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ prior to lithography. The spectral range for single photon exposure is given from 350 to 400 nm.
The exact mechanism of multiphoton-induced crosslinking of SU-8 has not been fully clarified yet.
--[p268]-- With eight epoxide moieties per molecule, very high crosslink density is possible, and chemical and thermal stability of the cured system are good. Additionally, high aspect ratios as well as straight sidewall profiles can be achieved.
--[p270]-- Because of the limited success with MPL using conventional PAGs, Marder et al. synthesized the cationic photoinitiator BSB-S based on a bis[(diarylamino)styryl]benzene core. The PAG is quadrupolar, with dialkyl aryl sulfonium moieties covalently attached to the donor amines [90]. The initiator was then used to fabricate a “stack-of-logs” photonic bandgap structure with the SU-8 resin. Despite its numerous advantages, SU-8 does not allow the production of PhCs with spectrally broad PBGs.
--[p269]-- Ti:sapphire (800 nm) femtosecond lasers have proven to be suitable for efective MPL structuring. Compared to other polymeric materials for MPL microfabrication, SU-8 formulations do not undergo a liquid–solid phase transition under illumination, which turns out to be advantageous for feature resolution.

--[p268]-- Another important advantage is the comparatively high glass transition temperature of the non-cross-linked resist of about $5 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ This turns out to be critical to the attainment of high-resolution features, since difusion of the PAG prior to post-exposure baking is limited [86]. Some of the more important properties of SU-8 are summarized in Table 10.3 [87].
While SU-8 has very good material properties, there are some disadvantages to the resin.
--[p269]-- As a result of pulse irradiances near the dielectric breakdown (around $1 \mathrm { T W } \mathrm { c m } ^ { - 2 } )$ and thus a highly ionized environment, the cationic photopolymerization is likely to proceed in a diferent manner from the single-photon-induced reaction. It can be explained by the absorption of the spectrally broad emission of thermally nonequilibrated electrons when high-irradiance femtosecond pulses are applied to the resist [89].

--[p267]-- Cationic Polymerization and Stereolithography
Photo-induced cationic polymerization is not as rapid as radical polymerization but has a number of advantages that make it popular in the field of SLA. Notably, cationic processes are not susceptible to oxygen inhibition, and polymerization shrinkage tends to be fairly low [80].
--[p270]-- Transmission measurement revealed that the optical absorption by the microlens array was negligible: for example, a 1.5-μm-thick polymerized SU-8 film had a transmittance of 99% in the wavelength range 360–1100 nm. The group of Chanda fabricated a three-level optical difractive element using SU-8 [94]. In another paper, Turberfield et al.
--[p270]-- This is due to its relatively low refractive index of 1.6, which is well below that of many inorganic optical materials. While this is a shortcoming for direct fabrication of optical elements, SU-8 is well suited as a template material for PhCs. Deubel et al. [91] produced PhC templates via MPL with SU-8, stating that the small refractive index change upon polymerization made it possible to structure behind already polymerized regions.
--[p268]-- The epoxy-based monomer SU-8 (Figure 10.17), originally developed as a UVcurable negative photoresist by IBM, is well established in the field of MPL microfabrication, as it is readily available and inexpensive [82]. SU-8 formulations typically contain 10 wt% triphenylsulfonium hexafluoroantimonate as PAG with a variable ratio of SU-8 resin to organic solvents. The ratio of solvent is used to control viscosity, which is a key process term for producing films of defined thickness.
--[p268]-- In a resin consisting of multifunctional epoxy monomers (ECC, BADGE, and SU-8), a network will rapidly form with propagation of the cationic chain. While ring-opening polymerization is slower than radical chain growth, termination is also slower and conversion can be significantly advanced by heating the material after lithography. In case of SU-8, the photocuring step itself may be performed at an elevated temperature [84]. SU-8 has good optical transparency and a high refractive index [85].

===== [p211-211] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Organic Composites for Lasing Microstructures (6块) =====
--[p211]-- Organic Composites for Lasing Microstructures
Organic microlasers (OMLs) have attracted much attention because of their potential for enabling functional devices, particularly lab-on-a-chip systems. The advantages of having a microlaser that uses organic and polymer materials as a gain medium include low cost, compact size, low power consumption, versatility, tunability, and easy integration [46, 49].
--[p211]-- The photopolymerized dendrimer encapsulates the laser dye, providing site isolation which limits intermolecular energy transfer and fluorescence quenching. The DCM-doped dendrimer gain medium concentration reached a maximum of 4.2 wt% compared to only 0.5 wt% without the dendrimer. The microcavity was optically characterized and found to lase at $\lambda { = } 6 1 2$ nm with a spectral bandwidth of 0.15 nm above threshold under an optical excitation intensity of 0.05 μJ per pulse [50].
--[p211]-- To overcome these problems, and to increase the concentration of the gain medium, a solid-state microcavity was fabricated using a 4-(dicyanomethylene)-2-methyl-6-(4-dimethlyaminostyril)- 4H-pyrane (DCM)-doped dendrimer incorporated into a pre-polymer material [50]. The dendrimer was made using repeated ester-branching groups and 64 end groups reacted with acrylic L-phenylalanine and FMOC-L-phenylalanine.
--[p211]-- A cuboid-shaped OML was fabricated using the 1,3,5,7,8-pentamethyl-2,6-ditert-butylpyrromethen-difluoroborate complex (PM-597) as the gain medium, doped in a SR9020 triacrylate monomer mixed with a photoinitiator without the aid of dendrimer to increase the concentration [51]. The concentration of the OML gain medium was 0.8 wt%. The OMLs were fabricated directly on the glass substrate or on top of a body-centered tetragonal (BCT) woodpile PC pedestal (Figure 9.3).
Fabricating the OML atop a PC decoupled the laser from the substrate and reduced the lasing threshold by a factor of 30.
--[p211]-- However, when using organic components as an active medium, the photochemical processes can become complex because of the potential interplay of absorption and energy transfer between the photoinitiator and chromophore [50]. Additional challenges may include poor solubility of the chromophores in the resin, clustering, self-aggregation, and fluorescence self-quenching at high concentrations [49, 50].
--[p211]-- Using a carbosilane dendrimer and fluorescein as the gain medium, the laser dye doping concentration was increased to 4.95 wt% in the photocured resin [49]. Using MPL, a distributed feedback resonator composed of cuboid arrays with suspended fibers was created. The resonator was observed to have an amplified spontaneous emission at $\lambda = 5 5 6$ nm with a threshold of 0.30 μJ per pulse.

===== [p89-95] Light Sources and Systems for Multiphoton Lithography > Laser Light Sources (28块) =====
--[p93]-- The micromechanical mirrors provide high contrast (on/of); however, the gray scales have to be simulated by spatial or temporal averaging. LC-based SLMs allow the modulation of the intensity, phase, or polarization of a light beam spatially and in time by computer-generated patterns and holograms. They provide high fill factors (liquid crystal on silicon microdisplays provide 90% fill factor), and typically refresh at a rate of some 10 Hz, and each dot can be addressed in real gray scale.
--[p92]-- The combinations of the technologies mentioned earlier allows us to take advantage of their particular strengths, for example, combining a laser scanner with three linear motors results in a system that can be very fast (laser scanner) and at the same time cover large processing areas (linear motor).
It is important to consider how the positioning system is controlled.
--[p93]-- For a quite complex geometrical object, this was demonstrated in [21]: the authors polymerized a multi-dodecahedral microstructure moving the foci of five holographic beams in three dimensions at $9 \mu \mathrm { m } s ^ { - 1 }$ scanning speed.
SLMs allow the illumination of a complete resin layer in one step (Figure 4.4). A typical setup is shown in Figure 4.5.
--[p92]-- Some basic controllers do not support coordinated motion, which means that each axis of the positioning system travels at a diferent speed to the commanded position without taking into consideration the position and speed of the other axes. In particular, it is not even guaranteed that the movement is strictly monotonic, as some controllers use iterative algorithms to approach the set position.
--[p93]-- It was demonstrated that by means of a computer-generated hologram, the laser focus (behind the objective) can be modulated into several foci for parallelization of the 2PP processing [17, 18]. For example, with 10 foci instead of a single focus, the production of periodical structures can be speed up by a factor of 10 [11, 19, 20].
--[p92]-- The obvious disadvantage of this approach is that a considerable amount of the processing time cannot be used for polymerization, as it is not possible to polymerize while the positioning system moves from one position to the next one.
This can be overcome with controllers and actuators that allow coordinated motion. Coordinated motion means that the controller is able to guide two or more axes of the positioning system synchronously so that the combined movement follows a well-defined 3D path.
--[p93]-- Subsequently, it moves along a defined path at a defined speed to the end position, triggers the laser, and decelerates (Figure 4.3). This can result in a much more eficient use of the processing time, as the dead time between the exposure oftwo dots is eliminated; instead, the material is exposed continuously, which even results in smoother surfaces.
--[p92]-- Most positioning systems for 2PP include three linear actuators to provide movement in all spatial directions. Frequently used actuators are linear motors and piezoelectric actuators; in addition, often galvanic laser scanners are used.
--[p95]-- However, there remain challenges in transferring this technique to the production of complex, arbitrary 3D objects: First and foremost, the calculation of the corresponding computer-generated holograms providing an appropriate electromagnetic field distribution inside and around the object in a nonlinear absorbing medium is a complex task.
--[p95]-- of information, there is only limited control on the field distribution inside the predefined layer and – also worth mentioning – outside of this layer. As a result, structures generated with SLM typically show lower geometrical fidelity with less accurate edges and shape in comparison with line-by-line generated structures.
--[p89]-- Laser Light Sources
The light source that is used for a MPP process strongly impacts the nonlinear process by its physical properties. Relevant properties include (but are not limited to)
• laser wavelength
• pulse energy
• pulse duration
• repetition rate
• spectral bandwidth
• beam quality and profile.
spot inside the photomaterial where polymerization can be initiated. To trigger the polymerization reaction, the threshold of the photomaterial has to be overcome.
--[p92]-- Each of these technologies has its own advantages and disadvantages: stateof-the-art piezoelectric actuators can provide sub-nanometer resolution in positioning; but at the same time they typically have a very short travel range of not more than a few hundred micrometers, limited speed of ${ \sim } 1 0 0 \mu \mathrm { m } \mathrm { s } ^ { - 1 }$ , and may require sophisticated hysteresis compensation.
--[p93]-- By this technique, arrays of hundreds and more foci can be generated, which has the potential of substantial reduction of the processing time of periodic structures. Even dynamic control of the foci is feasible, but it is limited to the refresh rate ofthe display.
--[p93]-- In general, a controller can achieve this either by relying on predefined axis parameters or by continuously comparing the set point and the actual value in real time during the movement. Some controllers, for example, piezo actuators, apply learning algorithms that use dry runs to measure the discrepancy between the set point and actual value (e.g., because of hysteresis or strong acceleration) and iteratively improve a certain movement vector.
--[p93]-- In addition to coordinated motion, a position-synchronous switching of a trigger signal that can be used to control the exposure of the photoresist is required. If a positioning system allows coordinated motion and position-synchronous triggering, the dot-by-dot exposure can be replaced by a line-by-line exposure. A system based on line-by-line exposure accelerates the axis system to the desired polymerization speed and triggers the laser when the required start position is reached.
--[p92]-- Galvanometer laser scanners can be even faster than linear motors or piezoelectric actuators, but the processing area is limited to the field of view of the objective (e.g., about $1 0 0 \times 1 0 0 \mu \mathrm { m } ^ { 2 }$ for a 100× NA 1.4 objective), and the efective speed of a laser scanner is reduced at the magnification factor of the objective.
--[p95]-- For certain geometries this can be overcome, for example, if a photoresist layer is prepared that is thin in comparison with the length of the focal region by spin coating a sample. In this way, the SLM technique can be used to generate 2D structures with very well-defined height in a single step. For a layer-by-layer generation of complex 3D structures, careful control of the field distribution in the focal region is beneficial.
--[p92]-- Nonetheless, such positioning systems can be used (and they are used) to build complex 3D structures realizing a dot-by-dot exposure of the photoresin. A system based on dot-by-dot exposure executes a sequence that commands the axes at the set position and waits until the set positions are reached and the axes are settled. Then, the laser is triggered to expose the resin for a defined time (Figure 4.2).
--[p93]-- It was demonstrated that with imaging devices such as DOEs, fly’s-eye type lenses, digital mirror devices (DMDs), and liquid crystal (LC)-based SLMs, further optimization steps in 3D polymerization can be done. Instead of single line-byline processing, a parallelization of the 2PP processing can be achieved with such devices.
--[p93]-- Fly’s eye, microlens arrays, and DOEs provide static patterns of10 or even of 100 spots, which can be used to modulate a single laser beam into a pattern of multiple beams, which allows the parallelization of the generation of periodic structures [13, 14]. DMDs [15, 16] are arrays of tiny micromechanical mirrors with a high fill factor (>90%), which can be switched between two tilt states at refresh rates of10 kHz and more.
--[p93]-- However, with increasing processing speed v, longer acceleration paths are required, $s = \nu ^ { 2 } / ( 2 a )$ , which again creates dead times of t = v/a before and after the processing of each line. Consequently, acceleration and speed should be well balanced during fast processing. Most currently used 2PP systems operate on the basis of this approach of coordinated motion.
--[p92]-- The size of the polymerized volume pixel (voxel) is controlled by tuning the focal intensity with respect to the polymerization threshold, which allows the realization of voxels at a size well below the optical difraction limit of the objective.
Complex three-dimensional (3D) structures are generated by moving this focus within the resin to the desired positions. The way to realize this is influenced by the positioning system or the beam deflection unit used.
--[p92]-- After that, the generation of a volume dot is finished, and the sequence is repeated with the subsequent positions [10–12]. The advantage of this approach is that each voxel is polymerized at well-controlled parameters and it can be realized with nearly any type of positioning system.
--[p94]-- Laser light does not only exist inside the desired layer, but also the light field evolves while passing through the focus. As a phase hologram on a display with finite resolution can carry only a finite amount
the SLM, an intermediate image is created between lens 1 and lens 2 for control, selection, and demagnification, and the microscope objective generates the final image for processing on a 3D stage.
--[p94]-- To preserve as much of the laser power as possible, this is usually done by calculating a phase hologram for the entire structure inside one layer. During exposure, the hologram is transformed by the objective into the image of a full layer, which is polymerized in a single step. However, it should be mentioned that some problems arise in application of this technology for a layer-by-layer exposition.
--[p95]-- It is remarkable that an SLM can be even used to shape a laser beam three dimensionally. It would be, of course, the dream of technology developers to build an arbitrary 3D object in a single exposure. For certain geometries and structures, this has already been demonstrated; for example, cylindrical and helical microstructures could be designed and generated by SLM holographically shaped 3D beams [9, 22, 23].
--[p95]-- In addition, the amount of information that can be encoded into a hologram is limited by the SLM display, and, finally, a large polymerization volume requires a correspondingly high total laser power.
--[p92]-- Linear motors combine high speed (more than several centimeters per second) and acceleration with long travel ranges (even travel ranges of meters are available), but more work needs to be done to achieve nanometer precision.

===== [p338-344] Three-Dimensional Microstructures for Biological Applications > 3D Structures for Cells Studies (33块) =====
--[p343]-- However, they observed evidence of cell proliferation in the other niches (Figure 14.5a,b). Nuclei of dividing cells co-express Ki-67 and DAPI and appear pink in the merged image.
The authors found no significant diference in the average cell density between short and tall niches (Figure 14.5c).
--[p338]-- Human fibrosarcoma cells (cell line HT1080) were labeled with greenfluorescent protein (GFP) by GFP plasmid transfection in order to enable 3D cell monitoring on the microscafolds by confocal fluorescence microscopy. The labeled cells were seeded on the scafolds (cell density 500 000 cells per ml), and cultured for a period of 24 h prior to starting the imaging procedure.
--[p342]-- Furthermore, fluorescent compounds facilitate confocal observation. Figure 14.5 shows a summary of the results presented in the work of Raimondi et al. [10], in which nuclei were labeled with DAPI (blue); actin filaments are labeled with phalloidin FITC (green), and dividing cells are visualized by anti-Ki67 Cy3 (red).
Raimondi et al. considered the niche with the smallest 10 μm pore size unviable because they had incompatible dimensions for cell development.
--[p338]-- A representative bright-field image of cells, obtained 5 h after seeding, is presented in Figure 14.2a [9], for a microstructure with a pore size of 52 μm. It is interesting to point out that, in the beginning, the cells were situated only on the cover slip, but they started to move in three dimensions a few hours after seeding, as illustrated in Figure 14.2a.

--[p340]-- PCL is obtained by ring-opening polymerization of �-caprolactone and has appealing features for biological application, including high degradability under typical physiological conditions (e.g., human plasma). The chemical degradability of PCL occurs by hydrolysis of ester groups, which makes this biomaterial suitable for applications in medical implants [14, 15].
--[p344]-- [19], 3D scafolds were fabricated with AKRE37 and SR2080 photopolymers, which are recognized materials for 3D micro and nanostructuring. In their work, typical parameters for fabrication, such as the average laser power and sample scanning speed, were determined, where they tested the scafold’s biocompatibility using adult rabbit myogenic stem cells.
--[p344]-- Furthermore, a cell growth comparison was performed in scafolds made with Ormocer and PEG-DA (Poly(ethylene glycol) diacrylate), which are biocompatible materials commonly used as the reference photopolymers for determining biocompatibility for cell growth [20, 21]. The scafolds produced had a truncated octahedral form and exhibited high mechanical strength.
--[p340]-- These results clearly showed the possibility of using 2PP to develop 3D scafolds with controlled geometrical parameters that could be employed to either monitor or control cellular growth and adhesion, for instance. Aside from the obtained results, the relevance of this investigation [9] reside on the method described (fabrication and analysis technique), indicating it as a viable approach for cell migration studies in 3D environments.
--[p342]-- Diferent height and pore dimensions (regions where bacteria or cells can enter the microenvironment) are interesting factors to explore in microenvironment fabrication.
For instance, Raimondi and coauthors [10] evaluated the biocompatibility, cell migration, and adhesion ofcells in microenvironments, or niches, developed using diferent heights (20 and $8 0 - 1 0 0 \mu \mathrm { m } )$ and lattice pore dimensions $( 1 0 , 2 0 , 3 0 \mu \mathrm { m } ,$ and graded).
--[p341]-- These structures might be interlocked in a lego-like manner to be used as single or stackable tissue engineering scafolds. Figure 14.4 shows a fluorescence image of neuroblast-like cells on one of these samples.
The bright green shape in Figure 14.4b,d is one of the pillars in the scafold. The pillars are 20 μm high and have a diameter of 75 μm. In these structures, the incubated cells tend to be oriented along the pillar walls.
--[p338]-- 2 \mathrm { d } , \mathrm { e } ,$ for the $5 2 \mathrm { - } \mu \mathrm { m }$ pore size scafold the cellular distribution in the central region of scafold is a little more uniform than for the 25-μm pore size scafold, since the cells can migrate more easily because of appropriate pore size. For the 12-μm scafold (Figure 14.2f), the cells remain mostly in the outer part of the structure because the pore size is smaller than the size of a single cell, which is estimated as ∼15 μm.
--[p341]-- All the neuroblast-like cells were found alive after 48 h of incubation and their numbers increased with time, indicating that the scafolds do not impair cell development. Two-photon polymerization of 3D interlocking scafolds provides a useful method for engineering of multilayered tissues.
The geometric shape of microstructures employed for biological applications is a very interesting feature to be considered before starting the fabrication process.
--[p340]-- In this figure, a structure based on triblock copolymer poly(-caprolactone-co-trimethylenecarbonate)-bpoly(ethyleneglycol)-b-poly(-caprolactone-co-trimethylenecarbonate) consisting of squares and horizontal/vertical lines that work as cell dividers is shown. This structure was fabricated via 2PP, using a power of 30 mW and a scanning speed of 50 m s<sup>1</sup>.
--[p341]-- After 48 h of incubation, fluorescence images revealed that all cells remained alive. The authors observed an increase in the number of neuroblast-like cells over time. The cell growth rates on polystyrene or Ormocer surfaces presented no noticeable diference. These results led to the conclusion that Ormocer materials do not significantly impair cell growth or viability.
Three-dimensional scafolds in the form ofarrays ofcylindrical pillars were produced on both sides of an Ormocer surface.
--[p339]-- By using image analysis, the authors [9] have tracked cell movement by analyzing images acquired for a period of 8 h at intervals of 10 min. The results demonstrated that, although the lateral speed and persistence ofthe cells in the x–y plane of the 3D structure were the same as the ones obtained in a 2D substrate, the overall mean speed was higher in the 3D scafold.
--[p338]-- Although relatively simple, the advantage of this scafold is that it allows cell migration as well as flow of the culture medium throughout the entire structure. Prior to use in cell studies, the microstructures were carefully rinsed in methanol to eliminate the toxic resin that was not polymerized during the fabrication process. Additionally, the authors carried out experiments to evaluate the mechanical properties of the scafolds and their biocompatibility for cell growth [9].
--[p340]-- Although the authors observed distortion of the structure due to polymer shrinkage during photopolymerization, the shape and porosity was maintained. Further, cytotoxicity tests revealed that the biodegradable polymer did not afect cell proliferation, which is a crucial feature in tissue engineering and biomedical scafolds applications.
Three-dimensional microstructures fabricated by 2PP using acrylated poly(ethylene glycol) have also been demonstrated [17].
--[p338]-- In their work, authors were able to monitor cell movement by assigning it spatial coordinates $( x , y , z )$ as a function of time using the intensity ofthe image obtained from the confocal microscope. Cell migration was then analyzed using three parameters; (i) the average speed, (ii) probability of movement, and (iii) motion persistence.
--[p341]-- Doraiswamy and coworkers [18] used organically modified ceramic (Ormocer) materials for fabricating 3D microstructured scafolds for tissue engineering because oftheir biocompatibility and good mechanical properties. These scafolds were produced on glass substrates by means of 2PP.
In order to determine cell growth viability on Ormocer, live/dead assays of B35 neuroblast-like cells were performed.
--[p338]-- 3D Structures for Cells Studies
Standard approaches to study cell behavior in tailored environments are usually carried out on two-dimensional (2D) patterned substrates [12, 13] fabricated using well-known methods such as soft lithography and photolithography. The use of 2PP as a tool to fabricate scafolds, however, has allowed the study of cell behavior in controlled 3D microenvironments, which has led to important advances to this area.
--[p341]-- The authors fabricated tubular scafolds having a hexagonal outer shape and hollow hexagonal area in the central part, and seeded cells into these structures by using the laser-induced forward transfer (LIFT) technique. This technique uses a propulsive force, arising from laser-induced shock wave, to propel individual cells or groups of cells from a donor substrate toward the receiver substrate.
--[p341]-- Prior to the test, the Ormocer material was placed in a plastic Petri dish sterilized by exposure to ultraviolet-B light. The cells were then incubated in these Petri dishes. Living cells were dyed to present green fluorescence, while dead cells would emit red fluorescence. The cells were imaged after diferent time intervals aiming at establishing a growth profile. This was made by using an inverted optical microscope and a camera to record the cells’ epifluorescence.
--[p341]-- This approach combines accurate control to fabricate structures within the size scale of the natural cell environment with precise deposition of cells on specific structure site. Such advantages are fundamental for precise 3D fabrication of scafolds for in vitro studies as well as tissue engineering applications.
--[p340]-- A further advance in the fabrication of 3D microstructures for cell studies was achieved by using biodegradable and biocompatible polymers, which are important to design improved scafolds for cellular growth platforms and tissue engineering. Biodegradable polymers of great importance are �-polycaprolactone (PCL) and its derivatives.
