===== [p175-176] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > Photoinitiator Selection (10块) =====
--[p176]-- Irgacure® 2959 [2-hydroxy-1-(4-(2-hydroxyethoxy)phenyl)- 2-methylpropan-1-one] has perhaps the most widespread use, and decomposes under UV light (� = 365 nm) to form both an acetone ketyl and a substituted benzoyl radical (Figure 8.2a) [51]. Though these active radicals initiate chain polymerization rapidly in aqueous systems, the parent PI sufers from poor water solubility (∼1 wt%, ∼50 mM).
--[p175]-- Photoinitiator Selection
PIs are organic compounds capable of converting absorbed light into active chemical species that can initiate polymerization [50]. Depending on the chemical makeup of the parent PI, the active species can be cationic, anionic, or radical in nature, thereby enabling all types of chain-growth and step-growth polymerizations to be controlled with light.
--[p175]-- As both cationic and anionic polymerization methods are highly moisture-sensitive, polymerizations within a biological context are performed nearly exclusively by free-radical methodologies. Radical PI systems can contain one or more components. Type I initiators operate independently and are typically based on the heterolytic cleavage of aromatic ketones or disilanes.
--[p175]-- These wavelengths usually have enough energy to initiate polymerization, but not so much as to denature proteins and damage cellular DNA (known to occur rapidly at � = 254 nm) [52, 53].
Despite the restrictive requirements posed in performing photopolymerization in the presence of live cells, several key PIs have been identified as invaluable for such tasks (Figure 8.2).
--[p176]-- This, coupled with relatively low activity at cytocompatible wavelengths of light, dramatically limits the initiation rates [54]. More recently, lithium phenyl-2,4,6-trimethylbenzoylphosphinate (LAP) has been rediscovered as a water-soluble (∼8.5 wt%, ∼300 mM) type I PI that exhibits high molar absorptivity in the range $3 0 0 < \lambda < 4 0 0$ nm (Figure 8.2b) [55, 56]. Upon exposure to UV light, LAP dissociates into a 2,4,6-trimethylbenzoyl
--[p175]-- A complete description of photoinitiators amenable to MPL is presented in Chapter 6. In general, this class of photoinitiators – those that can be activated by multiphoton excitation – can be applied to fabricate biomaterials; however, certain constraints apply. In all biomaterials, the constituent components as well as the associated degradation products or leachants must be minimally biocompatible.
--[p175]-- As most PIs absorb light through the presence of conjugated aromatic moieties, the significant hydrophobicity of these same functionalities leads to poor water solubility. If a candidate PI is able to meet these restrictive conditions, the small molecule need also be non-cytotoxic at the concentrations used to initiate photopolymerization, as well as in the range of light in which they operate. Typically, light with � 365 nm is considered appropriate for use in biological systems [51].
--[p175]-- Radical polymerization must operate under stringent physiological conditions if the interest is in performing the polymerization in the presence of live cells: body temperature (37 <sup>∘</sup>C), pH (7.4), oxygen content (5%), and bufering salts must all be accommodated. Additionally, biological conditions further necessitate an aqueous environment, dramatically limiting the selection of available PIs.
--[p175]-- In this context, this means that cells, tissue, and organisms must tolerate the photoinitiators and their cleavage products post fabrication. Additional constraints apply for hydrogels, particularly those employed for cell encapsulation.
--[p175]-- Type II PIs do not themselves cleave, but instead work in conjunction with a co-initiating species (usually non-photoactive) to induce polymerization, often through hydrogen abstraction. As greater energy is associated with bond photolysis than molecular excitation, type I PIs are largely confined to UV light sources (� < 400 nm), while type II PIs can be initiated with light extending well into the visible range (� < 600 nm).

===== [p167-169] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > Introduction (25块) =====
--[p167]-- To clarify, in this chapter photopolymerization will be used predominantly to describe the photocrosslinking of liquid precursors into solid or viscoelastic materials. Photopolymerization reactions have been leveraged within the biomaterials community to address niche design constraints, such as defined geometries (e.g., bone implants) or post-application sol–gel transition (e.g., in situ curing hydrogels) [7].
--[p168]-- This approach minimally requires a precursor solution containing multifunctional monomers and a photoinitiator, as well as an appropriate light source (a comprehensive overview of these components is provided in Section 8.4). Photopolymerized materials are then fabricated by irradiating the precursor solution with light. Molds or masks constrain the geometry, while the dosage and intensity of the light control the extent and kinetics of reaction, respectively [17].
--[p168]-- Photopolymerized biomaterials have been successfully implemented as barriers, cell delivery vehicles, tissue engineering scafolds, and drug delivery systems [11].
For many applications, it is desirable to achieve increased feature resolution within 3D objects. Highly ordered or structured materials play an essential role in many modern technologies, including microprocessors, photonics, and optics [13].
--[p167]-- Introduction
The advent of the polymer industry in the 1950s introduced a vast array of novel materials that have impacted all areas of life [1]. Today, polymers and polymerization reactions are utilized additionally in a range of biomedical applications, including as drug delivery systems [2], tissue engineering scafolds [3], and as medical devices [4].
--[p169]-- In this chapter, we will discuss, review, and provide perspective on the use of MPL as a technique for the fabrication and modification of biomaterials and hydrogels with special emphasis on the photopolymers and techniques that can be performed in or are amenable to the biological context.
--[p168]-- The high swelling degree of hydrogels makes them attractive for biomedical applications, as they mimic the mechanical properties of tissues within the body and aford facile transport or nutrients, waste, and signaling molecules [12]. In either case, the spatial and temporal control aforded by photopolymerization enables the production of highly structured materials with predefined geometries and the ability to polymerize in situ.
--[p169]-- As a result, researchers have begun to explore the use of multiphoton irradiation to facilitate nanometer- and micrometer-scale phenomena within the photopolymerization and biological context [27].
--[p167]-- Additionally, photopolymerization, which we define generally as the class of reactions that transforms solutions of low molecular weight monomers or prepolymers into high molecular weight species or crosslinked networks upon exposure to visible, ultraviolet (UV), or infrared (IR) light, afords high reaction rates under ambient conditions with relatively low energy input in both non-aqueous and aqueous solutions.
--[p167]-- The use of light as the catalyst for polymerization provides the experimenter with an unprecedented level of spatial and temporal control over the reaction. That is, the reaction proceeds only where and when the user desires, allowing for facile production of geometrically defined polymeric materials on demand.
--[p169]-- An alternative approach for rapid prototyping and micrometer-scale material fabrication is laser scanning lithography (LSL)-based photopolymerization, whereby the focal point of a laser is rastered or serially scanned through defined regions of a precursor solution [26]. Photoinitiation occurs only where the solution is illuminated by the focused laser light, which provides the necessary photons to drive polymerization.
--[p167]-- Concurrently, multiphoton lithography (MPL) techniques have been applied in photopolymerization, and the necessary tools are becoming more widely available to the biomedical community. In this chapter, we discuss, review, and provide perspective on the intersection between MPL and photopolymerization for biomaterials with a special emphasis on the chemistries, platforms, and techniques that are relevant to the biological context.
--[p168]-- Alternatively, interfacial photopolymerizations are achieved by adsorbing or grafting a photoinitiator to a biomaterial surface from which a photopolymerized surface or brush can be generated [19]. These approaches enable conformal coatings, molded materials, or in vivo implants; however, none of these approaches exploits the full 3D and spatiotemporal resolution that photoinitiation afords, as they are confined to planar patterning.
--[p169]-- Increased spatial resolution has been achieved by leveraging photolithography techniques used extensively in the microelectronics industry for the fabrication of integrated circuits [20]. Here, a photomask is used to selectively constrain illumination of the underlying precursor solution. Regions of illumination are photopolymerized into insoluble blocks, while the other nonpolymerized regions can be washed away post fabrication.
--[p168]-- Therefore, technologies used for ex vivo organ growth, three-dimensional (3D) cell culture, or as injectable cell carriers could all benefit by adopting strategies developed for high-content feature patterning in other industries.
Traditional photopolymerizations employed for biomaterial fabrication are often accessed through a photo-induced free-radical polymerization [16].
--[p168]-- Demands in these industries push for increased information content or feature density, which has fostered innovation in the materials science community to write micro- and nanoscale features into functional materials with high fidelity [14]. Photolithography and stereolithography remain the most accessible techniques for rapid and reliable fabrication at the micrometer scale for the fabrication of functional biomaterials.
--[p167]-- These biomedical applications have flourished as the available polymer chemistries and processing techniques have matured, enabling the creation of materials that can be tailored specifically for each use. In particular, advances in synthetic chemistry and photophysics have provided the tools needed to initiate and propagate polymerization reactions with light in a controlled manner [5, 6].
--[p168]-- Photopolymerizations have been instrumental in the advancement of several modern industries, including coatings and adhesives, integrated circuits, and optics, on account of their unique properties [8, 9]. Historically, the ancient Egyptians were the first to explore photopolymerization reactions as they utilized sunlight to crosslink oil-soaked linens to create environmental barriers during mummification [10].
--[p169]-- In this manner, the user can generate defined planar geometries with micrometer-scale resolution and access 3D structures with layer-by-layer fabrication [21]. However, photolithography requires high-resolution photomasks for each geometry, remains difraction-limited, and can form only simple 3D structures. Soft lithography can also be adapted to photopolymerizations [22]. Here, a master mold is formed in an elastomeric material, such as polydimethylsiloxane (PDMS), with a desired geometry.
--[p169]-- On account of scattering and attenuation of the focused laser light, LSL is typically confined to the surface of the precursor solution. In this manner, 3D objects can be generated adopting stereolithographic techniques, whereby an automated stage with xyz control moves the object through the focal point of the laser. Layer-by-layer photolithography and LSL combined with stereolithography are currently utilized in the 3D printing industry.
--[p168]-- In recent decades, photopolymerizations have been leveraged within modern medicine to fabricate biocompatible networks and hydrogels from monomers and end-functional polymers [11]. Here, networks refer to crosslinked polymeric materials that are utilized in the non-solvated state, whereas hydrogels refer to water-swollen crosslinked polymeric materials.
--[p169]-- In each of these approaches, material fabrication remains limited in spatial resolution to the micrometer scale and fails to access easily all arbitrary 3D geometries.
Specifically, developments in multiphoton technologies have made ultrafast lasers increasingly accessible to the materials science, biology, and biomaterials communities.
--[p169]-- The mold is filled with a precursor solution, which is photopolymerized to recover the desired features. This approach has been applied to microfabricate biomaterials for drug delivery [23], tissue engineering [24], and microfluidic biosensing [25]. Soft lithography is also limited in that it requires a master mold tailored for each application and involves a multistep process to achieve micrometer-scale materials.
--[p168]-- However, as our knowledge of biological systems is increasing, it is becoming clear that traditional monolithic photopolymerization reactions fail to provide materials with the information content needed for all applications. For example, the extracellular matrix (ECM), that is, the local environment that supports and instructs cell function, possesses a high degree ofcomplexity with nanometer- and micrometer-scale architecture that varies with time [15].
--[p169]-- MPL – the use of ultrafast, multiphoton lasers to execute LSL – enables the experimenter to photopolymerize materials with feature content down to the nanoscale in three dimensions with high fidelity, owing to the narrow focal region of a multiphoton laser and minimal radical generation outside of the focal spot [28].
--[p168]-- In vivo photopolymerization (or in situ photopolymerization) is achieved similarly by placing the precursor solution into the appropriate location within the body, and subsequently initiating the polymerization with light [18]. In this manner, biomaterials can be fabricated rapidly that conform to desired tissue geometries.

===== [p324-326] Nanoreplication Printing and Nanosurface Processing > Nanosurface Metallization Processing (10块) =====
--[p326]-- [3] and also by Chen et al. [32] used a slightly diferent strategy than the previously described reports by taking advantage of the inherent chemistry of the acrylic monomers used for the TPIP resin. Using a Michael reaction, ethylene diamine was covalently attached to the surface of their microstructures [3]. This amine functionality was then used as a handle to immobilize Pd(II) catalysts, which then enabled gold or copper electroless reduction.
--[p325]-- They were able to create ohmically conductive, silver-coated microstructures using this method.
Nickel-coated photonic crystals were also prepared by Misawa and coworkers using SU-8 photoresist [31]. After fabrication and development, the samples were dipped successively in aqueous solutions of Sn(II) and then Pd(II) before being put in an electroless Ni plating bath. These Ni-coated SU-8 photonic crystals showed a bandgap in the 3–5 μm range.
The work by Farrer et al.
--[p324]-- The necessity of a reducing agent, which is usually an organic molecule, also means that frequently there is an organic impurity in or on the metal that can limit its conductivity.
Another strategy that is used more often is to create 3D metal patterns by coating polymer microstructures with a metal [27]. The first metalized polymer microstructures were made by Kawata and coworkers, who used Tollen’s test reagents to electrolessly deposit silver [28, 29].
--[p326]-- This amine density is dependent on the exposure dosage during polymerization, and they were able to show gray-scale patterning of this amine functionality. They demonstrated applications for biopatterning using this gray-scale method, but they did not demonstrate gray-scale metallization. The method used by Chen et al. [32] was similar using a diamino compound, but instead of using Pd to catalyze further metal deposition they used gold nanoparticles [32].
--[p326]-- One important benefit of this reaction scheme is that the Michael reaction is significantly slower in its reaction with methacrylates compared to acrylates. Therefore, if a single microstructure is made in two steps using a methacrylate resin and an acrylic resin, then only the acrylic parts of the structure will undergo metallization (Figure 13.9). Li et al. went on to show that the density of the amine modification was a function of the amount of unpolymerized acrylate groups [33].
--[p325]-- The sample was then soaked in an aqueous solution of ammoniacal silver nitrate and glucose, which resulted in the formation of a silver mirror on the microstructures. Figure 13.8 shows a schematic of this process as well as electron micrographs of a coil microstructured before and after silver deposition.
Using an analogous strategy, Terzaki et al.
--[p325]-- added a tertiary amine-containing monomer, 2-(dimethyl amino) ethylmethacrylate (DMAEMA), to their silicon– zirconium hybrid resin to aid its metal-binding capability [30]. Following fabrication, they demonstrated two silver electroless plating techniques: (i) by forming gold nanoparticle or (ii) silver nanoparticle seeds on the microstructure.
--[p325]-- First, a polymer microstructure was made using a resin having monomers containing phenyl groups, which aided silver adhesion. The sample was fabricated on a hydrophobic glass surface. Next, they sensitized the surface with an aqueous solution of Sn(II). Since the surface was hydrophobic, the Sn(II) did not adhere to the substrate but only to the microstructure.
--[p325]-- These seeds then provided a catalytic surface for the reduction of silver using either Tollen’s reagents or a solution containing gum Arabic, citrate bufer, and silver lactate. By increasing the amount of the DMAEMA, the researchers could increase the density of the nanoparticle seeding, but at the expense of feature resolution. The best compromise of smaller features and high seeding density was 30 mol% DMAEMA.
--[p324]-- Nanosurface Metallization Processing
Direct multiphoton reduction of silver and gold has been used to create metal microstructures in two dimensions and to a lesser extent in three dimensions. One issue with direct photoreduction is that the resulting structures are granular and often quite rough. This granularity is due to the mechanism of photoreduction, which first creates nanoparticles that grow and eventually coalesce.

===== [p282-285] Fabricating Nano and Microstructures Made by Narrow Bandgap Semiconductors and Metals using Multiphoton Lithography > Photoresin Development (14块) =====
--[p282]-- Photoresin Development
As the top-down strategy encounters the problems in narrow bandgap and high refractive index materials, the bottom-up strategy is utilized to fabricate 3D PbSe nano and microstructures by two-photon lithography, the basic concept of which is as follows: First, the basic chemical components to form PbSe need to be put into the fabrication photoresin beforehand.
--[p282]-- Then, under the irradiation of the fabrication laser beam, the basic chemical components in the photoresin can produce PbSe to form the designed PbSe micro/nanostructures. To avoid any significant refractive index mismatch, the developed photoresin should have a low refractive index (Figure 11.1b). To enable 3D fabrication, the photoresin is required to have no single-photon absorption but should have two-photon absorption at the excitation wavelength.
--[p285]-- A red lamp was used as an illumination source for the CCD camera. The 3D structure was fabricated on the top surface of the bottom cover slip by controlling the movement of the scanning stage. After fabrication, the sample was annealed at $1 2 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 5 min.
--[p282]-- After dissolving of PbOA and TOPSe, the final mixture had a refractive index of ∼1.50, which served as a low refractive index platform for the two-photon lithography of PbSe without aberration compensation. ODE is a colorless solution and has no single-photon absorption at the laser wavelength range 500–800 nm. This provided the possibility of fabricating 3D PbSe structures in ODE. TOPSe in ODE was also a colorless solution. Pure PbOA was a wax-like solid.
--[p284]-- The sandwiched sample was afixed to a $2 0 0 \times 2 0 0 \times 2 0 0$ μm xyz piezoelectric scanning stage (P562, Physik Instrumente) for fabrication. A mechanical shutter, synchronized with the scanning stage, was used to control the on and of status of the laser exposure. Both the mechanical shutter and the scanning stage were controlled by a computer program with the designed structure. The fabrication process was monitored in real time by a charge-coupled device (CCD) camera.
--[p284]-- Figure 11.2b shows the absorption spectrum of the photoresin, TOPSe precursor, and PbOA precursor. The absorption spectra of both PbOA and TOPSe precursors in Figure 11.2b show an absorption band edge at 410 and 340 nm, respectively. The photoresin shows an absorption band edge at 400 nm, which confirms no absorption in the wavelength range 500–800 nm. A laser beam with a wavelength of 580 nm was used as the lithography excitation source for twophoton absorption.
--[p285]-- The cover slip without the stick of the fabricated structure was mechanically taken of with care, and then the whole bottom cover slip with the fabricated structure attached was immersed into a pure toluene solution for 5 min to remove the unsolidified photoresin and to reveal the fabricated structure. The fabricated structure was finally dried in air at room temperature.
The fabricated structures were measured with a ZEISS Supra 40 VP fieldemission scanning electron microscope (SEM).
Before measurement, the sample was coated with a gold layer of thickness 2–3 nm.
--[p284]-- A femtosecond pulsed beam of wavelength 800 nm, generated from a Ti:sapphire laser (Spectra Physics Mai Tai), was set to go through an optical parametric oscillator (Coherent Mira (OPO) optical parametric oscillator). The output beam with a wavelength of 580 nm, a repetition rate of 76 MHz, and a pulse duration of 200 fs was first expanded and then focused into the photoresin with a high-NA oil immersion objective lens (Olympus, NA = 1.4, 100×) to induce PbSe formation.
--[p283]-- After that, the mixture was kept under nitrogen gas and the temperature of the mixture was allowed to increase to $1 8 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ The mixture was kept at this temperature for 15 min in a three-necked flask under nitrogen gas. Then, the mixture was quickly cooled down to the room temperature under the protection of nitrogen gas. The mixture of PbOA in ODE was centrifuged to remove the small amount of undissolved PbO.
--[p283]-- At room temperature, the Pb mixture and the Se mixture were mixed together at a volume ratio 1 : 1, which corresponds to a mole ratio of 1 : 2. This mole ratio (rather than 1 : 1) contributed to the fast formation of PbSe with high chemical eficiency [21]. To ensure complete mixing of these two precursors, the final mixture was vigorously shaken in a sealed glass vial. The final mixture was used as the fabrication photoresin.
--[p282]-- The photoresin for fabrication was prepared by dissolving Pb in the form of lead oleate (PbOA) and Se in the form of TOPSe (Se dissolved in trioctylphosphine) in the organic solvent 1-octadecene (ODE). This organic solution had a high solubility of PbOA and TOPSe, which allowed the formation of PbSe with suficient amount of elemental Pb and Se in the local position of the excitation laser beam focal spot. The refractive index of ODE is 1.44.
--[p282]-- After dissolving PbOA and TOPSe in ODE, the final photoresin was a transparent and colorless solution (Figure 11.2a).
For synthesizing PbOA as the precursor ofthe fabrication photoresin with maximum dissolved of Pb elements, a mixture of PbO (0.506 g), oleic acid (1.280 g), and technological-grade ODE (2.300 g) was stirred and heated to $1 2 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 1 h under vacuum.
--[p283]-- The obtained solution after the centrifugation was PbOA dissolved in ODE, which was a colorless solution. The concentration of PbOA was ∼0.5 mol l<sup>−1</sup>. The TOPSe solution was prepared by dissolving an excess amount ofselenium (0.358 g) in trioctylphosphine (3.220 g). The mixture ofSe and TOP (trioctylphosphine) was centrifuged to remove the undissolved Se. The concentration of TOPSe was ${ \sim } 1 . 0 \mathrm { m o l l ^ { - 1 } }$ .
--[p284]-- Under excitation with the laser, the photoresin can absorb 580 nm light via two-photon absorption and trigger the formation of PbSe within the focal spot volume.
The sample for fabrication was prepared by sandwiching a drop of the photoresin between two cover slips separated by about 60 μm with thick sticky tapes. The sample was then used for the two-photon lithography.

===== [p45-46] 2 Characterization of 2PA Chromophores > 2.3.2.3 Degenerate Four-Wave Mixing (DFWM) (10块) =====

--[p45]-- Another standard geometry is the so-called boxcar geometry shown in Figure 2.21, where three input beams (four where homodyne detection is used to determine the phase) are arranged to give a PC/signal beam, which forms the fourth corner of the “box” [69]. --[p45]-- 2.3.2.3 Degenerate Four-Wave Mixing (DFWM)
Another excite–probe measurement is degenerate four-wave mixing (DFWM), but in this case two excitation beams are used. For short-pulse, nonlinear measurements, a standard geometry is shown in Figure 2.20, where all beams can be temporally delayed. The excitation beams are usually the forward and backward excitation beams that are counterpropagating, while the probe comes in at a small angle with respect to the forward excitation.

===== [p214-214] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Multiphoton Processing of Oxide-Based Materials (5块) =====
--[p214]-- Surface functionalization of metal oxide nanoparticles has been used to suppress phase separation and achieve high weight loading [64]. Creating a metal oxide backbone via sol–gel processing can provide access to structures with specific properties [65–67]. Sol–gel processing is relatively simple and gives the added benefit of mechanical and chemical stability, although structures made from this process also sufer from shrinkage and distortion.
Sol–gel processing has been used extensively for Si–O inorganic–organic hybrids, and is reviewed in detail elsewhere in this volume.
--[p214]-- Metal oxide nanoparticles have been homogenously dispersed in polymers, forming photo-patternable composites for MPL [57, 58]. Inorganic–organic hybrid polymers have also been employed, where the inorganic backbone contains the species needed for the targeted metal oxide, and post-MPL processing is used to transform the patterned hybrid into a metal oxide 3D form [59–61]. Metal oxides have been introduced through sol–gel processing [62].
--[p214]-- Multiphoton Processing of Oxide-Based Materials
Bulk metal oxides can have unique properties that depend sensitively upon their chemical composition and structure. These include large mechanical hardness, large and often tunable refractive index, wide optical bandgap, either high or low electrical resistivity depending upon structure and composition, photemissivity, and ferromagnetism.
--[p214]-- The simplest approach involves MPL using polymer composites containing metal oxide nanoparticles. Ideally, the weight loading of the metal oxide should be as high as possible; however, it is limited because high weight loading can interfere with the MPL process. Additionally, high weight loading increases the viscosity of the resin, making it dificult to mix and process, and possibly also resulting in phase separation [8, 63].
--[p214]-- Reports have shown that some of these properties can be obtained by blending oxides into organic materials, thereby forming composites. Metal oxide nanoparticles are known for their promising electronic properties, which can be exploited for cancer treatment, chemical and biosensing, and photovoltaics [56].
Several approaches have been demonstrated for creating metal oxide microstructures by MPL.

--[p123]-- 6.1
Introduction for Photoinitiators for Multiphoton Absorption Lithography

===== [p59-59] Modeling of Polymerization Processes > Introduction (6块) =====
--[p59]-- This method was initially proposed by Strickler and Webb [8] in 1991 for 3D optical data storage applications. In 1997, Maruo et al. [9] performed 3D microstructuring with multiphoton polymerization (MPP). In their works, the characteristic scales of the resulting structures were on the order of micrometers. Currently, the structures of sub-100-nm size are fabricated [10–13].
--[p59]-- This allows penetration of the beam into the sample for the writing of 3D structures. The photopolymerization process is initiated only in the focal spot of the beam due to multiphoton absorption (though other initiation mechanisms are possible, e.g., ionization [7]). After irradiation of the sample, the nonpolymerized resin is dissolved and washed away during the development, leaving only insoluble polymer structures.
--[p59]-- Introduction
The photopolymerization process locally initiated by multiphoton absorption of laser light provides the most versatile modern nanofabrication and nanoprototyping technology [1, 2]. Arbitrarily shaped three-dimensional (3D) solid structures can be made with submicrometer detail by means of the direct writing with a focused ultrashort-pulsed laser beam.
--[p59]-- Photopolymerization is a set of chemical reactions that transform molecules of monomer, which is the main ingredient of the polymerizable resin, into macromolecules of the polymer. The chemistry and physics of the polymerization process constitute the whole branch of modern science and technology. To study this field, the reader must refer to a number of textbooks, including the classical ones [14, 15].
--[p59]-- Modern polymerizable resins [3, 4] allow the creation of highly transparent and mechanically stable structures for applications in photonics, optoelectronics, plasmonics, as well as microelectromechanical and microfluidic systems. Biocompatibility of some polymers is important for biomedical applications [5, 6].
The structures are written with the focused beam of an ultrashort pulsed laser in the resin transparent to the laser wavelength.
--[p59]-- In this chapter, the peculiarities of the polymerization process initiated at a submicrometer scale are theoretically considered. Here, the major part is played by the nonlocal phenomena that are not of primary importance in the case of the bulk polymerization. Among those phenomena are difusion of species and fluctuations. This chapter does not aim to address particular nanofabrication systems and polymerizable chemicals.
Instead, the common traits of the submicrometer-scale polymerization process are analyzed by employing simplistic theoretical models.

===== [p209-209] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Polymer–Organic Composites (4块) =====
--[p209]-- Combinations ofmonomers have been used to improve specific properties, such as increasing the structure hardness or stability, or reducing shrinkage that can occur during polymerization or post-MPL processing [41]. Using higher performance materials avoids the need to add supports or solid frames that can compensate for anticipated shrinkage or distortion, but alters the overall structural form [42].
--[p209]-- Using composites also provides a means for enhancing diferent material, optical, and chemical properties. Monomers such as acrylates [43] and epoxides [44] have been used interchangeably or in combination to fabricate diferent components and devices. However, most of these compositional changes yield passive devices that are limited in properties and use.
--[p209]-- Polymer–Organic Composites
Most commonly, MPL is performed using an organic composite, which is a homogeneous mixture of two molecular species: (i) a photoinitiator, responsible for multiphoton excitation, and (ii) a monomer, responsible for forming a polymer chain or crosslinked polymeric matrix, where the polymerization reaction is activated by the excited photoinitiator.
--[p209]-- To enhance the properties of fabricated structures or to create active devices, additional components have been incorporated, such as fluorescent dyes, optically active chromophores, and electrically active species.

===== [p260-260] Fabrication of Waveguides and Other Optical Elements by Multiphoton Lithography > Thiol–Ene Resins (5块) =====
--[p260]-- As an example, NOA 61 resin consists of a mixture of isocyanate-derived thiols [47]. NOA 63, 72, and 81 are other resins based on polyurethane mercapto esters. Although details are proprietary, tetrahydrofurfuryl methacrylate is disclosed as a component of NOA 72 [48]. The viscosity of these resins ranges from 155 to 2000 cP, which make them amenable to many processing techniques.
--[p260]-- Another advantage is lower sensitivity to oxygen inhibition, since thiols can transfer hydrogens to peroxy radicals to give highly reactive thiyl radicals. Yield and specificity of thiol–ene chemistry are high, which are hallmarks of “click” methodology and allow polymers with a range of structural moieties. Excellent reviews have been written by Hoyle and Bowman [44–46].
UV-curable thiol–ene resins are commercially available from Norland Optical Adhesives (NOA).
--[p260 [表]]-- Their mechanical properties are given in Table 10.1.
Table 10.1 Some important mechanical properties of NOA resins for stereolithography.

Modulus (MPa) | 930-1100 | 1650 | _a) | 1380
Tensile (MPa) | 19-24 | 34 | _a) | 28
Elongation at failure (%) | 35 | 6 | _a) | 25
Shore D hardness | 85 | 90 | 75 | 90
--[p260]-- The curing of NOA resins is recommended in two steps: precure in the time range of seconds, followed by a final cure of a few minutes. This brings about full crosslinking and solvent resistance. Surface adhesion can be improved during the time after cure, when chemical bonds are formed with the substrate. The thiol–ene polymerization exhibits lower shrinkage (e.g., 1.5% for NOA 61) than other resin types. Moreover, comparatively high refractive indices of around 1.56 at 635 nm can be achieved.
--[p260]-- Thiol–Ene Resins
Another interesting class of material for MPL is thiol–ene resins. In contrast to acrylates, polymerization follows a step-growth mechanism (Figure 10.11), which has great bearing on the architecture of the polymer network and thus on material properties. In addition to having higher refractive indices (due to the presence of sulfur), thiol–ene polymers tend to have higher impact strength, which arises from a more homogeneous network structure.

===== [p180-180] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > Thiol–yne photoaddition (2块) =====
--[p180]-- The net result is the double addition of two thiols to a single alkyne to generate a dithioether. The uniqueness of this double reaction allows the facile creation of dendrimers [80, 81], the rapid generation of multivalent peptides [75], and crosslinked hydrogel materials to be formed from monomers containing just a single yne group [82, 83].
--[p180]-- Thiol–yne photoaddition
Similar to the thiol–ene reaction, the thiol–yne reaction proceeds through the addition of an initiator-generated thiyl radical to an alkene [79]. The resulting carbon-centered radical abstracts a hydrogen from an additional thiol, thereby generating a vinyl sulfide intermediate and regenerating the sulfanyl radical. The monosubstituted vinyl sulfide can undergo further reaction with an additional thiyl radical via a thiol–ene reaction.

===== [p288-288] Fabricating Nano and Microstructures Made by Narrow Bandgap Semiconductors and Metals using Multiphoton Lithography > Principle of Resolution Improvement by Increasing Photosensitivity in Photoreduction (6块) =====
--[p288]-- Meanwhile, the photosensitivity of the photoreduction solution dramatically increases with increasing the laser energy, as the nuclei increase the chemical reaction on their surface [26]. In this case, the minimum feature size of the silver dot can be realized by carefully tuning the laser energy to achieve the fabrication threshold (Figure 11.5d). The efective focal spot in terms of the size of the photoreduction area can be adjusted smaller than the focal spot of the laser beam.
--[p288]-- Principle of Resolution Improvement by Increasing Photosensitivity in Photoreduction
Photosensitivity is an intrinsic property of a material to evaluate the utilization rate oflight in fabricating a structure. Tuning photosensitivity can not only provide an energy-eficient way for the fabrication but also improve fabrication resolution.
--[p288]-- For photoreduction-based multiphoton fabrication, as the metal structure is realized through sequential three-step reactions, namely, nucleation, nuclei growth, and particle aggregation, the photoreduction photosensitivity difers from each other at diferent reaction step. Photoreduction of metal ions can show ultrahigh photosensitivity with a photon flux dependence, as the reduced metal enhances the light absorption and promotes the electron transfer from donors to acceptors.
--[p288]-- The nucleation threshold determines the minimum laser energy to initiate the photoreduction reactions, while the laser energy below the threshold cannot reduce the silver ions to generate silver nuclei. Above the nucleation threshold, the silver nuclei require more laser energy to grow and aggregate to form a silver dot, which imposes a higher fabrication threshold.
--[p288]-- As the photosensitivity of the photoreduction solution increases, the size of the efective focal spot can be further squeezed (Figure 11.5a) at the threshold level of the laser energy.
--[p288]-- Consequently, this type of the photosensitivity can provide significant improvement in the resolution during fabrication.
For reducing the silver ions and producing a silver dot, the laser energy should exceed two thresholds (Figure 11.5). One is the nucleation threshold, and the other is set up by the formation of silver dots through the aggregation of growing silver nanoparticles accompanied by an accelerated photoreduction of silver ions (Figure 11.5a,b).

===== [p344-348] Three-Dimensional Microstructures for Biological Applications > Biocompatible Materials (19块) =====
--[p344]-- This material is highly transparent, and allows three-photon polymerization using an infrared femtosecond laser source, yielding optical resolution surpassing those attained by 2PP. Biotin was subsequently attached to the hybrid structure using a photosensitive compound called photobiotin, which can be activated by UV light. The integrity of the photolytically immobilized biotin was confirmed by fluorescence microscopy, which could detect the binding of avidin to it (see Figure 14.6a).
--[p347]-- Therefore, it is not possible to produce microparticles capable of retaining translational and rotational degrees of freedom.
In order to address this problem, Shear and coworkers [34] have developed a protein-based material with high viscosity that allows the production of free-floating microstructures with arbitrary shapes.
--[p347]-- This is achieved through a controlled evaporation process of a mixture of the desired soluble protein, a photosensitizer, and DMSO (dimethyl sulfoxide) in aqueous bufer, which is used to demonstrate the production of a series of unconstrained microstructures. Incorporation of DMSO in the mixture is necessary to achieve a homogeneous material.
--[p347]-- The production of microparticles with complex three-dimensional shapes can be of great importance for characterizing and tailoring phagocytotic uptake [32, 33] in order, for example, to optimize drug delivery processes. But in most 2PP systems it is not possible to produce free microparticles. Because of the drifting of the structure during the fabrication process, which results in shape distortion, the fabricated objects must be attached to a fixed surface.
--[p344]-- SEM images of 3D structures containing chitosan are displayed in Figure 14.7, which demonstrate the high spatial resolution and structural integrity attained by the fabrication method.
To verify whether the chitosan was retained in the polymer structure, Raman scattering spectra were obtained from neat and chitosan-containing structures.
The Raman spectrum of the neat 3D structure is displayed in Figure 14.8a, which shows vibrational lines at 1637 and $1 7 3 6 \mathrm { c m } ^ { - 1 }$ .
--[p348]-- Fabrication of protein-based microstructures is important for studying drug delivery mechanisms since recent investigations have indicated that phagocytosis might greatly depend on microparticle’s size and shape. The versatility of this type of material paves the way for the production of new functional and biocompatible microdevices with improved flexibility and environmental sensitivity.
--[p347]-- Several soluble proteins can be photocrosslinked by means of 2PP, and those are capable of retaining their native molecular activity so that structures with unique chemical and biochemical properties can be fabricated. Also, conjugation of protein-based hydrogels can be used to tailor their chemical functionality, resulting in structures with various bioactive species. Therefore, the production of protein microstructures has great potential for biological and biomedical applications.
--[p344]-- Biocompatible Materials
As previously mentioned, the use of biocompatible materials is of utmost importance for fabricating structures with improved features, aiming at biological applications. In this direction, 3D microstructures containing biomolecules have been proposed by Drakakis et al. [23]. In their work, they constructed 3D microstructures using the commercial and biocompatible hybrid Ormocer.
--[p347]-- For this experiment Pseudomonas aeruginosa, a 2-μm-long and 0.5-μm-wide bacterium, was introduced into the structure. These bacteria are propelled by a single flagellum. Rotation of the paddlewheel, due to its interaction with moving bacteria, could be observed even at low bacterial density.
As a first structure to study the interactions between living microorganisms and free-floating microparticles, a trap containing an unconstrained probe was built.
--[p347]-- The trap was designed to capture and retain motile bacteria; a funneled entry-way led to a small entrance which minimized the escape of the bacteria. This funnel led to a dome, attached to a surface. Inside this compartment an 18-point star was fabricated, which would interact with trapped bacteria.
The trap worked well in accumulating bacteria inside the interaction chamber.
--[p344]-- Three-dimensional samples treated only with avidin but not with photobiotin showed no fluorescence (see Figure 14.6b), as avidin could not be retained in the porous matrices. This is due to the fact that avidin incorporation occurs only by a chemical bound to the biotin present in the structure. When irradiated by UV light, photobiotin released reactive biotin, which reacted with dangling Si–O bonds at the surface of the polymerized structure [23]. The results attained by Drakakis et al.
--[p346]-- Again, Raman measurements did not reveal any specific chitosan peak, but a monotonic increase in background signal, characteristic of the chitosan fluorescence. These data indicate that the structures fabricated retained chitosan in their bulk. Another remarkable feature is that hardness measurement showed that the microstructures containing chitosan retained the same properties as those of neat microstructures, which is important for biomedical applications [24].
--[p344]-- [23] demonstrate the possibility of using this approach to build complex 3D structures with several types of biomaterials.
Aiming at designing biocompatible scafolds, 3D microstructures containing chitosan fabricated by 2PP have been proposed by Correa et al. [24].
--[p347]-- Although 2PP is usually applied to photosensitive materials composed mostly of polymeric resins mixed with a suitable photoinitiator, 2PP can as well be used to produce solid microstructures from protein photocrosslinking [28, 29]. In this case, the final structures display high porosity, permeability, and tunable elasticity [30, 31].
--[p347]-- To demonstrate the possibility of creating functional mechanical devices, a paddlewheel inside a cylindrical chamber was produced from a bovine serum albumin (BSA) protogel. The paddlewheel had a diameter of 12 μm and spun around a vertical post that extended from the bottom to the top of the cylindrical chamber. This device had an opening at the cylinder wall, which allowed the introduction of motile bacteria.

These results demonstrate the great potential of protein-based microstructures with translational and rotational degrees of freedom to study the interaction with motile microorganisms.
--[p344]-- Chitosan presents appealing features including biodegradability and biocompatibility, and has been employed for biomedical applications, including blood coagulation, taste sensors, tissue and bone regeneration, antibacterial action, and drug delivery systems [25–27]. The authors incorporated chitosan in triacrylate-resin-based structures.
--[p344]-- Chitosan [(14)-2-amino-2-deoxy--D-glucan] is a cationic polysaccharide usually obtained by deacetylation of chitin [(14)-2-acetamide-2-deoxy--D-glucan], which is a structural polysaccharide found in shrimps and other crustaceans shells.

===== [p336-337] Three-Dimensional Microstructures for Biological Applications > Introduction (10块) =====
--[p336]-- Experimentally, fabrication is usually carried out by using either a stage scanning system or a mirror scanning system, depending on specific details of the microstructure to be designed. While in the first method the laser beam is scanned with respect to the sample, in the second one the sample is scanned relative to the laser beam focus.
--[p336]-- The desired 3D microstructure is produced by controlling the scanning mirror and the stage using a computer. Upon completion of the microstructure fabrication, the unpolymerized resin is rinsed with an appropriate solvent.
Among the several applications of 2PP, its use as a method to fabricate structures tailored for biological applications has received special attention in the last few years.
--[p336]-- An important compound for 2PP is the photoinitiator. This compound is responsible for absorbing two or more photons and starting the polymerization process. Several molecules have been employed as photoinitiator, aiming at improving yield of conversion, and therefore 2PP eficiency. Among the diferent means to start the photopolymerization, radical polymerization has been the most commonly used for fabrication via 2PP [8].
--[p336]-- Figure 14.1 illustrates a typical fabrication setup that uses a scanning mirror system; a mode-locked Ti:sapphire laser oscillator (100 fs, 800 nm, and 80 MHz) is employed to induce the two-photon absorption in the photopolymer. The laser beam is deflected by the scanning mirrors in the x–y direction and then focused into the sample with a microscope objective. The sample is placed on an axial z-stage, which is responsible for its vertical scanning.
--[p337]-- This approach allows, for instance, precise control of the architecture, material, and doping of the 3D microenvironment in which microorganisms can be cultured, thereby enabling the study of the influence of diferent parameters on their adhesion, migration, and development. Therefore, the use of 2PP has prompted an interesting approach to obtain crucial information on microorganisms’ behavior in controlled 3D environments.
--[p336]-- Furthermore, diferent molecules can be conveniently incorporated to obtain functionalized structures, giving special features to the final device. Functionalized structures allow several technological applications, such as optical data storage, reversible birefringence, optically active photonics crystals, and selective metal coating [4–7]. 2PP can be applied to a wide range of polymers, ceramics, and composites, providing high versatility to this fabrication method.
--[p337]-- In this chapter we present some recent advances on the use of 2PP for the fabrication of microstructures for biological applications in topics related to (i) scafolds and structures for cell migration investigations, (ii) structures containing biomaterials, (iii) structures for bacterial investigation, and, finally, (iv) microstructures for drug delivery.
--[p336]-- Introduction
Several methods have been proposed for the fabrication ofthree-dimensional (3D) microstructures, aiming at application in distinct fields of science and technology, from microelectronics to biology [1–3]. Among such approaches, two-photon polymerization (2PP) stands out because of its ability to produce complex 3D microstructures with high spatial resolution and virtually no geometrical constraints.
--[p336]-- In 2PP, two near-infrared photons are simultaneously absorbed by the photoinitiator in such a way that their combined energy promotes a transition to a higher excited state, initiating radical formation and, thus, the polymerization reaction. Since two-photon absorption rate scales with the square of light intensity, the photopolymer excitation, and consequently the polymerization, is confined to the vicinity of the focal region, providing high spatial selectivity to this method.
--[p337]-- For example, structures fabricated by 2PP have been elucidating aspects of cancer cell migration in engineered scaffolds [9] and used to study stem-cell homing [10]. Aspects of bacterial growth and development have also been observed in two-photon fabricated structures [11], even containing antibiotics. Additional applications include areas such as tissue engineering, stem-cell growth and diferentiation, and drug delivery.

===== [p234-234] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > 9.5.5 (1块) =====
--[p234]-- 9.5.5

===== [p78-80] Modeling of Polymerization Processes > Difusion of the Growing Chains (12块) =====
--[p79]-- Furthermore, we study how the minimum resolution length $2 L _ { \mathrm { m i n } }$ 2L (the minimum length at which two separate nanostructures can be formed, see Section 3.3) is afected by the difusion. The calculation results are shown in Figure 3.9.
It is seen that the minimum resolution distance is on the order of
$$
L _ { \mathrm { d i f f u s } } = \sqrt { \frac { D _ { 1 } } { k _ { \mathrm { p } } M _ { 0 } } }\tag{3.51}
$$
--[p78]-- \widetilde { D } ( \vec { r } ) = \frac { h \nu } { \eta } \int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } k _ { \mathrm { p } } M R _ { \mathrm { m } } d t ,\tag{3.47}
$$
which can be calculated by means of Eqs. (3.43) and (3.44). At the beginning of the polymerization process (near the zero conversion level), those equations yield [18]
$$
--[p79]-- We assume that the voxels are written by a short series of laser pulses; thus, the difusion of short radicals occurs when the conversion is close to zero and thus formula (3.47) is applicable. The actual absorbed energy density distribution is
$$

$$
--[p79]-- \overline { { D } } ^ { ( n ) } ( \vec { u } ) = \int \int _ { R ^ { 3 } } \int \int \displaylimits _ { } ^ {  } ( \vec { D } ^ { ( n ) } ( \vec { r } ) \cdot \exp \{ - j \vec { u } \vec { r } \} d \vec { r }\tag{3.49}
$$
is the Fourier image of the actual absorbed energy density distribution (3.46).
--[p78]-- Since the timescale ofthe growth up to length m and difusion is short compared to the overall polymerization time, the efect of the difusion can be calculated by substituting the actual absorbed energy density distribution

--[p78]-- The efect of the quadratic termination of small radicals is studied in [18] and is shown to deteriorate the spatial resolution.
The number density of the large radicals obey Eq. (3.1) without the inhibition term and with efective source term $k _ { \mathrm { p } } M R _ { \mathrm { m } }$
$$
\frac { \partial { R _ { \mathrm { G } } } } { \partial { t } } = k _ { \mathrm { p } } M { R _ { \mathrm { m } } } - k _ { \mathrm { t R R } } { R _ { \mathrm { G } } } ^ { 2 } .\tag{3.45}

$$
--[p78]-- $$

$$
We assume that the termination reactions can be neglected at a timescale of the growth of the radical up to the length m. This assumption makes sense when the average length of the polymer chain is much greater than m.
--[p78]-- Difusion of the Growing Chains
In the following model [18], the growing radicals are divided into two groups, small and large. The small radicals are difusing, whereas the difusion of the large radicals can be neglected. The total radical number density is
$$
R ( \vec { r } , t ) = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { m } R _ { i } ( \vec { r } , t ) + R _ { \mathrm { G } } ( \vec { r } , t ) ,\tag{3.42}
$$
--[p78]-- where $R _ { i }$ are the number densities of the difusing radicals comprising i monomers, and $R _ { \mathrm { G } }$ denotes the number density of the nondifusing radical centers. The generation and growth of the small radicals along with their difusion are described by the following equation set, assuming the absence of the inhibitor:
$$
\frac { \partial R _ { 1 } } { \partial t } = S ( \vec { r } , t ) + D _ { 1 } { \cdot } \Delta R _ { 1 } - k _ { \mathrm { p } } M R _ { 1 } ,\tag{3.43}
$$
--[p79]-- The geometrical size of macromolecules that are shorter than the persistent length scales as $d _ { \mathrm { m } } ( i ) \propto i .$ Thus, the difusion coeficients are estimated as $D _ { i } = D _ { 1 } / i$ from Eq. (3.41).
Let us consider again the problem ofthe spatial resolution oftwo polymer spherically symmetric voxels at nanodistance.
--[p80]-- regardless of the actual widths of the absorbed energy density distributions. For $w \gg L _ { \mathrm { d i f f u s } } ,$ the difusion of the growing chains can be neglected. Similar implications can be made [18] for other geometries of polymer structures, including nanorods and nanoplates at nanodistance.

===== [p312-313] Microfluidic Devices Produced by Two-Photon-Induced Polymerization > Microfluidic Devices Driven by an Optical Vortex (4块) =====
--[p313]-- To construct a viscous micropump, the microrotor was built in a U-shaped microchannel as shown in Figure 12.23. By illuminating the Laguerre–Gaussian beam with TC = 16, the microrotor was successfully rotated and a tracer particle was transported in the U-shaped microchannel. In this experiment, the rotational speed of the microrotor was 38.5 rpm, and the velocity of the tracer particle was $3 . 1 \mu \mathrm { m } \mathrm { s } ^ { - 1 }$
--[p313]-- The rotational speed was proportional to TC. In the experiments, it was found that the rotational speed attained the maximum value at $\mathrm { T C } = 1 6$ . According to the results, we designed a microrotor whose diameter was same as the diameter of the doughnut beam with $\mathrm { T C } = 1 6$ . Figure 12.22 shows the SEM images of the microrotor fabricated by two-photon microfabrication.
--[p312]-- To design the microrotor, we first examined the dependence of the rotational speed of microparticles by changing the diameter of doughnut-ring-like optical intensity distribution. The diameter of the doughnut beam could be controlled by changing the topological charge (TC) of the Laguerre–Gaussian beam with the spatial light modulator.
Figure 12.21 shows the polystyrene microparticles (diameter: 1 μm) driven by Laguerre–Gaussian beams with diferent TCs.
--[p312]-- Microfluidic Devices Driven by an Optical Vortex
As another promising way to rotate a microrotor, we have utilized an optical vortex. In our experiments, we generated a Laguerre–Gaussian beam by using a spatial light modulator [36]. Since a Laguerre–Gaussian beam has orbital angular momentum, microparticles can be rotated along the doughnut-ring-like optical intensity distribution at a focal plane [26, 27].

===== [p149-149] Photoinitiators for Multiphoton Absorption Lithography > Conclusion (2块) =====
--[p149]-- Therefore, the molecular design, the structure–property dependence, the optical property, and the two-photon absorption property of some typical photoinitiators, focused on improving the two-photon absorption cross section, have been reviewed. Generally, photoinitiators with large two-photon absorption cross section, good solubility, and high initiating eficiency are preferred for MAL. Finally, the application of photoinitiators in MAL was discussed.
The investigations of photoinitiators would provide good impetus for further development of MAL.
--[p149]-- Conclusion
In summary, the study of the photoinitiators for MAL has been briefly introduced in this section. The photoinitiators were introduced according to the classification of molecular symmetry. The two-photon absorption cross section is one of the most important factors determining the potential for the application as photoinitiators in multiphoton nanofabrication.

===== [p37-38] 2 Characterization of 2PA Chromophores > 2.3.1.5 Other Variants of the Z-Scan Method (4块) =====
--[p37]-- 2.3.1.5 Other Variants of the Z-Scan Method
Determining the physical mechanism or mechanisms responsible for the signal is seldom revealed by a single Z-scan experiment, although ultrashort femtosecond pulses can sometimes limit the significant responses to bound-electronic nonlinearities [45, 46]. Figure 2.8 shows that either one of the $Z .$ scans could have been used to extract erroneous values for a 2PA coeficient and $n _ { 2 } .$ . There have been
sjz-3-16
--[p38]-- In the particular setup given in [48], a circular flat-top beam is imaged and the light going into the originally dark area in the image plane is monitored. When it is fully aligned, the nonlinear index (and/or absorption) can be determined in a single shot.
As previously stated, the Z-scan technique is sensitive to all NLA processes and all NLR mechanisms. Unraveling the various physical processes that contribute to these nonlinearities cannot be unambiguously determined with single Z-scans.
--[p38]-- many variations of the Z-scan introduced over the past years. Many of these have shown the potential to further increase the sensitivity, for example, the EZ-scan [29], while others have introduced multiple beams, which have the potential to allow the measurement of nondegenerate nonlinearities [27, 35, 47]. However, all these “improvements” come at the cost of complicating the methodology, and the simplicity of this technique is the likely reason why it became so popular.
--[p38]-- The 4f coherent imaging method [48] and many other techniques are closely related to the Z-scan. Here, for example, a beam profile is focused through a nonlinear sample and imaged in the linear regime. Then the irradiance is increased and the change in the imaged irradiance distribution is observed. The nonlinearly induced phase distortion can be calculated and the nonlinear index determined. NLA can also be analyzed.

===== [p218-218] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Silicon Dioxide (3块) =====
--[p218]-- The precursors are sol–gel-processed, patterned by MPL, and then developed to form silicacontaining microstructures. High-refractive-index composites have been created by using phenyl-substituted polymers [66]. High-index siloxane waveguides have been patterned within a cladding of lower index material [83]. A composite based on silanol-terminated polysiloxane and methyl-substituted acryloxysilane has been developed for fabricating optical waveguides by MPL [67].
--[p218]-- Silicon Dioxide
There are numerous examples of microstructures fabricated by MPL using composites based on silicon dioxide $\left( \mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 } \right)$ nanoparticles and hybrid polymers containing a silicon–oxygen backbone [81, 82]. Silicon oxide nanoparticles blended into composites without any organic modification improves hardness and reduces shrinkage. A wide range of Si–O hybrid polymers can be created by leveraging the large library of available silane precursors.
--[p218]-- The commonly used silicon oxide hybrid polymers known as ORMOCERs and ORMOSILs are discussed in detail elsewhere in this volume.

===== [p185-185] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > Anthracene dimerization (2块) =====
--[p185]-- These sequential reactions have been used for photocontrolled drug delivery [151], biomaterial formation and degradation [152, 153], and nanoparticle manipulation [154].
--[p185]-- Anthracene dimerization
Anthracene is capable of absorbing light over a broad spectrum (250 <  < 400 nm), most frequently yielding a fluorescence emission (390 <  < 450 nm). However, when anthracene is exposed to UV light ( = 360 nm), a dimerization reaction can also occur [148, 149]. Interestingly, this reaction can be partially reversed when the dimers are illuminated with higher intensity light (260 <  < 280 nm) [150].

===== [p187-190] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > Biomaterial Modulation (12块) =====
--[p188]-- By combining a visible-light-mediated thiol–ene addition reaction with a UV-mediated oNB cleavage, DeForest and Anseth demonstrated the reversible biochemical functionalization of a polymer-based material (Figure 8.9b) [184]. This approach allowed dynamic control over cell attachment to a material surface by introducing and, subsequently, removing adhesive ligands from the hydrogel in the presence of cells.
--[p187]-- Biomaterial Modulation
In addition to the synthesis of new biopolymers and biomaterials, photochemical reactions have been utilized to modify existing platforms at user-specified points in time and space. Through appropriately developed chemistries, both the physical and chemical material makeup can be altered on demand and with resolution below that of a single mammalian cell.
--[p187]-- In perhaps the most common addition-based chemistry, radical chain polymerization of acrylate-functionalized peptides or proteins has yielded hydrogel materials that promote endothelial cell adhesion as well as enhanced vascular lumen formation through the patterned introduction of the arginylglycylaspartic acid (RGD) binding motif and growth factors such as vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) [183].
--[p187]-- A light-activated enzymatic crosslinking employing factor XIII has also been used to photopattern peptides to direct mesenchymal stem cell outgrowth into a 3D material (Figure 8.9b) [104].
Photochemistries have also enabled material biochemical alteration through the selective removal ofcues within a material. o-Nitrobenzyl (oNB) ester cleavage has been utilized for the release of RGD, thereby enhancing chondrogenesis of human mesenchymal stem cells [186].
--[p187]-- Such high spatiotemporal control over biomaterial composition is critical in capturing the dynamic heterogeneity found in native tissue [15].
Spatiotemporally defined biochemical modulation of existing materials has been performed through both additive or subtractive light-based strategies.
--[p188]-- Additionally, oNB cleavage has been used to create functional surfaces to study collective and patterned cell migration [187]. Photocleavage of coumarin derivative dimers has been utilized for a variety of drug delivery applications [141, 188, 189]. While the aforementioned techniques have been used to either introduce or remove biochemical signals, the combination of diferent photochemical reactions can enable the reversible immobilization of such cues.
--[p190]-- Through these same chemistries, photochemical alteration in network enzymatic degradability has enabled spatial control over mesenchymal stem cell diferentiation (Figure 8.10a) [193, 194]. Thiol moieties have been photocaged in order to delay additional crosslinking with reactive vinyl groups upon exposure to light. These materials have been shown to influence the migration of human mesenchymal stem cells (hMSCs) [195].
--[p187]-- An alternative reaction between thiols and alkenes has been used to guide changes in cell function within a polymer hydrogel by photopatterning thiol-containing biomolecules into ene-functionalized networks (Figure 8.9a) [184, 185]. Similarly, simultaneous patterning of several proteins can be accomplished by covalently immobilizing protein physical-binding partners (e.g., biotin, barnase) via a photocaged thiol–maleimide reaction [101].
--[p190]-- Material stifness can also be decreased through the use of photochemical reactions. The Anseth group has demonstrated extensively the bulk and local photodegradation of materials in the presence of live cells through the introduction of an oNB moiety in the backbone of hydrogel networks (Figure 8.10b) [70, 133, 134]. In a similar approach, coumarin moieties introduced into hydrogel backbones yielded visible-light-degradable materials [143].
--[p190]-- Through the usage of MPL, micrometer-scale tunability in such parameters has been demonstrated. Given the size scale of a typical mammalian cell (∼10 μm), we expect these strategies to prove useful in modulating material properties about individual cells and in better understanding biological response to dynamic extracellular signals.
--[p190]-- The ability to control the mechanical properties of a gel dynamically has been used to guide cell migration and outgrowth [70, 133, 143], to probe cytoskeletal tension within cells (Figure 8.10c) [136], to assess biomechanical memory of hMSCs [137] and in a growing list of other applications.
Photochemical reactions have demonstrated precise control over many critical aspects of the cellular microenvironment, including those both chemical and physical in nature.
--[p190]-- Photochemical reactions have also proven advantageous in controlling network biomechanics in the presence of cells. Strategies to increase culture platform compliance have focused on the photochemical increase in material crosslinking density. The radical chain photopolymerization of (meth)acrylates has been utilized to increase culture stifness in the presence of cells [26, 190].

===== [p230-231] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Structures Functionalized using NP–Polymer Composites (5块) =====
--[p231]-- The stopbands red-shifted with increasing NP concentration as a result of the increase of efective refractive index. At both low and high concentrations, the nanocomposites were transparent at visible wavelengths [127, 129, 130]. Venture et al. synthesized PbS NPs and had their surface chemistry modified in order to reduce aggregation before dispersing into the commercial photopolymer NOA63 [132].
--[p230]-- Structures Functionalized using NP–Polymer Composites
Free-standing 3D microstructures containing semiconductor NPs have been fabricated by MPL using polymeric photoresists that are preloaded with semiconductor NPs or by dispersing the NPs into a resin using the sol–gel method [127–130]. PbS and PbSe NPs have been used because of their NIR emission band, large Bohr radius, and high third-order nonlinearity [127].
--[p230]-- After photo-patterning, the unpolymerized material could be washed away, leaving the 3D nanocomposite structure [131].
Li et al. fabricated 3D woodpile PCs with a photonic bandgap in the NIR using a commercial PbSe NP-doped composite material (1550 nm PbSe Evicomposite); however, aggregation during the fabrication process was observed to contribute to structure defects [128, 131].
--[p231]-- The investigators were able to control the size of the NPs by varying either Pb:S ratio or the reaction time. PbS NPs were treated with 3-mercaptopropyl-trimethoxysilane followed by ultrasonication in order to overcome NP aggregation before dispersal in NOA63, and the resulting NP nanocomposites showed an increased refractive index. PCs made in the composite material containing modified PbS NPs in NOA63 were observed to have stopbands at telecommunication wavelengths.
--[p231]-- Three-dimensional woodpile PCs with stopbands of 80% suppression in transmission in the telecommunications wavelength region were created by Jia et al. using the hybrid organic–inorganic resin ORMOCER containing PbS NPs [127, 129, 130]. The NPs were found to be evenly dispersed in the resin, and Z-scan measurements confirmed high third-order nonlinearity of the polymerized nanocomposite film [127, 129, 130].

--[p9]-- Understanding light-matter interaction under tight focusing, high irradiance/ intensity, radiative and thermal energy transfer in sub-wavelength volumes, and photochemistry will help create a technology that either de“nes high-resolution fabrication, or is optimized for high throughput, or both at the same time.

--[p8]-- 3D (Nano)polymerization: Linear Properties
As technology evolves, the increasing complexity ofdevices requires 3D packaging in electronics, and now in fluidics, with integrated electrical, optical, and chemical functionalities. Another trend is the reduction in size/volume with an increase in the operational speed and detection sensitivity.
--[p8]-- This shows that resists that have properties comparable to those of glass (a transparent dielectric) can be formed into a 3D shape in the mesoscale spanning from 20 nm [27] to 1 mm reaching nanoscale precision when required.
Polymerization is usually achieved by the addition of photoinitiators up to a few weight percent (wt%) to absorb at the wavelength of exposure and to promote polymerization, which occurs via opening of chemical bonds (formation of radicals) and subsequent crosslinking.

===== [p236-236] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Functional Structures Fabricated in Bulk Chalcogenide Glasses (4块) =====
--[p236]-- A single-mode buried waveguide was written in GLS and GCIS for use at $\lambda \leq 1 1$ μm [144]. Spectral beam shaping and temporal pulse-width tuning were employed to optimize the optical properties of the waveguides [141].
--[p236]-- McMillen et al. and Ródenas et al. created waveguides by MPL in bulk GaLaS (GLS) and $7 5 \mathrm { G e S } _ { 2 } - 1 5 \mathrm { G a } _ { 2 } \mathrm { S } _ { 3 } - 4 \mathrm { C s I } - 2 \mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { S } _ { 3 } - 4 \mathrm { S n S }$ (GCIS) [141, 144]. GLS and GCIS were chosen because of their high transmission in the mid-infrared. GLS was chosen in particular for its high nonlinear figure of merit (FOM) and superior environmental stability [141].
--[p236]-- Arsenic trisulfide $( \mathrm { { A s } } _ { 2 } \mathrm { { S } } _ { 3 } )$ is a common ChG chosen because of its transparency in the IR (620 nm to 11 μm) and potential for optical applications [142]. Waveguide structures [143] and gratings [142] have been written in bulkand fiber-form ${ \mathrm { A s } } _ { 2 } { \mathrm { S } } _ { 3 }$ . Raman spectroscopy revealed that light exposure breaks As–S bonds and forms As–As homopolar bonds, increasing in the disorder of the glass [143].
--[p236]-- Functional Structures Fabricated in Bulk Chalcogenide Glasses
MPL using chalcogenide glasses (ChGs) has been explored as a means for creating functional nanophotonic devices, without the need for multistep post-fabrication chemical processing. ChGs have a high nonlinearity and a wide IR transmission window with direct application in sensors, photonic waveguides, acoustooptics, and IR optics [141].

===== [p184-185] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > Disulfide displacement reactions (3块) =====
--[p185]-- Two of the most promising systems to date that can be used in a biological context include the reversible dimerization of anthracene and the allyl sulfide addition–fragmentation chain-transfer reaction. Both examples are shown in Figure 8.7.
--[p184]-- Disulfide displacement reactions
Disulfide linkages, formed through the oxidation of thiols, have been demonstrated to be susceptible to radical cleavage by carbon- and phosphorus-center radicals [145, 146]. As such, disulfide linkages can be selectively displaced with radical species, including initiator fragments generated from exposure of PIs to light. Anseth et al.
--[p184]-- demonstrated that polymer-based hydrogels crosslinked with disulfide linkages could undergo complete and rapid photodegradation when exposed to suficient amount of initiator radicals [147].
Reversible Photoconjugation Though the majority of the utilized chemistries have focused on either photomediated bond formation or cleavage, great interest lies in the development of photochemical processes that can be controlled in a fully reversible manner.

===== [p250-250] Fabrication of Waveguides and Other Optical Elements by Multiphoton Lithography > Introduction (8块) =====
--[p250]-- In CMOS fabrication, negative photoresists are transient. By comparison, photo-cured polymers in photonic ICs are meant to last for many years and to retain the optical and mechanical properties required for their application. Thus, new photo-curable polymeric materials, and particularly those with tailorable refractive indices, are investigated for optical applications.
--[p250]-- Where board-to-board optical interconnects are commercially common today, chip-to-chip and on-chip optical integration are less widespread but hold great promise for the future [4]. Research in photonic integrated circuits (ICs) has been an active field for more than 30 years that has advanced borrowing from the complementary metal oxide semiconductor (CMOS) fabrication platform.
--[p250]-- Organic polymers are increasingly becoming common in hybrid photonic ICs because of their versatility in function, ease of processing, and their high thermo-optic coeficients (Δn/ΔT), which are exploited in tunable lenses and switches [6]. However, polymers tend to be not very thermally stable, which can put constraints on vapor deposition methods [7]. Lithography is a common technique for the fabrication of photonic circuits and circuit elements.
--[p250]-- Photonic ICs, which use photons instead of electrons to mediate a function, consist ofcircuit elements including lenses, attenuators, switches, and wavelength filters. Photonic circuit elements are constructed from optical materials including silicon, silica, III–V and II–VI semiconductors, and organic polymers.
--[p250]-- CMOS coating processes include chemical vapor deposition (CVD), sputtering, and spin coating, while subtractive processes include chemical etching and focused-ion-beam milling. Photolithography is pervasive in CMOS and has evolved with specialized techniques including interferometric lithography, nanoimprint lithography, nanosphere lithography, and multiphoton lithography (MPL) [5].
--[p250]-- While chemists use the term hybrid for materials with both organic and inorganic molecular moieties, the term hybrid photonics refers to circuits or circuit elements constructed from two or more classes of material (i.e., waveguides with silica cores and polymer cladding) [3].
--[p250]-- We cover some more recent advances in photo-curable polymers for photonics with emphasis on the use of MPL, as this technique ofers unique solutions for constructing microscopic optical elements [8].
--[p250]-- Introduction
The demands for higher bandwidth data transmission and faster data processing encourage scientists to develop improved techniques for building ever smaller electrical and electrooptical components [1, 2]. Photonics has for many years provided solutions in long-distance broadband data transmission and has become an increasingly viable option for shorter distances at transfer rates of 10 GHz and beyond [3].

===== [p15-15] Rapid Laser Optical Printing in 3D at a Nanoscale > 3D (Nano)polymerization: Nonlinear Properties (4块) =====
--[p15]-- It is noteworthy that $Z \mathrm { - }$ scan measurements at intensities approaching $\sim 1 \ \mathrm { T W } \mathrm { c m } ^ { - 2 }$ become invalid due to strong Raman scattering. Other methods such as transmission measurements and photoluminescence usually overestimate the actual TPA cross section [55, 56]. The intensity-dependent TPA coeficient $\beta$ and nonlinear refractive index $n _ { 2 }$ have been measured for many crystalline and amorphous materials [25, 57].
--[p15]-- The same characteristic dependencies are also valid for molecular solutions of the highly eficient TPA absorber MBAPB [58], which is similar to the photoinitiators used in resists [59, 60]. Optical nonlinearities of dyes and photoinitiators measured by femtosecond Z-scan corroborated the expected spectral dependencies established in the case of inorganic solid-state materials [55, 56].
--[p15]-- 3D (Nano)polymerization: Nonlinear Properties
The most probable optically nonlinear efects occurring at the lowest irradiance/ intensity are TPA and refraction change via the Kerr efect. The direct measurements of these nonlinear parameters are carried out by the Z-scan method [54] using ultrashort laser pulses; for example, the best fit of an open aperture $Z .$ -scan data for the Gaussian spatial distribution of a laser pulse provides a measure of the TPA cross section.
--[p15]-- Based on these premises, wavelength of excitation for the most eficient TPA was determined for 3D polymerization of SZ2080 with diferent photoinitiators [28], whiich is discussed later.

===== [p155-155] Hybrid Materials for Multiphoton Polymerization > Scheme 7.2 (1块) =====
