The third step is photopolymerization. Provided a photoinitiator is present in the gel, the photoinduced radicals will cause the polymerization of the unsaturated moieties only in the area where the radicals are produced. At this step, there is no material removal and no volume loss.
The last step is the development, in which the sample is immersed in an appropriate solvent and the area of the sample that is not photopolymerized is removed.

===== [p178-179] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > Vinyl chain polymerization (3块) =====
--[p178]-- Vinyl chain polymerization
Perhaps the most common photopolymerization reaction, namely radical chain polymerization of vinyl monomers (i.e., those that contain
Photoinitiator-mediated
(a)
(b)
(c)
(d)
carbon–carbon double bonds), occurs through a series of initiation, propagation, and termination steps [43]. During initiation, free-radicalcontaining initiator fragments attack the pair of � electrons on the vinyl monomer.
--[p179]-- In a biological context, these can include reactions with oxygen, cellular proteins, DNA, and polysaccharides, making polymerizations in the presence of living cells more challenging [67]. Monomers are most typically substituted acrylates and methacrylates, where changes in substituent structure give rise to materials with diferent properties (e.g., temperature responsiveness, water content, compliance).
These systems have been used extensively for the formation and modification of biomaterials and hydrogels [7, 26, 68].
--[p179]-- This forms a bond between the initiator species and the monomer while simultaneously generating a new free radical at the chain end. The free radical on the growing polymer chains then reacts in a similar manner with additional monomer, iteratively increasing polymer length by one repeat unit during repeating steps of propagation. Polymer growth can be halted through a variety of diferent termination events, each of which destroys the active radical species.

===== [p15-16] Rapid Laser Optical Printing in 3D at a Nanoscale > Strongest Optical Nonlinearities (8块) =====
--[p16]-- The abscissa value $x = 1$ corresponds to the one-photon (or fundamental) absorption when the photon energy is equal to the bandgap $E _ { \mathrm { g } } .$ For our analysis, we use the wavelength corresponding to the absorption maximum, $\lambda _ { \mathrm { a b s } } ^ { \mathrm { m a x } }$ , as the wavelength of the most eficient 3D structuring [28].
--[p16]-- Free carrier efects are accounted for by including Drude contribution to the dielectric permittivity, as discussed in Section 1.2.3. In terms of linear optical properties, the presence of free carriers increases absorption (imaginary part of refractive index in not 0) and reduces the light penetration depth, both of which contribute to light localization and absorption at the focus.
--[p16]-- The $\lambda _ { \mathrm { a b s } } ^ { \mathrm { m a x } } = 4 7 0$ nm was considered corresponding to $x = 1$ , while the strongest TPA was at 680–710 nm, which is close to the expected location of the most eficient TPA [25, 57] (see Figure 1.7) at 671 nm (the $x = 0 . 7$ condition).
In terms of3D laser structuring, the spectral dependence ofthe functions G and F would suggest that the irradiation wavelength for the most eficient photoinitiation of absorption should be carefully chosen.
--[p16]-- The optimal case for two-photon structuring is at a photon energy of $0 . 7 E _ { \mathrm { g } }$ when $\beta$ is the largest and $n _ { 2 }$ is the smallest. One can also recognize an expected tendency; that is, closer to the fundamental absorption $x = 1$ , the $n _ { 2 }$ values become negative, as would be expected, because of free carrier absorption. Free carriers cause defocusing and alter light beam delivery to the focus.
--[p15]-- Strongest Optical Nonlinearities
The nonlinear refractive coeficient $n _ { 2 } ~ ( n = n _ { 0 } + n _ { 2 } I )$ is given by Boyd [25, 57]
$$
n _ { 2 } [ \mathrm { c m ^ { 2 } / W } ] = K \frac { \hbar c \sqrt { E _ { \mathrm { p } } } } { 2 n _ { 0 } ^ { 2 } E _ { \mathrm { g } } ^ { 4 } } G _ { 2 } ( \hbar \omega / E _ { \mathrm { g } } ) ,\tag{1.17}
$$
where $E _ { \mathrm { p } } = 2 1$ eV is an empirical constant and $G _ { 2 }$ is the universal function plotted in Figure 1.7.
--[p16]-- This wavelength defines the central wavelength of overlap between the absorption and emission spectra of the photoinitiator or the resist matrix.
Justification of this wavelength choice for $x = 1$ condition was tested on $Z .$ -scan data ofthe dye MBAPB [60].
--[p16]-- The functions $G$ and $F$ are defined by the following polynomial expressions: $G _ { 2 } ( x ) = ( 2 + 6 x - 3 x ^ { 2 } - x ^ { 3 } -$ $3 x ^ { 4 } / 4 - 3 x ^ { 5 } / 4 + 2 ( 1 - 2 x ) ^ { 3 / 2 } \Theta ( 1 - 2 x ) ) / ( 6 4 x ^ { 4 } )$ , where the Heaviside function $\Theta ( y ) = 0$ for $y < 0$ and $\Theta ( y ) = 1$ for $y \ge 0 ; F _ { 2 } ( 2 x ) = ( 2 x - 1 ) ^ { 3 / 2 } / ( 2 x ) ^ { 5 }$ for $2 x > 1$ Figure 1.7 shows the functional dependencies for $\beta$ and $n _ { 2 }$ .
--[p16]-- The nonlinear TPA coeficient $\beta ( \alpha = \alpha _ { 0 } + \beta I )$ is given by Boyd [25, 57]
$$
\beta [ \mathrm { c m / W } ] = K \frac { \sqrt { E _ { \mathrm { p } } } } { n _ { 0 } ^ { 2 } E _ { \mathrm { g } } ^ { 3 } } F _ { 2 } ( 2 \hbar \omega / E _ { \mathrm { g } } ) ,\tag{1.18}
$$
where $F _ { 2 }$ is the function plotted in Figure 1.7.

===== [p218-218] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Germanium Dioxide (2块) =====
--[p218]-- Germanium Dioxide
Like titania, germanium dioxide $\left( \mathrm { G e O } _ { 2 } \right)$ is widely used for optics because of its high transparency at near-infrared (NIR) and optical wavelengths. As a result, there is great interest in processes that can be used to create germanium dioxide structures by MPL. This has been achieved by sol processing silicon alkoxides mixed with up to 40 wt% of germanium alkoxide [60].
--[p218]-- The refractive index of these composites can be tuned by changing the germanium content, but it was noted that high germanium content compromises the achievable resolution [59].

===== [p298-300] Microfluidic Devices Produced by Two-Photon-Induced Polymerization > Fabrication of Movable Micromachines (11块) =====
--[p298]-- Fabrication of Movable Micromachines
Two-photon microfabrication allows the creation of 3D microstructures by scanning the laser focus inside a photopolymer, unlike conventional stereolithography. Fabrication is accomplished either by scanning the sample relative to the laser focus or the laser focus relative to the sample.
--[p298]-- Although such movable microstructures have been produced by a sacrificial layer technique using micromachining in silicon based on photolithography, two-photon microfabrication makes it possible to produce 3D movable microstructures by direct writing using a femtosecond pulsed laser beam inside a photopolymer.
The process of fabricating a microgear is illustrated in Figure 12.2 [13].
--[p299]-- The circular part of a gear wheel is fabricated by scanning the laser beam circularly with increasing radius. Finally, the teeth of the gear wheel are fabricated by circularly scanning the laser beam by increasing the radius while the scanning speed is changed periodically. In this case, the nonlinear response of two-photon absorption enables the solidification of the resin only in the region where the scanning speed is low.
--[p300]-- This is because the focal depth is enlarged by reducing the numerical aperture of the objective lens. Using an objective lens of low numerical aperture, the input laser power must be increased to generate twophoton-induced polymerization. If the laser power exceeds a critical value, the solidified microparts will be destroyed owing to laser-induced breakdown. For this reason, the thickness of the movable microstructure is limited by the critical laser power.
--[p300]-- To make thicker microparts, the layer-by-layer process is introduced into the laser direct writing method. Figure 12.4a–c shows the SEM images of microgears fabricated by the layer-by-layer process without any supporting parts. In addition, microturbines with curved blades were fabricated by the layer-by-layer process as shown in Figure 12.5. The sophisticated movable parts are useful for functional microfluidic devices.
--[p299]-- In conventional microstereolithography, although simple raster scanning of a laser beam is used to make a microstructure fixed to a substrate, it is not suitable for the assembly-free fabrication of freestanding microparts because of the shrinkage of the photocurable resin during polymerization. The problem is overcome by optimizing the scanning pattern of a laser beam to reduce the deformation of the solidified microstructure.
--[p299]-- For example, circular scanning of a laser beam is used to make a rotation-symmetric movable microstructure such as a microgear [13]. An attached shaft and stopper are first fabricated by scanning a laser beam circularly at each layer, while the stage is lowered step by step at a constant interval along a central axis. Next, the stage is moved to the position to make a movable gear wheel.
--[p298]-- Additionally, fragile micromodels, such as lattice models, can be also produced without deformation due to the surface tension of a rinse during the washing process by supercritical drying technique [12].
On the other hand, a mirror scanning system using galvanometric scanners is suitable for making freely movable microstructures such as microgears [13] and nanotweezers [14].
--[p300]-- Not only thin but also thick movable microparts can be fabricated by the direct laser writing method. Figure 12.3 shows scanning electron microscopy (SEM) images of microgears with diferent thicknesses [15, 16]. The microgears were fabricated by changing both the numerical aperture of an objective lens and the exposure conditions. As the numerical aperture is reduced, a thicker microgear is fabricated.
--[p300]-- Several types of manipulators could be also fabricated by optimizing the scanning pattern of a laser beam. Figure 12.6 shows the SEM images of nanotweezers [14]. The arm parts of the tweezers were fabricated by circular scanning, and then the probe tips were fabricated by point-by-point exposure to the sharp tips. The diameter of the probe was 250 nm.
--[p298]-- Since a stage scanning system can provide an ideal focused spot without aberrations and can be used to fabricate over a large area, this approach has been used to produce ultraprecise 3D microstructures. Figure 12.1 shows some example of 3D microstructures made by two-photon microfabrication. This process allows the fabrication of 3D microstructures not only on flat substrates but also on curved surfaces.

===== [p313-313] Microfluidic Devices Produced by Two-Photon-Induced Polymerization > Future Prospects (4块) =====
--[p313]-- The reduction of the required laser power to drive micromachines enables the reduction of both the size and cost of the total driving system. Therefore, portable lab-on-a-chip systems will be realized for medical diagnostic applications in the near future.
--[p313]-- To produce practical analytical systems based on optically driven microfluidic devices, there remain some issues such as the improvement of the optical driving eficiency and chip-to-world connections. As a promising method to improve the optical driving eficiency, we have recently utilized metalized micromachines [37, 38]. The metalized micromachines are fabricated by the combination of twophoton microfabrication and electroless plating.
--[p313]-- Since the metalized micromachines are be driven by repulsive force due to reflection of the incident laser beam at the metalized surface, the optical radiation pressure is drastically improved. We have already demonstrated that a metalized microrotor could be driven by a laser beam whose laser power was less than 1 mW. Such metalized micromachines will be useful for high-eficiency, optically driven microfluidic devices.
--[p313]-- Future Prospects
We have developed various kinds of optically driven microfluidic devices by using two-photon microfabrication. The microfluidic devices have unique features such as synchronous remote drive of multiple components, high integration of functional elements, and ultralow flow rate. The optical-driven microfluidic devices can be used for low-cost, all-polymer lab-on-a-chip devices, because all of builtin components can be remotely controlled without any wiring and power supply.

===== [p125-126] Photoinitiators for Multiphoton Absorption Lithography > Characterization of Two-Photon Initiators (10块) =====
--[p125]-- Characterization of Two-Photon Initiators
From both fundamental and application perspectives, it is thus necessary to monitor and quantify the strength of the 2PA process and to determine how material parameters and experimental conditions control it. Over the years, a number of diferent experimental techniques have been used to determine the 2PA spectra of materials and to measure their 2PA cross sections.
--[p126]-- Indirect measurement of TPA cross sections is usually referred to as the TPEF method, in which the two-photon approach to determining the excitation (TPE)
been proposed to increase TPA activity through constructing structures with improved intramolecular charge transfer (ICT) properties. We will introduce the research on the molecular design of photoinitiators briefly in this chapter.
--[p125]-- The techniques most often used can be classified as direct methods, in which the attenuation of the excitation beam is monitored, and indirect methods, in which a secondary efect of the 2PA process, such as induced fluorescence, phosphorescence, or a temperature change in the sample, is measured [15].
--[p125]-- Because of the nonlinear absorption process, the transmittance depends on the incident energy. Knowing the optical configuration and pulse characteristics, it is possible to determine the TPA cross section of the material. It should also be pointed out that inherent in the NLT measurement is the assumption that TPA is the predominant process causing the observed intensity-dependent nonlinear absorption.
The deduced equations for a Gaussian beam in an ideal case are the following:
$$
--[p125]-- Generally, direct methods ofdetermining TPA are the nonlinear transmission (NLT) technique [16] and Z-scan measurement [17], while the indirect method is usually referred to as the TPEF method [18].
Direct measurement of TPA cross sections is usually dificult because only a small fraction of photons is absorbed in the two-photon process. The efect of 2PA is observed as a decrease in the intensity of the excitation beam itself [15].
--[p125]-- TPA cross sections can be obtained by monitoring the transmittance changes of a sample when the incident intensity changes. NLT and Z-scan measurements are examples of techniques in this category.
In an NLT experiment, as shown in Figure 6.2, a sample is placed in the path of the excitation beam and the pulse energy is measured before and after the sample [16, 19, 20]. The measurement is repeated over a range of incident pulse energies.
--[p126]-- As shown in Figure 6.3, the transmittance is measured at each position of the sample. For typical configurations (where the sample is thin, there is no 1PA, and all the transmitted light is collected), the transmittance is 1 when the sample is far from the focus of the beam, decreases as the sample is moved closer to the focus, and reaches a minimum at the focus (where the intensity is maximum). The trend is reversed as the sample is moved away from the focus in the opposite direction.
--[p126]-- T _ { \mathrm { i } } = [ \ln ( 1 + I _ { 0 } L \beta ) ] / ( I _ { 0 } L \beta )\tag{6.1}
$$
$$
T _ { \mathrm { i } } = I _ { \mathrm { i } } / I _ { 0 }\tag{6.2}
$$
$$
\delta _ { \mathrm { T P A } } = ( h \nu \beta / N _ { \mathrm { A } } ) \times d \times 1 0 ^ { - 3 }\tag{6.3}
$$
--[p126]-- where $T _ { \mathrm { i } }$ is the transmittance, $I _ { 0 }$ is the incident laser intensity, L is the thickness of the sample cuvette, $\beta$ is the nonlinear absorption coeficient (centimeter per gigawatt), $I _ { \mathrm { i } }$ is the laser intensity passing the sample cuvette (gigawatt per square centimeter), $\delta _ { \mathrm { T P A } }$ is two-photon absorption cross section $( \mathrm { c m ^ { 4 } s p h o t o n ^ { - 1 } } ) , N _ { \mathrm { A } }$ is Avogadro constant $( 6 .
--[p126]-- 0 2 3 \times 1 0 ^ { 2 3 } )$ , d is the concentration (M), h is the Planck constant $( 6 . 6 2 6 \times 1 0 ^ { - 3 4 } \mathrm { J } \mathrm { s } )$ , and � is the incident laser frequency.
In a Z-scan experiment, the pulse energy is kept constant, but change in intensity is achieved by moving the sample along the propagation direction z (hence the term Z-scan) of a focused beam and thus varying the beam cross-sectional area [17, 21–23].

===== [p285-286] Fabricating Nano and Microstructures Made by Narrow Bandgap Semiconductors and Metals using Multiphoton Lithography > Two-Photon Lithography of PbSe Structures (7块) =====
--[p286]-- is much smoother, and the feature size of the line is reduced as well compared to Figure 11.3b, which shows an efective way to improve the quality ofthe fabricated structure.
--[p285]-- The significant refractive index change at the position of the fabricated lines indicates the formation of the high-refractive-index material. The experimentally achieved line width in Figure 11.3b is about 900 nm, which confirms the sub-micrometer fabrication resolution. To show the 3D fabrication ability, pillars with a height of ∼12 μm were fabricated by moving the scanning stage vertically away from the objective (Figure 11.3c).
--[p285]-- We also fabricated 3D structures with diferent layers (Figure 11.3d). As we used a linearly polarized beam, lines at diferent layers fabricated along perpendicular directions (top layer and the second layer in Figure 11.3d) show significant line width diferences (the top layer line width is about 900 nm, the second layer line width is about 500 nm) because of the nonsymmetrical property of the laser beam focal spot.
--[p285]-- This laser-induced instant formation of PbSe in the photoresin enabled the fabrication ofPbSe structures in a solution and facilitated the successful fabrication of narrow bandgap semiconductor materials by two-photon lithography. By moving the relative position of the photoresin with a scanning stage, two-dimensional structures could be fabricated. A typical image obtained from the CCD shows the fabrication of lines with dark color (Figure 11.3a).
--[p285]-- Two-Photon Lithography of PbSe Structures
At room temperature, the mixed PbOA and TOPSe in ODE can form PbSe at a slow reaction rate. The formed PbSe has a dark color, therefore the color of the mixture changes from colorless to dark brown. This is due to the formation ofPbSe nanoparticles in the photoresin.
--[p285]-- However, as can be seen from the SEM images (Figure 11.3b–d), the surface of the fabricated structure is rough. This originates from the fast reaction rate of PbOA with TOPSe under the irradiation of the femtosecond pulsed laser beam, which leads to the uncontrolled growth of PbSe within the volume of the focal spot. To make it smoother, the sample was annealed at $1 2 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 5 min after the fabrication and before washing. The structure after annealing (Figure 11.3e)
--[p285]-- However, as the reaction rate is slow, this can be observed only 12 h after the mixing of PbOA and TOPSe in ODE, which leaves a long enough time interval to use two-photon lithography in this photoresin.
Under the irradiation of a femtosecond pulsed laser beam with wavelength 580 nm, PbSe can be formed instantly.

===== [p320-320] Nanoreplication Printing and Nanosurface Processing > �TM of Complex Geometries (5块) =====
--[p320]-- Then the intended daughter structure as the master contains a membrane to prevent the formation of a closed loop during the molding process. As shown in Figure 13.4, if a structure containing a closed loop were fabricated, then upon molding and mold release, the original structure would be destroyed. However, if the original structure is built having a thin membrane (<1 μm thick), then upon molding no closed loop is formed.
--[p320]-- �TM of Complex Geometries
μTM has been demonstrated to work well for challenging geometries; however, it is still unable to reproduce all of the true 3D features that can be made using TPIP. Closed loops, for example, such as a ring or a coil, can be easily made by TPIP, but cannot be replicated by μTM.
A solution to the closed-loop topological problem was found by Fourkas and coworkers with their development of membrane-assisted micro-transfer molding or (MA-μTM) [15].
--[p320]-- The mold is readily released from such a structure with moderate undercut features. The mold can then be pressed together, and the area where the membrane was can now be sealed, because PDMS self-heals. This mold can then be filled with a monomer, which is polymerized by exposure to UV light. Upon release from the mold, the daughter structure contains a closed loop. The mold can be then be reused to create more closed-loop structures.
--[p320]-- In Figure 13.5 are shown more examples of structures created using MA-μTM, including a table, a coil, and a free-standing ring. Maruo and coworkers have also
used this technique to create a freely moving gear, a vertical coil, and a micromanipulator [16]. These features were significantly smaller than previously demonstrated objects and were $< 1 0 \mu \mathrm { m }$ in size. The membrane in these structures was ∼100 nm wide.
--[p320]-- This technique takes advantage of a unique characteristic of PDMS: it is self-healing. This self-healing is shown in Figure 13.3, where the seam between two pieces of PDMS nearly disappears when they are pressed together. Upon contact, the two pieces of PDMS form a liquid-tight junction that can be formed reversibly.
In MA-μTM, the master structure is intentionally made to be diferent.

===== [p221-221] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Chemical Vapor Deposition (3块) =====
--[p221]-- Because CVD is performed using a comparatively low vacuum, molecular collisions cause metal to deposit conformally on the target surface. However, CVD is slow compared to most other metal coating techniques, sometimes requiring as much as 1 h to deposit 2 nm of the target metal [90]. Additionally, it is not necessarily selective for deposition onto a microstructure versus a supporting substrate or other features on the surface.
--[p221]-- Wegener’s team created functional split-ring resonators (SRRs) by fabricating the forms by MPL in SU-8, coating with a protecting semiconducting oxide layer, and then depositing silver onto the protected structures by CVD using the precursor Ag(hfac)(cod) [90]. Careful control over chamber pressure and temperature, precursor evaporation temperature, and structure temperature yielded a smooth coating of silver metal.
--[p221]-- Chemical Vapor Deposition
CVD uses gaseous organometallic precursors to deposit metal onto a structure via a chemical reaction that occurs at the structure’s surface [89]. The structure is heated to an elevated temperature, which depends on the organometallic precursor. When the gaseous precursor comes into contact with the heated structure, it decomposes, generating organic fragments that largely difuse away from the structure leaving behind the residual metal.

===== [p234-235] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Structures Functionalized by Silicon Inversion (8块) =====
--[p234]-- Some of these limitations were overcome by the introduction of the Si double-inversion (SDI) technique, which was reported in 2006 by Tétreault et al. and used to create a Si replica of a polymeric PC (Figure 9.20) [15]. PC templates were first fabricated by MPL using a photo-polymer, and then infiltrated with silica $\mathrm { ( S i O _ { 2 } ) }$ via ALD. The polymeric lattice was removed by plasma etching or calcination in air to reveal the $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ inverse lattice.
--[p234]-- Structures Functionalized by Silicon Inversion
Silicon has a high refractive index $\left( n = 3 . 4 5 \right)$ and is optically transparent in the infrared, which makes it a good material for creating PCs. But techniques for microfabrication using Si are time consuming and expensive, requiring costly photolithography masks, and multistep procedures [139].
--[p235]-- introduced a modification of the SDI procedure in which a single inversion was used to create a Si inverse PC lattice [22]. In this method, a polymeric PC template was created by MPL and then conformally coated with a thin layer of $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ by ALD. Next CVD was used to completely infiltrate the lattice forming a PC in Si, which was an inverse of the original polymeric template.
--[p234]-- Hydrofluoric acid was then used to remove the $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ , leaving behind a doubly inverted lattice of Si that was a replica of the original polymeric PC, but now in a material with much higher refractive index. Using this approach, a Si PC was created having a complete photonic bandgap of 8.6%. A similar method was followed by Gratson et al. to create hollow woodpile PCs (Figure 9.21) [139].
Hermatschweiler et al.
--[p234]-- Ifneeded, the $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ inverse structure was re-infiltrated with additional $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ by ALD to fine-tune the size of the rods comprising the PC. Next, low-pressure CVD was used to deposit Si within the $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ lattice.
--[p235]-- By applying a thin coating of silica, the aspect ratios of the elliptical woodpile rods and volume fill fraction of the final Si structure were improved and the thin coating provided suficient stability for the high-temperature Si CVD process. Using this approach, the team fabricated high-quality woodpile Si structures with gap/midgap ratios of 14.2% centered at a wavelength of 2.5 μm.
Staude et al.
--[p235]-- Si woodpiles with embedded acceptor-type waveguide arrays were optically characterized, and reflectance measurements showed the presence of a broad stopband.
--[p235]-- created 3D Si woodpile PCs with a complete photonic bandgap (PBG) in the telecommunication window by the SDI method using the photoresist IP-L (Nanoscribe) [140]. An additional step of tempering the Si woodpiles at $6 0 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ was introduced. This transformed the amorphous Si generated by CVD into polycrystalline Si, which possessed a lower refractive index (shifting the bandgap to shorter wavelengths) and lower absorption coeficient.

===== [p317-317] Nanoreplication Printing and Nanosurface Processing > 13.1 (5块) =====
--[p317]-- 13.1
Introduction: Limitations of Multiphoton Lithography
Multiphoton lithography is unique in its ability to create nanoscale objects with any arbitrary 3D geometry, but sufers from two limitations: slow fabrication speed and limited options for polymerizable materials [1]. Since the patterning process is serial, where each device is fabricated one at a time, it is necessarily time consuming to scale-up for the mass production of parts.
--[p317]-- Since the replicas can be created much more quickly than the masters, this method lends itself to mass production. Also, since the crosslinking reaction used in the mold is not TPIP, diferent materials can be used to cast devices that cannot be created directly from TPIP.
--[p317]-- This chapter will summarize the recent work where various molding techniques, such as μTM, have been used to nano-replicate structures made by TPIP. It will also discuss nanosurface processes where added functionality has been introduced to the device by controlling its surface chemistry.
--[p317]-- Another solution to the limited material availability is to chemically alter the microstructure post fabrication and give it a functional coating: for example, by depositing metal on the outside of a structure, thereby creating electrically conductive devices [3]. Yet another solution is to use laser ablation to remove material, which may enable some structures to be fabricated faster and can generate a more reactive surface on which to perform reactions.
--[p317]-- Also, since twophoton induced polymerization (TPIP) can only take place in specific kinds of materials, such as acrylates or epoxies, the choice of materials and, therefore, the mechanical, magnetic, electronic, and optical properties of these materials are quite limited.
One solution to these issues can be found by using TPIP to create master structures and then to mold them using micro-transfer molding (μTM) to create replicas [2].

===== [p223-225] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Electroless Metallization from Solution (18块) =====
--[p224]-- used a similar approach in which parts of a microstructure were fabricated in an acrylate-based resin and others were fabricated using a methacrylate-based resin [13]. After fabrication, the sample was treated with a diamino alkane, which selectively adds to acrylates by Michael addition, and then exposed to a copper EM medium [104]. The metal was deposited selectively at the amine-functionalized surfaces, leaving portions of the structure created from methacrylates un-metallized.
--[p223]-- Kawata and coworkers fabricated a wide range of microstructures by MPL using a commercial photopolymer, activated their surfaces using $\mathrm { S n C l } _ { 2 }$ , then deposited silver using a modified Tollens’ reagent (Figure 9.12) [96]. They also demonstrated that large-area metallized structures could be created by patterning structures in parallel using a microlens array and then metallizing the structures.
--[p225]-- 2 \ \mathrm { a t } \ 4 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . The microstructures fabricated using this process were shown to be partially reflective in the infrared. Ren et al. used a similar process to create nickel-plated PCs and showed that they behaved like thermal emitters when heated (Figure 9.15) [113]. Wang et al. also used a nickel EM process to create ferromagnetic microstructures that could be manipulated using a magnetic field [114].
--[p225]-- These metallo-dielectric PCs behaved like “hot mirrors” in which the structure transitions from reflecting to nonreflecting at a wavelength that can be tuned by simply changing the period of the lattice.
Misawa et al. fabricated woodpile PCs in SU-8 and coated these structures in nickel [111, 112].
--[p225]-- Some microstructures remained attached to the substrate, such as a cantilever [105], while other microstructures were not attached to the substrate but held in place by interconnecting parts, such as a micro-rotor [106].
Kuebler et al. used a modification of an EM process first reported by Danscher [107, 108] to deposit electrically conductive and highly reflective silver conformally onto 3D microstructures and woodpile PCs fabricated by MPL in SU-8.
--[p225]-- The metal coating also increased the hardness and modulus of the microstructure by 10- and 9-fold, respectively.
--[p224]-- The selective functionalization was achieved by either copious cleaning or plasma-etching the substrate prior to processing [12, 94, 102], or through chemical modification of the substrate, such as application of a hydrophobic coating prior to casting the photosensitive resin [17].
Selective metallization can be achieved through a two-step process in which a microstructure is fabricated using two diferent resins, only one of which is active toward EM.
--[p224]-- Kawata and coworkers fabricated part of a microstructure by MPL using the methacrylate resin KC1102 (JSR), developed to remove unpolymerized material, and then continued fabrication using a composite of KC1102 blended with a methacrylamide, and developed again. The surface of the finished structure was treated with a Tollens’-like reagent. Silver deposited selectively onto those portions of the structure that were fabricated using the methacrylamide-doped resin [103]. Fourkas et al.
--[p225]-- The surfaces of polymeric microstructures were activated toward EM by attachment of Au NPs [109, 110]. Silver deposited in this way was shown to have a conductivity of $1 \times 1 0 ^ { 4 } \Omega ^ { - 1 } \mathrm { c m ^ { - 1 } }$ , which is 1.5% of that of bulk silver metal [12, 94]. Terzaki and coworkers have compared several approaches for silver deposition and used them to create metallized microstructures created with organic–inorganic hybrid polymers [95].
Kuebler et al.
--[p225]-- This process involves fabricating a microstructure by MPL in SU-8, sensitizing the surface using aqueous $\mathrm { S n C l } _ { 2 } ,$ , activating the surface for EM with aqueous $\mathrm { P d C l } _ { 2 } ,$ and then finally immersing the structure in a nickel EM bath consisting of $\mathrm { N i C l } _ { 2 }$ $\mathrm { N H _ { 4 } C l } _ { \mathrm { i } }$ $\mathrm { N a H _ { 2 } P O _ { 2 } }$ and $\mathrm { N H _ { 4 } O H }$ at $\mathrm { p H } = 9 .
--[p224]-- This technique was used to fabricate a functional micro-inductor that was electrically characterized (Figure 9.13).
Copper EM has also been used to create movable metallic 3D microstructures [105, 106]. Three-dimensional microstructures were fabricated using an acrylic resin consisting of Sartomer SR-368 and Sartomer SR-499 and functionalized with ethylene diamine. The surface of the amine-functionalized microstructure was sensitized using $\mathrm { P d C l } _ { 2 }$ .
--[p225]-- fabricated functional metallo-dielectric PCs by using EM to deposit copper onto polymeric lattices created by MPL in SU-8 (Figure 9.14) [102]. Infrared reflectance spectra showed that EM significantly transformed the optical properties of these PCs. Whereas the polymeric PCs exhibited the usual narrow stopband, metallization created a broad “plasmonic” stopband, characterized by high reflectance at all wavelengths below the stopband and low reflectance at wavelengths above the stopband.
--[p225]-- Copper metal was then deposited by EM using 0.6 M Rochelle salt, 0.14 M NaOH, 0.28 M $\mathrm { C u S O _ { 4 } } ,$ , and 0.3 M EDTA. The parts of the microstructure attached to the substrate were then laser-ablated, which freed the microstructure from the surface, allowing the microstructure to move.
--[p223]-- Tollens’-like approaches have been used to create a wide range of metallized 3D microstructures [95, 99–101].
One of the appeals of EM is that the approach can be selective toward a given surface. Several groups have shown that EM can be used to deposit metal on a polymeric microstructure selectively, leaving the supporting substrate largely metal-free [12, 17, 94, 102].
--[p223]-- In EM, a metal ion precursor and a reducing agent are co-present in the solution, along with a stabilizing agent that prevents them from reacting spontaneously. A surface to be metallized is then chemically sensitized and immersed into the EM bath. The sensitizer enables rapid reaction of the metal salt and the reducing agent, forming a conformal coating of metal on the surface.
--[p223]-- [13, 97] are among the earliest reports of using EM to deposit metal conformally onto 3D structures created by MPL. A widely used approach for depositing silver onto microstructures involves using a modified Tollens’ reagent [98]. This EM bath consists of silver cations dissolved in basic aqueous media and an aldehyde reducing agent. The reducing agent can be a sugar, such as glucose, which exists in solution in an equilibrium between the cyclic form and the aldehyde-terminated open form.
--[p223]-- Electroless Metallization from Solution
Microstructures can be conformally and rapidly coated with metal by electroplating or electroless deposition in solution. Electroplating of polymeric surfaces is inherently dificult because most are not electrically conductive. With electroless metallization (EM), a conductive surface is not required.
--[p223]-- Polymeric surfaces can be sensitized by reaction with $\mathrm { S n C l } _ { 2 }$ , binding metal NPs, or other means. Both EM and electroless deposition produce metal with a nanoscale morphology that is sensitive to the metal type, the deposition conditions, and additives that can be co-present to control the kinetics of the reaction [12, 94, 95].
The works by Kawata et al. [17, 96] and Fourkas et al.

===== [p190-192] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > Biological Design Constraints (6块) =====
--[p190]-- As a caveat, these guidelines were determined in non-radical-generating systems and there is the potential for additional cellular damage based on the type and concentration of radicals generated during polymerization.
--[p192]-- The materials need to be functional and integrate with biological systems, as well as be comprised of components that are compatible with living systems, including each of the constitutive components and potential leachants. While the majority of the chemistries in this chapter have been previously utilized for biomedical applications, novel chemistries should be assessed for compatibility with biological systems.
--[p190]-- When material fabrication or modification is conducted in the presence of cells or organisms, it is also necessary to balance the photophysics of the reaction with the potential for photodamage to the biological specimen. For instance, it has been shown (and utilized for experimentation) that suficient pulse energies can sever actin filaments within cells [38, 192, 196].
--[p190]-- Biological Design Constraints
In general, when fabricating materials with MPL photopolymerization, it is critical to balance the power needed to initiate polymerization against the power that will damage or cavitate the precursor solution. For a PEG-based hydrogel, it was found that pulse energies in excess of 2.3 nJ induced cavitation within the highly water swollen gel [136].
--[p190]-- The constraints of biology may further confine the operating space for MPL photopolymerization in the presence of cells; however, the benefit of a small focal volume and little out-of-plane absorption confines potential damage to a narrow window through which the focal point is rastered.
Additional constraints exist in the use of MPL for biological applications.
--[p190]-- Additional work has shown that femtosecond pulsed two-photon lasers can be utilized in the presence of cells, but pulse energies above 1.5 nJ induced intracellular photoablation [196] and those above 4 nJ induced cell death [197]. In order to avoid nonspecific damage to intracellular structures and to maintain high cell viability, the experimenter should operate the laser with a pulse energy below 1.5 nJ.

===== [p186-187] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > Biomaterial Fabrication (8块) =====
--[p186]-- The field of photonics was an early adopter of the MPL paradigm for application-based materials. MPL polymerization has been employed to generate functional waveguides [166], interferometers [167], microlenses [168], and microlasers [169]. Based on the ability to generate features on the sub-micrometer scale, the community has adapted these approaches to generate photonic crystals with stop bands in the visible range.
--[p186]-- Biomaterial Fabrication
Initial work with MPL photopolymerization focused on the development on nonbiological materials [162]. Polymerizable resins (or negative-tone photoresists) have been employed in MPL material fabrication, often starting as viscous liquids or amorphous solids. These systems can be easily transformed into complex structures such as interconnected microchannels [163], interlocking chains [164], or renditions of the Venus de Milo [165].
--[p186]-- In other approaches, MPL polymerization has been exploited to generate active mechanical devices including springs, cantilevers, and nanorotors [162]. Based on the successful implementation of these techniques, researchers began exploring the use of polymer precursors for the fabrication of structured materials for biological applications.
Cells reside in the body within a complex, dynamic, and highly structured ECM, which choreographs cell fate through bidirectional cell–ECM interactions.
--[p186]-- Minimally, the material should support cell colonization, migration, growth, and diferentiation, as well as provide appropriate biophysical and biochemical cues for the specific cell type of interest [172]. In general, two classes of MPL photopolymerized materials have been introduced as biomaterials: biocompatible networks into which cells are introduced post fabrication and hydrogels into which cells can be directly encapsulated.
--[p186]-- Within this paradigm, photopolymerization reactions via MPL enable precise recapitulation of ECM properties (e.g., biophysical and biochemical signals) in 3D biomaterials with nanometer and micrometer resolution. When exploiting MPL to generate photopolymerized biomaterials, it is important to consider the essential functions and design constraints that apply to materials for cell culture applications.
--[p186]-- Many spatiotemporal signals are integrated by the cell to inform output cellular functions including adhesion, proliferation, diferentiation, migration, and tissue morphogenesis [170]. The specific mechanisms through which the ECM directs cell function is still not fully understood. Biomaterial strategies provide attractive platforms to dissect complex and dynamic cell–ECM communication as well as to engineer scafolds to leverage these cues for regenerative medicine applications [15, 171].
--[p187]-- MPL polymerization afords the generation ofhighly ordered structures in three dimensions that are biocompatible, degradable, and easily fabricated – all important features for biological implementation. These techniques have been adapted to generate cytocompatible microneedles, biodegradable scafolds, and ossicular replacement prostheses (Figure 8.8) [173, 174, 177].
--[p187]-- To better recapitulate the context of the in vivo milieu, MPL has been extended to the fabrication of hydrated polymer networks in the form of hydrogels for cell culture applications [172]. Hydrogels are structurally similar to the native ECM and present an attractive class of materials for tissue engineering and regenerative medicine applications [171]. Specific examples include woodpile lattices, microfabricated porous structures, and vascular trees (Figure 8.8) [42, 175, 178, 179].
Additional work has focused on the manipulation of native proteins for biomedical applications (Figure 8.8) [176, 180–182].

===== [p215-215] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Titanium Dioxide (4块) =====
--[p215]-- Sol–gel hybrids were created by combining various amounts of titanium(IV) isopropoxide (TIP) and MAPTMS. This approach was used to tune the refractive index by varying the ratio of TIP and MAPTMS in the composite (Figure 9.6) [69, 70]. These and related approaches for creating 3D structures of nearly pure $\mathrm { T i O } _ { 2 }$ [71, 72] have shown how efective the methods are for creating microscale photonic devices with very high or very specific refractive index.
--[p215]-- Titanium Dioxide
Titanium dioxide $( \mathrm { T i O } _ { 2 } ,$ titania) has both high refractive index and high transparency at visible wavelengths, which is not available with many other materials. Along with somewhat enhanced robustness, these properties have made composites of $\mathrm { T i O } _ { 2 }$ very worthwhile to explore. Structures of titania composites have been fabricated by processing complexes of titanium(IV) acrylate or titanium(IV) ethoxide blended with a photopolymer [8].
--[p215]-- The resulting composite contains $\mathrm { T i O } _ { 2 } ,$ , which can be annealed, changing the titania morphology and eliminating organic components. This process yields a structure with high refractive index and was used to create the first titania PC having a complete bandgap at visible wavelengths [68].
Three-dimensional structures have been fabricated by MPL using composites based on titania nanoparticles or silicon/titanium sol–gel hybrids.
--[p215]-- After fabrication, the nanoparticle composite contained titanium hydroxide as a result of air exposure. The structure was then converted to $\mathrm { T i O } _ { 2 }$ by annealing to drive of the organic components.
Titanium(IV) n-butoxide complexed with benzoylacetone undergoes bond cleavage when irradiated with UV light or by MPL, rendering it insoluble in organic solvents like acetone.

===== [p222-222] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Functionalization by Attachment of Nanoparticles (5块) =====
--[p222]-- This can allow NPs with specific photonic or binding properties, which can be used in sensing, photonics, or any number of other applications.
Aekbote et al. [91] used the Turkevich synthesis [92] to produce citrate-capped Au NPs which were bound to a 3D microstructure to increase their ability to be captured in an optical trap (Figure 9.11). MPL was used to fabricate the 3D structures in SU-8.
--[p222]-- Next, a 50-nm gold film was produced on a separate substrate by MS. The PC was then placed near the gold film, and LIT was used to transfer gold NPs onto the surface. This technique produced Au NPs with a uniform size across the PC surface, although the coating was not conformal.
--[p222]-- Functionalization by Attachment of Nanoparticles
Metal NPs synthesized ex situ can be chemically bound to a polymeric 3D microstructure created by MPL. Although this technique does not provide complete coverage of the structure, the process can deposit NPs onto all sides of a microstructure, including overhangs. The major advantage of producing NPs separately lies in being able to control the identity of the metal, the NP shape, or the chemical protecting group surrounding the NP.
--[p222]-- In LIT, an amplified laser is focused onto a noble metal surface, causing localized melting and pressure gradients at the focal point. This results in a jetlike structure that ejects molten metal from the area in which the laser is focused. The surface tension of the small droplets causes the metal to form NPs. Reinhardt et al. used LIT to coat an organic–inorganic hybrid polymer with Au NPs [93]. First a woodpile PC was created by MPL in an organic–inorganic hybrid polymer.
--[p222]-- These were then treated with 3-(aminopropyl)-triethoxysilane to functionalize the microstructure surfaces with amino groups. Immersing the amine-functionalized structures in a low-pH solution protonated the surface and attracted negatively charged citrate-capped Au NPs onto the structures.
Laser-induced transfer (LIT) is another approach for depositing metal NPs onto a surface.

===== [p209-209] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > 9.2 (1块) =====
--[p209]-- 9.2

===== [p51-53] 2 Characterization of 2PA Chromophores > Tetraone Dye (6块) =====
--[p52]-- Figure 2.27 shows the results of excite–probe measurements of TD2765 with a probe wavelength of 670 nm and three diferent excitation wavelengths. Exciting at 710 nm (curve 1), no ESA is observed as verified by the full recovery of the probe signal within the pulse temporal length. At 700 nm (curve 2), excite–probe measurements show a small fraction of ESA (signal lasting after the pulse), indicating mostly 2PA evidenced by a recovery of 94% of the normalized transmittance.
--[p51]-- Tetraone Dye
The characterization of this dye, labeled TD2765 and shown in Figure 2.26, is similar to the squaraine dye described earlier. The methodologies are similar to those described in Section 2.4.1, and the results are shown in Figure 2.26.
The 2PA spectrum in this case was also taken using a combination of 2PF and Z-scan methods. However, close to the linear absorption edge $( \lambda \approx 7 0 0 \mathrm { n m } )$ the Z-scan gives 2PA cross sections as large as 35 000 GM.
--[p52]-- If the ESA was not accounted for in these measurements, it would have appeared that we measured a 2PA cross section in excess of 35 000 GM, and the errors would probably be significantly larger even closer to the absorption edge. Analysis of the excite–probe data allows us to determine the relative contribution of ESA and 2PA and correct the Z-scan data to give the 2PA spectrum shown in Figure 2.26.
--[p51]-- Since this is very
close to the linear absorption region, one must be cognizant that ESA from excited states produced by 1PA can easily be mistaken for 2PA, as described in Section 2.2. The best means of determining the mechanisms for the NLA in such cases is to perform excite–probe measurements, since the 2PA has an ultrafast response while the ESA will have a decay time equal to the fluorescence lifetime, typically hundreds of picoseconds to nanoseconds.
--[p53]-- To measure the ESA spectrum shown in Figure 2.28, a femtosecond excitation near the linear absorption resonance is used along with a WLC probe. A description ofthis technique can be found in Refs. [53] and [58] and is discussed in Section 2.3.2.2.
--[p52]-- Significant ESA is observed with an excitation wavelength of 690 nm (curve 3). At 690 nm, the linear absorption cross section is $1 . 6 \times 1 0 ^ { - 1 8 } \mathrm { c m } ^ { 2 }$ , which is almost three orders of magnitude smaller than the peak absorption cross section, yet there is significant ESA.

===== [p226-228] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Multiphoton Lithography of Nanoparticles Supported in a Polymer Matrix (7块) =====
--[p226]-- AF 380 was included as a photo-activatable reducing agent that could transfer electrons to ${ \mathrm { A u } } ^ { 3 + }$ cations upon photoexcitation, leading to the formation of Au NPs. After MPL, the microstructures were developed as usual. Figure 9.16a shows lines fabricated on a glass substrate under diferent exposure conditions. --[p226]-- This can be advantageous for certain applications and, in principle, could simplify the fabrication process, insofar as the metallized structure can be produced in a single MPL step.
Shukla et al. introduced a method for creating Au NP-containing SU-8 microstructures for various photonic applications [115, 116]. In their approach, $\mathrm { H A u C l } _ { 4 }$ and the two-photon-absorbing dye AF 380 were blended with SU-8.
--[p226]-- Microstructures fabricated in the presence of the gold precursor had smaller line widths than those fabricated with neat SU-8 (Figure 9.16b). The fabricated lines were conductive to within an order of magnitude of bulk gold due to the high loading fraction of the gold precursor.
--[p226]-- Microstructures with metal NPs have been fabricated in several other materials, including Ag NPs in poly(vinylpyrrolidone) [117], Ag NPs in SU-8 [118], Ag NPs in a porous $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ framework [62], Au NPs in an acrylate matrix [119], and Au, Cu, and Ag NPs in borosilicate glass [56].

Data could be stored digitally by writing Au NP-rich pixels within the volume of the material and reading out the data by laser-induced fluorescence. This system is useful for write-once–read-many (WORM) applications, like long-term data storage.
--[p228]-- took advantage of the fact that ${ \mathrm { A u } } ^ { 3 + }$ quenches the fluorescence of rhodamine B dye, whereas Au NPs do not, to develop a direct-write medium for 3D optical data storage [120]. The investigators doped poly(methylmethacrylate) matrix (PMMA) with rhodamine B and $\mathrm { H A u C l } _ { 4 }$ .
--[p226]-- Multiphoton Lithography of Nanoparticles Supported in a Polymer Matrix
Metallized 3D microstructures can also be prepared by incorporating metal ions in the photopolymer used to create the structure. This approach can yield structures with metal distributed throughout the interior of the 3D microstructure, rather than merely on the surface.

While the wavelength is tuned from 800 to 580 nm, the weight of the reduced silver dot fabricated with the laser energy in the threshold region increased faster with increasing the laser energy, which suggests that the shorter wavelength rendered higher photosensitivity.

===== [p176-177] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > Photoinitiators (2块) =====
--[p176]-- Photoinitiators
and a phosphonyl radical, both of which can initiate polymerization eficiently in cellular systems. Though the LAP PI is not commercially available, its two-step synthesis is straightforward and nearly quantitative [56].
Several type II PIs have also been utilized for photopolymerization in the presence of cells. These most commonly used include rose Bengal, eosin Y (Figure 8.2c,d), and camphorquinone [57].
--[p177]-- When exposed to light, these compounds get excited to a triplet state, which reacts with a hydrogen-donating co-initiator (e.g., an amine or thiol containing molecule) to produce an initiating free radical. Despite their wide usage, as well as their unique ability to perform efectively in the visible range, the photokinetics of type II systems are less understood than their type I counterparts [58].

===== [p20-20] Rapid Laser Optical Printing in 3D at a Nanoscale > Conclusions and Outlook (4块) =====
--[p20]-- By varying the exposure conditions, one can achieve control in the fourth dimension based on mechanical shape memory [74] or degree of chemical modification [75], thus “unlocking” the possibility of 4D additive manufacturing.
--[p20]-- It extends dramatically emerging fabrication accuracy of 3D printing by three orders to the realm of nanoscale, ofering unmatched, rapid prototyping and additive manufacturing possibilities. Lastly, tightly focused femtosecond pulses enable tunable light–matter interaction level at ultralocalized dimensions.
--[p20]-- By precisely adjusting the temperature at the focal region at a high speed $\gg 1$ mm $\mathbf { s } ^ { - 1 }$ scanning, high-resolution patterns can be fabricated over areas useful for microoptical and microfluidic research and practical applications. The proposed nanostructuring approach is not only limited to cross-linkable polymers (resins) but can also be further implemented for pure optical printing of glasses, metals, or even viable proteins [70–73].
--[p20]-- Conclusions and Outlook
Analysis ofthe light delivery at tight focusing and thermal accumulation presented here shows the importance of avalanche ionization and thermal processes in 3D polymerization by DLW employing high-repetition, femtosecond pulses.

===== [p77-77] Modeling of Polymerization Processes > Difusion of Molecules (3块) =====
--[p77]-- Difusion of Molecules
Polymerization involves many types of molecules, including growing and dead chains of diferent lengths, crosslinked macromolecules, the inhibitor, the photoinitiator, and so on. Their contribution to the spatial profile of the final conversion is not equal. The difusion coeficient of a macromolecule in a dilute solution can be evaluated with the Einstein–Stokes formula
$$
--[p77]-- Among them, the contribution of the short dead chains to the overall conversion is minimal. On the contrary, the difusion of the growing chains is important. They difuse fast during the short-length stage, grow, and then make significant contribution to the conversion.
In this section, the difusion efects are studied separately for the growing chains and the inhibitor (quencher).
--[p77]-- D _ { \mathrm { m } } = \frac { k _ { \mathrm { B } } T } { 3 \pi \eta _ { \mathrm { m } } d _ { \mathrm { m } } } ,\tag{3.41}
$$
where $k _ { \mathrm { B } }$ is the Boltzmann constant, T is the temperature, $\eta _ { \mathrm { m } }$ is the viscosity, and $d _ { \mathrm { m } }$ is the diameter of the molecule. Thus, the contribution of the difusion of long chains and large crosslinked clusters is smaller than that of the fast and small molecules.

===== [p154-154] Hybrid Materials for Multiphoton Polymerization > Sol–Gel Preparation (3块) =====
--[p154]-- A photosensitive sol–gel process usually involves the catalytic hydrolysis of the sol–gel precursors and the polycondensation of the hydrolyzed products and other sol–gel active components present in the reaction medium to form a macromolecular hybrid network structure. The formed gel is subsequently reacted through photopolymerization to give a product similar to glass.
Three-dimensional structure fabrication by MPP generally involves a four-step process, described in Figure 7.1.
--[p154]-- Sol–Gel Preparation
The first materials employed in MPP were acrylic photopolymers and the negative photoresist SU8 [12]. Over the last few years, research on MPP materials has focused on photosensitive hybrid materials [13], such as the commercially <sub>available ORMOCER</sub>® <sub>(organically modified ceramic microresist) [14–16]. The</sub> sol–gel process is based on the phase transformation of a sol of metallic oxide or an alkoxide precursor to form a wet gel.
--[p154]-- The first one is hydrolysis and condensation, in which precursors or monomers such as metal oxides or metal alkoxides are mixed with water, which then undergo hydrolysis and condensation to form a porous interconnected cluster structure (Scheme 7.1). Either an acid such as HCl or a base like $\mathrm { N H } _ { 3 }$ can be employed as the catalyst.
$$
- S i - O R + H - O H \xrightarrow [ ] { H y d r o l y s i s } - S i - O H + R - O H
$$

===== [p216-216] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Zirconium Dioxide (2块) =====
--[p216]-- Zirconium Dioxide
Zirconium dioxide $( \mathrm { Z r O } _ { 2 } )$ is a hard material with high refractive index, which makes it well suited for composite microstructures. Chichkov and coworkers explored the use of the Zr-containing polymer sol–gel composite SZ2080 for MPL (Figure 9.7) [65, 74–76]. The zirconium oxide increases the hardness and reduces the shrinkage of the material upon processing, which are two common problems associated with fabricating high-resolution 3D structures by MPL.
--[p216]-- The resolution of this system was improved by formulating composites containing 2-dipentaerythritol pentamethacrylate [61]. This molecule features a large number of reactive acrylate groups, which substantially increases crosslinking and serves to reinforce the material. Minimum feature widths of ∼100 nm were achieved with the composite [61].

--[p28]-- It is therefore not surprising that most inconsistencies in the literature involve reported values for the nonlinear coecients being too large. ===== [p281-282] Fabricating Nano and Microstructures Made by Narrow Bandgap Semiconductors and Metals using Multiphoton Lithography > Challenges of Multiphoton Lithography with Top-Down Approach for Narrow Electronic Bandgap Semiconductors (6块) =====
--[p282]-- This excitation wavelength of longer than 5 μm does not facilitate the manipulation of the multiphoton lithography system and is the hurdle to sub-micrometer lithography resolution. Furthermore, the refractive index value of 4.82 is also a serious problem for 3D multiphoton lithography in bulk PbSe.
--[p281]-- The main challenges of 3D fabrication with multiphoton lithography in bulk semiconductor PbSe for the top-down strategy come from the narrow electronic bandgap and the high refractive index of PbSe.
Two-photon lithography is performed in a material that absorbs the excitation photon via a two-photon process. However, two-photon lithography in bulk PbSe requires the excitation wavelength to be longer than 5 μm, as the bandgap of bulk PbSe is 0.27 eV.
--[p282]-- For 3D multiphoton lithography in a material with refractive index much larger than that of the immersion oil, when the excitation focal spot is moved deeply into the material, the excitation focal spot is more defocused, more elongated, and more distorted [20]. This hinders a symmetric, high-resolution, and depth-unafected fabrication, which is clearly shown in Figure 11.1a.
--[p282]-- The large refractive index mismatch requires strong wavefront aberration compensation, which is a big challenge for 3D multiphoton lithography in bulk PbSe.
--[p282]-- As multiphoton lithography uses a high numerical aperture (NA) objective lens to focus the excitation laser beam into the fabricated material, the refractive index of the objective immersion oil (normally about 1.52 for a 1.4 NA objective) is much smaller than 4.82.
--[p281]-- Challenges of Multiphoton Lithography with Top-Down Approach for Narrow Electronic Bandgap Semiconductors
The challenges of multiphoton lithography with top-down approach to get metal 3D nano and microstructures are obvious because light cannot be focused inside a metal. But the challenges ofmultiphoton lithography with the top-down approach to get 3D nano and microstructures made by narrow electronic bandgap materials are not straightforward.

===== [p229-229] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Metal Backfilling by Electroplating (3块) =====
--[p229]-- This resulted in the growth of continuous bulk metal within the helical holes of the patterned polymer film. Plasma etching was then used to remove the polymer matrix, leaving behind a structure of freely standing individual gold helices. These structures were shown to selectively reflect circularly polarized light of one handedness and transmit the other.
--[p229]-- Metal Backfilling by Electroplating
As mentioned previously, it is inherently dificult to electroplate polymeric microstructures because of their lack of conductivity. However, Gansel et al. have found a way to use electroplating to backfill a micro-patterned polymeric film with gold [23]. The positive-tone resist AZ 9260 (MicroChemicals GmbH) was used to create helical voids on top of an indium-tin oxide (ITO) substrate, which served as the cathode.
--[p229]-- After MPL and development, a platinized tin mesh was placed on top of the polymer block, to serve as an anode. The two-electrode sample was placed in a solution of sodium disulfitoaurate(I), sodium sulfite, ethylenediamine for stabilization, and disodium ethylenediamine tetraacetic acid. The solution was held at constant temperature while a fixed potential was applied at constant current to reduce $\mathrm { A u ^ { + } }$ to gold metal at the ITO electrode.

===== [p231-231] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Structures Functionalized by In Situ NP Formation (4块) =====
--[p231]-- The high solubility ofthe chemical precursors in the organic solution allowed laser beam-induced formation ofstructures containing a high loading ofsuficient amounts of Pb and Se elements.Three-dimensional PbSe structures consisting of overlaying lines with sub-micrometer widths of 500 nm were fabricated using this composite. --[p231]-- used PbSe–NP composites to create narrow-bandgap PCs [133]. The PbSe precursors lead oleate (PbOA) and TOPSe (Se dissolved in trioctylphosphine) were dissolved in 1-octadecene (ODE) and then blended with a photopolymer.
--[p231]-- Structures Functionalized by In Situ NP Formation
NP-containing microstructures can be created by MPL in a liquid composite that contains chemical precursors of the NPs [21, 133, 134]. As the focused laser beam is scanned within the composite, it both forms the polymeric matrix of the microstructure and induces chemical formation of NPs. After laser patterning, the unpolymerized material can be washed away, leaving the 3D nanocomposite polymer structure.
Sun et al.
--[p231]-- fabricated 3D log-pile PCs using a composite that formed CdS NPs in situ during MPL (Figure 9.18) [21]. The composite consisted of acrylate monomers, oligomers, a photoinitiator, and cadmium methacrylate [21, 134]. After MPL, the microfabricated structures were treated with hydrogen sulfide gas to form the CdS NPs [21, 134]. Nanocomposites with tunable fluorescence maxima were created by controlling the amount of crosslinker in the resin [134].

===== [p209-210] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Fluorescent-Dye-Doped Organic Microstructures (7块) =====
--[p209]-- Fluorescent-Dye-Doped Organic Microstructures
Fluorescent 3D organic microstructures have been realized via MPL using acrylate composites doped with fluorescent dyes and through post-fabrication chemical functionalization [18, 41]. Doping with a fluorescent dye yields active structures that can be used for sensing, photonics, or biomedical functions [45, 46].
--[p210]-- After development, the polymeric resin doped with disodium fluoride was deposited onto the same substrate. A second part of the structure was fabricated by MPL and then developed again. Finally, the third part of the microstructure was fabricated using the polymeric resin doped with rhodamine. Fluorescence imaging showed that the three parts of the microstructure fluoresced at the diferent wavelengths, with no apparent evidence of the fluorophores having mixed between the parts.
--[p210]-- Microstructures that have a broad and tunable fluorescence emission from approximately � = 350 to 700 nm have been prepared (Figure 9.2) [45]. The polymeric resin used was doped with a specific fluorescent dye, namely stilbene 420, disodium fluorescein, or rhodamine B, which emit at center wavelengths 420, 535, and 580 nm, respectively. The microfabrication was done in stages. First, the polymeric resin doped with stilbene was used to create a portion of the microstructure by MPL.
--[p209]-- Optically active microstructures containing the conductive and luminescent polymer poly[2-methoxy-5-(2- ethylhexyloxy)-1,4-phenylenevinylene] (MEH-PPV) were observed to retain the fluorescence spectra of the dye after structure fabrication [41]. Complex 3D active microstructures were fabricated in acrylate resins mixed with the laser dye LD490 [48]. Two-photon confocal fluorescence showed that the microstructures were uniformly stained with the dye.
--[p210]-- Biofunctionalization was demonstrated by first immobilizing biotin onto the surface of a microstructure made using an organically modified ceramics (ORMOCER) followed by exposure to fluorescent-dye-labeled avidin [18] or streptavidin [19].
--[p210]-- In this process, the order of the stages is interchangeable depending on which part of the microstructure is required to emit at a given wavelength. A fluorescing artificial scafold with promising applications in cell growth and tracking was fabricated in the hybrid sol–gel photopolymer organically modified silicate (ORMOSIL) doped with rhodamine 6G, fluorescein, LC6500, and coumarin 152 [46].
--[p209]-- A microstructure created with a polymeric composite containing rhodamine 610 at 0.5 wt% exhibited red fluorescence upon one- and two-photon excitation at wavelengths of $\lambda = 4 8 0$ and 780 nm, respectively [47]. Incorporating rhodamine did not adversely afect the fabrication process, as the structure displayed good structural integrity and resolution.

===== [p181-181] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > Oxime ligation (3块) =====
--[p181]-- In an alternative strategy developed by Dumy, the alkoxyamine has been photocaged with a 2-(2-nitrophenyl)propyloxycarbonyl (NPPOC)
group [96]. Under brief irradiation with UV light (� = 365 nm), NPPOC undergoes irreversible cleavage to yield $\mathrm { C O } _ { 2 } ,$ a styrene byproduct, and the reactive alkoxyamine. This reaction has been exploited for the conjugation of small molecules and peptides to glass surfaces [96–98].
--[p181]-- Barner-Kowollik and coworkers demonstrated that the photocleavage (� = 365 nm) of o-nitrobenzyl derivatives, which typically leads to the generation of a nitrosobenzaldehyde, can be exploited to give spatiotemporal control over aldehyde formation [95]. The photogenerated aldehydes then react with hydroxylamines to form oxime linkages. This reaction sequence has been utilized to pattern fluorescent peptides onto reactive surfaces.
--[p181]-- Oxime ligation
The condensation reaction of alkoxyamines with aldehydes or ketones yields oxime linkages that are both formed and stable under physiological conditions. Photocontrol over oxime ligation has been independently efected through strategies that cage each of the two reactive species.

===== [p157-158] Hybrid Materials for Multiphoton Polymerization > Composite Hybrid Materials (5块) =====
--[p157]-- By contrast, the versatile chemistry ofsol–gel composites enables the co-polymerization of more than one metal alkoxide; this has been shown to enhance the material’s mechanical stability and endows it with modified optical properties [49–53].
In addition to the above-mentioned materials employed in the laser-induced fabrication by MPP, there are several examples of composite photosensitive hybrid materials used in MPL applications [4, 54–56]. Ovsianikov et al.
--[p157]-- Composite Hybrid Materials
While ORMOCER and other silicate-only-based hybrid materials have provided the possibility to fabricate high-resolution 3D structures with good optical properties, they do not allow the optimization and “fine-tuning” of the materials properties for specific applications.
--[p158]-- Diferent variants of zirconium–silicon composites have been investigated for their suitability for bone regeneration [64, 65]. Terzaki et al. decorated the surface of a zirconium hybrid with calcium-binding amyloid peptides, proposing a “scafold-on-scafold” hard tissue engineering strategy (Figure 7.6) [66].
--[p158]-- In addition to metal alkoxides, hybrid material chemistry provides the possibility of the inclusion of functional groups, such as nonlinear optical molecules [47, 67], quantum dots [68], and metal-binding materials [69]. Recently, a zirconium silicate doped with a monomer containing amine moieties was used for the
--[p157]-- showed that under specific fluence conditions, specific material combinations can be structured into complex 3D structures without shrinkage [57, 58]. Psycharakis et al. showed that doped silicates are biocompatible and can enhance cell growth [59]. A zirconium material was employed by Raimondi to fabricate niches for cell growth [60] and for the construction of microfluidic devices (Figure 7.5) [10, 61–63].

===== [p183-183] Photopolymers for Multiphoton Lithography in Biomaterials and Hydrogels > Benzophenone (3块) =====
--[p183]-- Light-Mediated Degradation Though the majority of photochemical reactions result in the formation of covalent bonds, there is growing interest in photoactive linkers that undergo cleavage in response to photonic irradiation. When incorporated into the backbone of polymeric species, these moieties provide a tunable handle for network depolymerization. Here, we highlight (Figure 8.6)
Photocleavage
o-nitrobenzyl esters, coumarins, and disulfide-containing compounds as photocleavable species that can be controlled in a biological context.
--[p183]-- The resulting potential for multiple activation gives rise to higher overall yields of photocrosslinking than with phenyl azide and diazirine crosslinkers. Though benzophenone as a photoafinity tag permeates much of biochemistry [118], the molecule has also been used to attach peptides and polymer films to solid surfaces [119–121].
--[p183]-- Benzophenone
When exposed to UV light, benzophenone photolysis yields a highly reactive triplet-state ketone intermediate [117]. The energized electron of this activated benzophenone can insert into hydrogen–carbon bonds within the target molecules. Interestingly, the activated intermediate relaxes to yield the original benzophenone moiety when no covalent linkage is formed.

===== [p220-220] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Thermal Evaporation (3块) =====
--[p220]-- Thermal Evaporation
In thermal evaporation (TE), a metal source is resistively heated under high vacuum and the metal vapor is deposited from a single direction because of the long mean free path of the particles in the deposition chamber [84]. TE is attractive because of its simplicity, but it is limited in that a fabricated structure cannot be conformally coated. Additionally, the heating associated with the process can deform the polymeric microstructure.
--[p220]-- Any unpolymerized resin, as well as the gold deposited on top of that resin, was then removed using acetone, leaving freestanding structures with a 50-nm gold coating on top. These USRRs were shown to function as optical filters/polarizers in the 2–7 μm wavelength range.
--[p220]-- However, TE can be efective if the fabricated device requires that only the metal is deposited onto a single surface and if suitable conditions can be found that preserve the integrity of the structures. Jia and coworkers fabricated U-shaped split-ring resonators (USRRs) by MPL in the photopolymer AR N 4340, and then metallized the USRRs with a 50-nm gold layer [11].

===== [p216-216] Multiphoton Processing of Composite Materials and Functionalization of 3D Structures > Iron Oxide (4块) =====
--[p216]-- In early reports, the iron oxide nanoparticles were capped with oleic acid to disperse them into an acrylate photoresist up to 2.4 wt% [58]. A similar method was used to coat the surface of cantilevers fabricated in the crosslinkable epoxide SU-8 (MicroChem), which enhanced their sensitivity for detecting volatile molecules [77].
--[p216]-- The magnetic field intensity needed to drive a device can be reduced by using a high weight loading of $\mathrm { F e } _ { 3 } \mathrm { O } _ { 4 } .$ Although direct doping of ferric oxide nanoparticles into an organic photoresist has given little success, much higher weight loading has been achieved by modifying the surface of the nanoparticles using organic functionalizing agents.
--[p216]-- The performance was further improved by chemically modifying the surface of the nanoparticles using 3-(trimethoxysilyl)propyl methacrylate or 6-(methacryloyloxy) hexanoic acid, which stabilized the nanoparticle dispersion toward crosslinking during MPL [78, 79]. The nanoparticle concentration was further increased to 4–5 wt% by functionalizing with 6-(methacryloyloxy)hexanoic acid (Figure 9.8) [64].
--[p216]-- Iron Oxide
Powered MEMS are needed for many applications, such as microfluidics, labon-a-chip devices, and clinical diagnostic equipment. However, such devices are dificult to actuate because their small size increases the complexity of power transfer. One viable and cost-efective method for making powered MEMS is to fabricate parts containing the ferromagnetic material iron oxide $\left( \mathrm { F e _ { 3 } O _ { 4 } } \right)$ and then using magnets to actuate the structures remotely.

===== [p287-287] Fabricating Nano and Microstructures Made by Narrow Bandgap Semiconductors and Metals using Multiphoton Lithography > Fabrication of Silver Structures with Multiphoton Lithography (5块) =====
--[p287]-- In this section, we will show the fabrication of silver nanodots as small as 22 nm by employing photoreduction-based nonlinear ultra-high photosensitivity to reduce the feature size.
--[p287]-- Two-photon photoreduction was first used to fabricate silver nanostructures in 2006 [24]. However, compared with the top-down strategy, there has been little work exploring ways to improve the lithography resolution for this bottom-up strategy. Using the super-resolution photo-induced inhibition nanolithography (SPIN) approach, multiphoton lithography technique based on bottom-up strategy can achieve feature size down to 9 nm and resolution about 50 nm [11, 24].
--[p287]-- Metals with higher light damage threshold can stand high temperatures for specified applications.
Bottom-up strategy is the only method currently used for multiphoton lithography to fabricate 3D metal nano and microstructures. Photoreduction provides a unique mechanism for multiphoton lithography to fabricate metal nano and microstructures. The mechanism is similar to PbSe fabrication in Section 11.2, but using photoreduction to change metal ions to metal.
--[p287]-- In comparison, the technique can only realize feature size and resolution for metal fabrication of more than 100 nm and several hundreds of nanometers, respectively, due to the difraction limit of light [25, 26]. For the PbSe structures mentioned in Section 11.2, the minimum feature size is realized at ∼500 nm. This is a significant limitation for the application of structures fabricated by multiphoton lithography and made of narrow electronic bandgap materials as well as metals.
--[p287]-- Fabrication of Silver Structures with Multiphoton Lithography
Metals have unique properties compared to dielectric materials. Metals with large dielectric permittivity allow the fabrication ofstructures ofhigh contrast in refractive index, which is beneficial to achieve a complete photonic bandgap [23]. Metals with surface plasmon resonance enable efective manipulation of light in the nanoscale, which contributes to miniature devices for photonic chip integration.
